#10879. Claude Code Hooks: Enforce Project Conventions with Gates and Linting

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Claude Code hooks solve a problem that every developer running agentic coding sessions eventually hits: the AI drifts from project conventions, and soft instructions in the prompt cannot prevent it. This article walks through two working hooks — a pre-write gate and a post-edit lint pipeline — that enforce conventions deterministically.

Claude Code hooks solve a problem that every developer running agentic coding sessions eventually hits: the AI drifts from project conventions, and soft instructions in the prompt cannot prevent it. A popular r/Frontend post captured this precisely. The developer reported that Claude Code wrote roughly 1,000 lines of code for a simple helper function that already existed in a project dependency. The prompt stated the project conventions. Claude ignored them anyway. The result was duplicated logic, bloated output, and wasted review time.

The root cause is structural, not incidental. Prompt-based instructions are probabilistic. They work well in short sessions, but as the context window fills during extended agentic loops, earlier instructions degrade in influence. Tool outputs, file contents, and intermediate reasoning bury the initial convention reminders. A long enough session means the agent effectively forgets what it was told. Any enforcement mechanism that relies solely on prompt text will exhibit this degradation pattern.

Claude Code hooks offer an alternative enforcement mechanism: shell scripts that bind to lifecycle events inside the agent loop. These scripts use exit codes to enforce rules. Exit 0 means proceed. Exit 2 blocks the action, and the script's stderr is surfaced to Claude as feedback. Any other non-zero exit code is a non-blocking error. This is deterministic. No matter how full the context window gets, an exit code of 2 stops the action every time.

No matter how full the context window gets, an exit code of 2 stops the action every time.

By the end of this article, you will have two working hooks: a pre-write gate that forces Claude to verify whether an implementation already exists before writing new code, and a post-edit lint and format pipeline that runs Prettier and ESLint on every changed file. Together, these hooks catch duplicated or unnecessary code before it lands and enforce style rules automatically.

Prerequisites: Claude Code installed and functional; a Node.js project with Prettier and ESLint already configured; jq installed (brew install jq / apt install jq); Prettier and ESLint must be installed as local node_modules dependencies (not relied upon via npx remote fetch); and basic familiarity with Bash scripting.

Hooks operate on an event-driven model. They fire at specific points in Claude Code's agent loop, intercepting actions before or after they occur. Four lifecycle events are relevant:

PreToolUse fires before any tool call executes. This is the interception point for blocking actions. For file-write gating, the tool names to match are Write, Edit, and MultiEdit. You can also match against Bash for command-level gating.

After a tool call completes, PostToolUse runs. This is the point for validation: linters, formatters, or test suites check the result of an edit. Because the tool has already executed, an exit code of 2 from a PostToolUse hook does not block the original action. Instead, it surfaces the script's stderr as feedback to Claude, which can then issue a follow-up edit to correct the issues.

When the user submits a new prompt, UserPromptSubmit fires. This is useful for resetting state between conversational turns.

Notification fires when Claude sends a notification. Non-zero exit codes from Notification hooks do not block any action. (The documentation does not specify any exit-code effect for this event type.) This makes it useful only for logging or side effects.

The contract is simple. When a hook script exits with code 0, the action proceeds normally. Exit code 2 is the blocking signal. For PreToolUse, exit 2 blocks the tool call and the script's stderr is fed to Claude as feedback. For PostToolUse, the tool has already completed, but exit 2 surfaces the script's stderr to Claude, which can take corrective action. The stderr content becomes part of the agent's reasoning context, which is what makes hooks corrective rather than merely restrictive. Any other non-zero exit code counts as a non-blocking error, not a block.

Configure hooks in the hooks object within .claude/settings.json for project-level scope or ~/.claude/settings.json for user-level scope. Each entry is keyed by event name and contains an array of matcher groups. Each group pairs an optional matcher (a pattern matching specific tool names) with a hooks array of one or more handlers, and each handler declares a type (command for a shell command) plus the command to execute.

This skeleton shows the structural pattern. The matcher field accepts pipe-delimited tool names. When a PreToolUse event fires for a Write tool call, Claude Code checks whether any hook entry matches, then executes the command, piping a JSON payload to its stdin containing the tool name and parameters.

The following configuration wires together all three lifecycle events. The PreToolUse entry fires before Write, Edit, or MultiEdit tool calls and blocks the first write attempt per prompt, printing a checklist for Claude to verify before proceeding. The PostToolUse entry fires after those same tool calls complete and runs Prettier (auto-fix) then ESLint (check) on the changed file. If ESLint finds issues, it exits 2 and prints them to stderr, surfacing the lint errors as feedback for Claude to self-correct. The UserPromptSubmit entry resets the pre-write gate so it re-arms on every new user prompt. No matcher is needed for UserPromptSubmit because this event has no associated tool. The reset is delegated to a dedicated script to ensure $PPID is evaluated consistently.

Important: Before relying on the UserPromptSubmit reset, verify that $PPID expands to the same value in this context as it does inside the hook scripts. You can add a temporary diagnostic command — echo "PPID=$PPID" >> /tmp/hook-ppid.log — to both reset-gate.sh and pre-write-gate.sh and compare the logged values. If they differ, the gate reset will not function correctly.

All three lifecycle events work together as a system: UserPromptSubmit resets state, PreToolUse enforces the gate, and PostToolUse validates output.

On the first file-write attempt after each new user prompt, the hook exits with code 2 and prints a checklist to stderr, which Claude Code feeds to Claude as the reason for the block. The checklist asks Claude to verify three things before writing new code: Does an implementation already exist in the project? Is there a standard-library equivalent? Is there a well-maintained dependency that handles this? This directly addresses the r/Frontend scenario where Claude wrote 1,000 lines duplicating existing functionality.

After Claude acknowledges the checklist (by seeing the stderr feedback, adjusting its plan, and attempting the write again), subsequent writes within the same prompt pass through. The gate opens for the duration of that conversational turn and re-arms when the user submits a new prompt.

The stateful pattern uses a temp file to track whether the gate has been opened. The file path follows the pattern /tmp/claude-hook-gate-$PPID. When the file exists, the gate is open and writes proceed. When it does not exist, the gate is closed and writes are blocked.

The UserPromptSubmit hook entry in settings.json handles the reset by running scripts/reset-gate.sh, which deletes this temp file, so every new user prompt re-arms the gate automatically.

Note on shared systems: The temp file path is predictable and based on a process ID, which can be a symlink attack surface on multi-user systems. On shared machines, consider using mktemp -d to create a session-specific directory and store the gate file there, or ensure /tmp permissions are appropriately restricted.

Each hook invocation spawns a new child process, so $PID (or $$) changes on every run. $PPID refers to the parent of the hook script. In the expected case, this is the Claude Code session process, which ties the gate state to the session rather than to the ephemeral script invocation.

However, this assumption should be verified in your environment. If Claude Code launches hooks through an intermediate shell (e.g., sh -c "bash scripts/..."), $PPID may point to that intermediate shell rather than the Claude Code session, and the value may change between invocations. Before relying on this mechanism, add a diagnostic hook that logs echo "PPID=$PPID" >> /tmp/hook-debug.log and confirm that the value is stable across multiple hook calls within a single session. If it varies, replace $PPID with a fixed session identifier.

This script reads the JSON payload from stdin, extracts the tool name and file path with jq in a single invocation (avoiding double-fork and potential echo mangling), checks for the gate file, and either allows or blocks the action. On the first blocked attempt, it prints the checklist and creates the gate file so the immediate retry succeeds. The UserPromptSubmit hook runs scripts/reset-gate.sh to delete the gate file and re-arm everything for the next prompt.

Note that set -uo pipefail is used rather than set -euo pipefail. The -e flag is deliberately omitted because it would cause the script to exit immediately on any non-zero subcommand return, potentially before reaching the touch or exit 2 lines. This would cause the gate to silently pass through instead of blocking, defeating the entire mechanism.

MultiEdit payload caveat: This script assumes the hook payload contains a single .tool_input.file_path key. The MultiEdit tool may pass an array of edits with a different payload structure. Before deploying, verify the actual payload schema by adding printf '%s' "$INPUT" >> /tmp/hook-payload.log before the jq line, triggering a MultiEdit operation, and inspecting the log. Adjust the jq extraction accordingly.

This script is called by the UserPromptSubmit hook to reset the pre-write gate for the current session. Using a dedicated script ensures $PPID is evaluated in the same way as in pre-write-gate.sh.

Verifying the hook works before relying on it in a live session takes three terminal commands. Pipe sample JSON into the script, check the exit code, then observe the behavior change after the gate opens.

The first run should print the checklist and report exit code 2. The second run, with the gate file now present, should exit silently with code 0. Deleting the gate file simulates what the UserPromptSubmit hook does automatically during a real session.

Note that $$ is used here instead of $PPID because these commands run directly in the terminal rather than as child processes of Claude Code. This means the PID used for the gate file will differ from production. These manual tests verify the script's logic (block-then-allow behavior), but they do not verify that $PPID is stable in the actual Claude Code environment. Use the diagnostic hook described in the PPID section above to confirm production behavior.

Claude has already written the file by the time this hook runs. Prettier auto-fixes formatting, then ESLint checks for lint violations. If ESLint reports issues, the hook exits 2 and prints the errors to stderr, surfacing them as agent feedback; Claude can then issue a follow-up edit to fix them. PostToolUse hooks cannot prevent a write; they can only trigger corrective action after it. If both tools pass, the hook exits silently with code 0.

Here is what that looks like in practice: Claude writes code, the hook formats it and checks it, and if something fails, Claude immediately sees what went wrong and retries. Prettier's step is non-blocking (it writes fixes in place), while the linting step surfaces errors for Claude to act on.

Claude writes code, the hook formats it and checks it, and if something fails, Claude immediately sees what went wrong and retries.

The script canonicalizes the file path using realpath and verifies that it falls within the project root directory. This prevents path traversal attacks where a crafted path like /tmp/../etc/passwd could bypass simple prefix checks. PROJECT_ROOT derives from the script's own location ($(dirname "$0")/..), which also ensures ESLint and Prettier configs are discoverable.

Prettier runs with --write to fix formatting in place; its exit code is deliberately ignored with || true because formatting fixes do not block the pipeline. The command -v check ensures both Prettier and ESLint exist locally before attempting to run them. ESLint runs with --max-warnings 0 to treat warnings as errors, enforcing a strict zero-warning policy. The timeout 30 wrapper prevents ESLint from hanging indefinitely on large or problematic files.

ESLint exit codes are handled distinctly: exit code 1 indicates lint violations (actionable by Claude), exit code 2 indicates a configuration or fatal error (requires developer attention, not a code fix), and exit code 124 indicates a timeout. This distinction prevents Claude from endlessly retrying lint fixes when the actual problem is a broken ESLint configuration. In each failure branch the hook itself exits 2 and writes to stderr, the combination Claude Code surfaces to Claude as feedback.

Both tools are invoked via ./node_modules/.bin/ rather than npx to guarantee that locally installed versions are used. Using npx without --no-install can cause it to silently download packages at runtime if they are not found in local node_modules, introducing network latency, non-determinism, and unexpected cost during hook execution.

Files outside the project root, such as /tmp scratch files that Claude sometimes creates during multi-step operations, are skipped by the realpath canonicalization and project-root check. This is more robust than a simple prefix check against /tmp/* or /var/*, because it also catches path traversal patterns like /tmp/../etc/passwd and handles symlinks correctly.

Missing Prettier or ESLint binaries are handled gracefully. Both steps check for the binary with command -v before running. If ESLint cannot find a config file, it reports a fatal error (exit code 2), and that error flows through stdout to Claude with an explicit "configuration error" label, making it clear that the fix needed is not a code change. This is preferable to silently swallowing the failure.

Binary files and non-JS/TS files are handled by the extension filter in the case statement. The hook exits with code 0 for any unmatched extension, letting the write proceed without interference.

Layer the two: use CLAUDE.md for the "why" behind conventions, and hooks for the "must." CLAUDE.md explains that the team prefers existing utilities over new implementations, while the pre-write gate hook enforces that preference. CLAUDE.md describes the formatting standard; the post-edit hook runs the formatter. Neither mechanism replaces the other.

Hooks run synchronously. While a hook executes, the agent is blocked. Aim for under one second per hook invocation. Prettier and ESLint on a single file meet this target on typical developer hardware with local node_modules. Scripts exceeding two to three seconds will noticeably stall the agent loop. Running a full test suite as a hook would not be appropriate.

Hook input payloads follow a JSON structure that may evolve across Claude Code versions. grep or sed parsing is brittle and breaks on structural changes. jq provides safe, schema-aware extraction and handles missing fields gracefully with the // empty fallback pattern used in the scripts above.

When setting up hooks for the first time, appending output to a log file (>> /tmp/claude-hooks.log) helps diagnose issues with path extraction, exit codes, and unexpected tool names. Remove or disable logging once you have confirmed correct behavior over several sessions, to avoid filling disk space during long runs.

Commit the scripts/ directory and .claude/settings.json to the repository. Every team member gets the same guardrails without manual setup. Hooks become part of the project's development infrastructure, not a personal customization.

Commit the scripts/ directory and .claude/settings.json to the repository. Every team member gets the same guardrails without manual setup.

The two-hook setup built in this article addresses both sides of convention drift: the pre-write gate catches duplicated code before it happens, and the post-edit pipeline enforces formatting and lint rules on every change. The pattern extends to security scanning, test runners, or any file-level validation that runs in under a few seconds.

#10872. Astro vs Next.js: How to Pick Between Islands and Server Components

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

The line between Astro and Next.js has blurred considerably. This guide replaces gut instinct with a structured decision matrix and side-by-side implementation patterns, exposing where each architecture genuinely excels and where it breaks down.

This guide targets Astro 4.x, Next.js 14.x (App Router), React 18.x, and Node.js 22.x LTS or later. Run node --version and verify your framework versions with npm list astro next react before proceeding. API surfaces differ across major versions; the patterns shown here may not apply to earlier or later releases.

The line between Astro and Next.js has blurred considerably. Astro added SSR, server endpoints, and hybrid rendering modes. Next.js introduced partial prerendering and static exports. Both frameworks now cover a wide enough surface area that choosing between Astro and Next.js requires more than a vague sense of which one "feels right" for a project. The wrong call early on locks a team into performance ceilings and hosting costs that are expensive to reverse — migrating routing, data-fetching patterns, and deployment pipelines typically means rewriting, not refactoring.

This guide replaces gut instinct with a structured decision matrix and side-by-side implementation patterns. The goal is to expose where each architecture genuinely excels, where it breaks down, and how to map project requirements to the right default.

Astro pages render as static HTML by default. Zero JavaScript ships to the browser unless a developer explicitly opts in. The mechanism for opting in is Astro's client:* directive system, which controls exactly when and how individual UI components hydrate. The available directives include client:load (hydrate immediately on page load), client:idle (hydrate once the browser is idle), client:visible (hydrate when the component scrolls into view), client:media (hydrate when a CSS media query matches), and client:only="react" (skip server rendering entirely; the framework name is a required argument, e.g., "react", "svelte"). Every piece of surrounding markup stays as static HTML. Critically, Astro is framework-agnostic: islands can be built in React, Svelte, Vue, Solid, or Preact, and multiple frameworks can coexist within the same project.

In the App Router, Next.js components are server-rendered by default. Client components are opted into with the "use client" directive at the top of a file. The server/client boundary is per-component, but unlike Astro's isolated islands, the entire page participates in React's unified component tree and streaming pipeline. This tight coupling to the React ecosystem means Next.js benefits from React's concurrent features, including Suspense boundaries and transitions, but it also means the framework is bound to React as the sole UI layer.

Astro treats a page as a static document with interactive widgets bolted on. The mental model is HTML-first. Next.js treats a page as a unified React component tree where the server/client split is a rendering concern. The mental model is app-first. This distinction shapes everything from default payload size to how teams reason about state, data fetching, and hydration boundaries.

Astro treats a page as a static document with interactive widgets bolted on. The mental model is HTML-first. Next.js treats a page as a unified React component tree where the server/client split is a rendering concern. The mental model is app-first.

Consider a straightforward blog post page: a title, author byline, Markdown body, and a static table of contents. For pure content like this, the architectural differences become immediately visible.

This assumes a content collection named blog is defined in src/content/config.ts. See Astro's Content Collections documentation to configure the schema.

The key detail: no client:* directive appears anywhere. This page ships zero client-side JavaScript. The HTML is generated at build time and served as a static file.

Create a content/blog/ directory at the project root and place your .md files there.

No "use client" directive here either, so this is a server component. The content renders on the server. When client components exist anywhere in the component tree, the React runtime ships to provide hydration scaffolding. For a fully static page with no "use client" components and output: 'export' configured, Next.js can produce zero client-side JavaScript, though this requires explicit configuration.

Now consider a search input with live filtering of article titles, debounced API calls, and result rendering. This is where the transition from static to interactive reveals each framework's trade-offs.

The React search component lives in its own file:

The Astro page imports it with client:visible:

The search island hydrates only when the user scrolls it into view. Everything else on the page remains static HTML with zero JavaScript cost.

This component is imported into a server component page and hydrates as part of the React tree. The isLoading state provides user feedback while fetches are in progress, compatible with React 18 and later.

Astro provides granular, directive-level control over exactly when JavaScript loads. The client:visible, client:idle, and client:media options let developers tie hydration to real user context rather than page load. Next.js provides deeper integration with React's data-fetching and state primitives but ships a larger baseline runtime, since the React component tree requires its hydration infrastructure regardless of how much interactivity the page actually contains.

Count which column a project's requirements land in more frequently. If the split is roughly even, the tiebreakers in the following section apply.

Astro's architecture fights back when the app requires global shared state across many interactive components, such as a real-time collaborative editor. Client-side routing with optimistic UI updates across multiple nested segments — think multi-step wizards with shared state spanning three or more route levels — pushes against the islands model. The clearest signal is when islands start outnumbering the static HTML on a page. At that point, the team is working against the architecture rather than with it.

The clearest signal is when islands start outnumbering the static HTML on a page. At that point, the team is working against the architecture rather than with it.

Next.js becomes overhead when the site is predominantly static content and the React runtime (~80-90 KB gzipped for React + ReactDOM + framework code) ships for no user-facing benefit. If the team does not use React and would need to adopt it solely for the framework, the learning curve and ecosystem lock-in are hard to justify. Additionally, Vercel-specific features such as built-in analytics and globally distributed ISR edge caching may be important, but ISR itself works on any Node.js server via next start. When budget or organizational policy prevents hosting on Vercel and the team specifically needs Vercel's global edge distribution layer, self-hosting Next.js adds operational burden that may not be worth the trade-off.

Lighthouse scores run against starter templates are misleading. A fresh Astro project and a fresh Next.js project both score near-perfect because neither contains real-world complexity. The meaningful difference is architectural.

With Astro, performance is a default. Zero JavaScript ships unless the developer explicitly adds it, and every addition is visible in the template as a client:* directive. Performance degrades only when a developer opts in, and every addition is auditable. Content sites built on Astro can ship under 50 KB total page weight before custom fonts and images — measure with astro build output and a tool like WebPageTest.

With Next.js, keeping payloads small demands discipline. Avoiding unnecessary "use client" directives, using streaming and Suspense boundaries effectively, and managing client component boundaries carefully all demand ongoing attention. Next.js apps range from under 100 KB for disciplined RSC pages to 500 KB+ when client boundaries are loose — measure your own bundle with next build output.

With Astro, performance is a default. Zero JavaScript ships unless the developer explicitly adds it, and every addition is visible in the template as a client:* directive.

Astro defaults to static site generation. To enable SSR for a specific route, add export const prerender = false as a module export in the component's script block (between the --- fences). Hybrid mode combines both, allowing most pages to be static while specific routes render on the server.

Next.js supports static generation, server-side rendering, ISR (Incremental Static Regeneration), partial prerendering (experimental as of Next.js 14; check release notes for current status), and an edge runtime option for middleware and select routes.

For a blog, both Astro SSG and Next.js SSG work well, but Astro ships less JavaScript. E-commerce product pages with frequently changing data pair well with Next.js ISR or Astro SSR backed by a CDN. Documentation sites get a strong out-of-the-box experience from Astro's purpose-built Starlight tool, while Next.js SSG is equally capable if the team already works in the React ecosystem.

Astro's static adapter works on any CDN. Server adapters exist for Node, Cloudflare Workers, Netlify, Deno, and Vercel. Fully static Astro sites can be hosted for free on most platforms.

On the Vercel platform, Next.js delivers its best experience. Teams can self-host with next start on Node or through Docker. ISR and image optimization work via next start on any Node.js server. Vercel adds globally distributed ISR cache replication and edge delivery; self-hosting provides the same features without that global distribution layer. SSR adds compute costs that static deployments avoid entirely.

If the site is content-first and interactivity is sporadic, choose Astro. If the product is an interactive web application built in React, choose Next.js. Projects that blend content with a few deeply interactive sections — search, comments, or embedded tools — can go either way: Astro with React islands or Next.js with disciplined RSC boundaries. The tiebreaker is team expertise and hosting preference.

The question is not which framework is "better." It is which default — zero JavaScript or a React component tree — matches where the project concentrates its complexity. That concentration can shift as requirements evolve. The decision matrix above is worth revisiting whenever project scope changes, a new feature pushes the interactivity ratio, or hosting constraints shift. The framework that aligns with the project's natural shape will demand the least friction over time.

#10866. LLM Proxy Setup with LiteLLM: Keys, Logging, and Fallbacks

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

An LLM proxy setup solves a problem that becomes obvious the moment a team scales past a single developer hitting a single API. LiteLLM is the open-source proxy that addresses key pain points directly: virtual API key issuance, unified per-request logging, and fallback routing across providers, all behind an OpenAI-compatible API surface.

An LLM proxy setup solves a problem that becomes obvious the moment a team scales past a single developer hitting a single API. Provider dashboards show billing totals, aggregated over a billing cycle, but they do not surface token-level time-series data that engineers can actually act on. For a scenario like a paid-subscription chat frontend, where attributing costs to each client determines margin, aggregate billing data is nearly useless. Teams need to know which client, which request, and which model consumed what, broken down by tokens, latency, and cost.

The second pain point is operational security. Developers consistently report wanting upstream provider keys kept out of agents, test harnesses, and sandboxes. A single master key scattered across environments is both a security liability and a revocation nightmare -- when one environment is compromised, every integration sharing that key is exposed.

Teams need to know which client, which request, and which model consumed what, broken down by tokens, latency, and cost.

LiteLLM is the open-source proxy that addresses both problems directly: virtual API key issuance, unified per-request logging, and fallback routing across providers, all behind an OpenAI-compatible API surface.

By the end of this tutorial, readers will have a Dockerized LiteLLM proxy with virtual keys, request logging to a PostgreSQL database, multi-provider fallback between OpenAI and Anthropic, and an OWASP-aligned security checklist to harden the deployment.

Prerequisites: Docker and Docker Compose v2 (the docker compose plugin, not legacy docker-compose v1) installed, at least one upstream API key (OpenAI or Anthropic -- both are required for bidirectional fallback as configured), basic comfort with YAML and the terminal, and curl available on the host. For the Python fallback test later in this tutorial: Python 3.8+ and the openai package (pip install openai>=1.0.0).

The architecture is straightforward. Every LLM call flows through a single gateway:

The proxy intercepts every call, validates the virtual API key, attaches metadata (user, team, key ID), logs the full request and response cycle, and then decides which upstream model to hit based on the configured routing strategy. The client never communicates with the provider directly and never holds a provider key.

The proxy consolidates several operational concerns into one service:

The entire deployment requires three files (plus a database schema initialization that LiteLLM handles automatically on first startup):

Everything below is copy-paste-safe. Start with the proxy configuration.

The model_list registers two providers under friendly model names. The litellm_settings block defines bidirectional fallbacks: if gpt-4o fails, the proxy tries claude-sonnet, and vice versa. Setting success_callback and failure_callback to ["db"] persists every request and response to the connected PostgreSQL database. The num_retries value controls how many times LiteLLM retries the same model before triggering the fallback. The cooldown_period of 60 seconds prevents retries from hammering a rate-limited or failing provider immediately after repeated failures. The request_timeout of 30 seconds prevents hung connections from blocking the proxy.

Note: Verify the fallbacks YAML structure against your installed LiteLLM version's schema; the fallback format has changed between major releases.

The LiteLLM image tag is pinned to main-v1.67.0. Confirm the tag exists before deployment: docker pull ghcr.io/berriai/litellm:main-v1.67.0-stable. For maximum reproducibility, pin to the image digest (@sha256:...) instead of the tag. Pinning matters for reproducibility and security auditing. The PostgreSQL service uses an Alpine-based image and includes a health check so the proxy does not start before the database is ready. The config YAML is bind-mounted read-only into the container. The DATABASE_URL is constructed in the Compose file's environment block so the database password is defined in only one place (POSTGRES_PASSWORD). The port binding is restricted to 127.0.0.1 so the proxy is not exposed on public interfaces; place a reverse proxy (nginx or Caddy) in front for external access with TLS.

Upstream keys live only in this file. They are never referenced in client code, never committed to version control, and never passed to agents or sandboxes. The master key authenticates administrative operations against the proxy's management API. All values must be set before the first run. Generate the master key with openssl rand -hex 32 and the database password with openssl rand -base64 32.

Immediately after creating this file, ensure it is excluded from version control:

On first run, LiteLLM automatically runs Prisma database migrations to create the required schema. This adds 30-60 seconds to the first startup compared to subsequent starts (the start_period: 60s in the health check accommodates this). If the litellm container exits immediately, check logs with docker compose logs litellm for migration errors before proceeding. Look for lines containing prisma or migration to confirm the schema was created successfully.

Use curl http://localhost:4000/health for a basic liveness check. For full readiness including model availability and database connectivity, use curl http://localhost:4000/health/readiness and confirm no error states are present. The readiness response includes the list of model names registered in the config, their provider status, and the database connectivity state. If any model fails to initialize (for example, due to a missing API key), it appears in the response with an error status. Confirming a clean readiness check before issuing virtual keys prevents debugging downstream failures that originate from misconfigured upstream credentials.

Virtual keys are the core isolation mechanism. Each client application, each agent, each staging environment receives its own key. That key can be restricted to specific models and capped at a maximum budget. If a sandbox key is compromised, revoking it has zero impact on production. If a single integration starts generating runaway costs, its budget cap halts spending automatically. Upstream provider credentials remain on the proxy server and are never exposed to consumers.

If a sandbox key is compromised, revoking it has zero impact on production. If a single integration starts generating runaway costs, its budget cap halts spending automatically.

Never use a placeholder master key in a running deployment. The commands below use a shell variable loaded from your .env file.

The response includes a key field containing the new virtual API key (prefixed with sk-), a key_name, the configured budget, and the expiration timestamp. The models array restricts the key to only the listed models. The proxy rejects any call targeting a model outside the allowlist.

In the commands below, replace sk-generated-virtual-key with an actual virtual key returned by /key/generate.

The /key/info response surfaces spend-to-date against the configured max_budget, the number of requests made, and the metadata attached at creation time. Revocation takes effect immediately for new requests. Any request that has already passed the authentication check may complete; the proxy rejects all subsequent requests with that key, returning a 401.

The success_callback: ["db"] and failure_callback: ["db"] directives in the config persist every request to the PostgreSQL database. This requires the database schema to be initialized (handled automatically by Prisma on first startup -- see the deployment section above). Each log entry captures the virtual key that made the call, the upstream model used, prompt and completion token counts, computed cost, response latency, and the HTTP status code. The failure callback logs failed requests (timeouts, 5xx errors, budget exceeded) with error details attached.

This is the data that provider dashboards do not surface as per-request rows queryable by key, model, and time range. Rather than a monthly billing total, engineers get per-request granularity keyed to their own virtual key identifiers, ready for time-series analysis.

LiteLLM also supports langfuse, prometheus, and custom webhook targets as callback destinations. These can be specified alongside or instead of "db" in the callback arrays.

Note: The virtual key is passed in a request header rather than as a URL query parameter to prevent it from being logged by reverse proxies, load balancers, and browser history. Verify the supported header name (X-Litellm-Key or equivalent) against your installed LiteLLM version's API reference.

The response is a JSON array of log entries, each containing the request ID, model, tokens, cost, and timestamp. Filtering by virtual key provides per-client cost attribution. Filtering by date range supports invoice generation and anomaly detection.

For teams that already run Langfuse, Prometheus, or Grafana, LiteLLM can forward callbacks to those systems. Adding "langfuse" to the success_callback array, along with the appropriate environment variables for the Langfuse API endpoint and keys, enables full trace-level observability with a UI. The LiteLLM documentation covers each integration's required configuration variables in detail.

The fallbacks key in the YAML config defines ordered fallback chains. When a primary model returns a 5xx error, times out (beyond the configured request_timeout), or raises a provider-level exception, LiteLLM automatically retries the request with the next model in the fallback list. The client sees no retry; the proxy handles it internally. The response includes metadata indicating which model actually served the request, but the client code does not need to implement any retry logic.

The num_retries: 2 setting means LiteLLM makes three total attempts on the primary model (the original request plus two retries) on retriable errors before triggering the fallback chain. The cooldown_period: 60 ensures that after repeated failures, the proxy backs off the failing provider for 60 seconds rather than immediately retrying against a provider that has explicitly refused requests. This prevents a single transient error from triggering an unnecessary provider switch while also respecting provider rate limits.

The openai Python SDK works unmodified because LiteLLM exposes an OpenAI-compatible API. The base_url points to the proxy, and the api_key is the virtual key. To verify fallback behavior, temporarily invalidate the upstream OpenAI key in the .env file and recreate the proxy container (docker compose up -d --force-recreate litellm) -- editing .env alone does not update a running container. All gpt-4o requests will then fail and fall back to claude-sonnet. The spend log entry for that request will show the fallback model as the one that served the response.

LiteLLM populates response.model with the actual serving model; if you find it returns the originally requested model name instead, verify the actual fallback model via the /spend/logs endpoint.

LiteLLM also supports a routing_strategy setting for load balancing across multiple deployments of the same model. For example, two separate OpenAI API keys for gpt-4o can be registered as separate entries in the model_list under the same model_name, and the proxy distributes requests between them. This is distinct from fallback routing, which switches to a different model or provider on failure. The two mechanisms can be combined: load balance across multiple deployments of the primary model, and fall back to a different provider if all deployments are unavailable.

The following checklist maps proxy hardening steps to relevant categories from the OWASP API Security Top 10 (2023 edition).

A single master key scattered across environments is both a security liability and a revocation nightmare -- when one environment is compromised, every integration sharing that key is exposed.

To verify the full setup end-to-end, run the fallback test from the "Testing a Fallback Scenario" section and confirm the spend log entry shows the expected model.

Three operational wins are now in place: upstream key isolation through virtual keys, per-request observability through database-backed logging, and provider resilience through fallback routing. Each is configured declaratively and reproducible from the three files built in this tutorial.

Connect Langfuse as a callback target to get a full observability UI with trace visualization. Set up alerting on spend thresholds so you receive notifications when a virtual key approaches its budget cap. You can also enable LiteLLM's caching layer to reduce redundant calls for repeated queries -- consult the LiteLLM caching documentation for hit-rate behavior and configuration details.

#10860. Review AI-Generated UI Changes with Storybook and Chromatic

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

AI coding agents like Cursor, Copilot Agent, Devin, and Claude Code produce UI changes faster than most reviewers can audit in raw diff form, especially once changesets exceed 50+ files. This article provides a repeatable playbook that combines prompt discipline, Storybook component isolation, and Chromatic visual regression testing to make AI-generated UI diffs genuinely reviewable.

AI coding agents like Cursor, Copilot Agent, Devin, and Claude Code produce UI changes faster than most reviewers can audit in raw diff form, especially once changesets exceed 50+ files. The core problem is straightforward: these tools generate sprawling, multi-file changesets where syntactically correct code can still produce broken visual output. Reviewing AI-generated UI changes in a traditional diff viewer is not just slow; it is structurally inadequate for catching the kinds of regressions these tools introduce.

This frustration is well documented. A recurring observation in frontend communities such as r/Frontend is that reviewing massive diffs from AI agents is daunting, with the practical suggestion being to reduce prompt scope as the first line of defense. That instinct is sound, but scope reduction alone is not enough. Without a visual verification layer, regressions in layout, spacing, accessibility, and responsiveness slip through code review undetected.

This article provides a repeatable playbook that combines prompt discipline, Storybook component isolation, and Chromatic visual regression testing to make AI-generated UI diffs genuinely reviewable. The tech stack covered is Storybook 8, 9, or 10, Chromatic, and React or Vue 3. Percy works as an alternative for teams already invested in that ecosystem.

Understanding how to verify AI UI changes requires recognizing two structural problems that distinguish AI-generated diffs from human-authored ones.

AI agents tend to touch many files per prompt. A single instruction to "update the pricing card design" can result in changes across component files, style sheets, test files, layout wrappers, utility functions, and type definitions. Research on code review effectiveness (e.g., Microsoft and Google studies on review quality vs. diff size) consistently shows that PRs over 20 files see measurably lower review quality. The sheer breadth of changes makes it easy to miss a subtle but critical modification buried deep in the changeset.

The second problem is more insidious. Syntactically valid JSX or Vue template changes can produce broken layouts, misaligned spacing, or silently dropped accessibility attributes. An AI agent might restructure a flex container in a way that passes linting and type checking while shifting a call-to-action button off-screen at certain breakpoints. Reviewers cannot catch these visual regressions in a diff view. You need pixel-level comparison against a known-good baseline. Without it, spatial regressions bypass review entirely and reach production undetected.

Reviewers cannot catch these visual regressions in a diff view. You need pixel-level comparison against a known-good baseline. Without it, spatial regressions bypass review entirely and reach production undetected.

The most effective way to make reviews easier is to constrain every AI agent prompt to a single component boundary. Rather than prompting "redesign the pricing page," scope the instruction to something like "Refactor the PricingCard component to use the new design tokens." This produces smaller diffs, which in turn enable faster reviews and higher confidence in the outcome. It also makes the resulting Chromatic snapshots more meaningful, since the visual diff focuses on one component's behavior rather than an entire page's worth of changes.

The following is a reusable system-prompt snippet that can be added to an AI agent's system instructions, a .cursorrules file, or a CLAUDE.md file in the project root (Claude Code's project-level system prompt). It encodes the single-component constraint, a file-touch limit, and an explicit instruction to write or update the corresponding Storybook story.

Paste this snippet directly into project configuration. It constrains the agent's output surface and ensures that story coverage ships with every component change, making visual review possible without additional human effort to write stories after the fact.

Storybook renders each component in isolation with defined states via props and args, removing the need to spin up the full application to inspect a change. For Storybook Chromatic AI review workflows, this isolation is the key enabler. Reviewers can visually inspect every meaningful state that an AI agent may have altered: default rendering, interactive states, edge cases with unusual data, and responsive behavior at different viewport widths. Without this isolation, reviewers must navigate the running application to find and verify each affected component, a process that does not scale.

The following example demonstrates a PricingCard.stories.tsx file using the Component Story Format 3 (CSF3) convention. It covers default, highlighted, disabled, and responsive states, providing the snapshot surface that Chromatic will use for visual diffing.

Note: These stories import a PricingCard component that you must create yourself. The component should accept the props shown in the args below (tier, title, price, features, highlighted, disabled). The mobile1 viewport used in the Responsive story requires @storybook/addon-viewport to be registered in your .storybook/main.js addons array.

The Responsive story uses Chromatic's viewports parameter to generate snapshots at three breakpoints, catching layout regressions that would otherwise require manual resizing. The LongContent story exercises text overflow behavior, a common source of AI-introduced regressions.

The equivalent Vue 3 story follows the same CSF3 structure with minimal differences:

The core story structure is identical; the Vue example omits additional story variants (Disabled, Responsive, LongContent) for brevity. Add them following the React example above.

The prompt scoping rule from Step 1 already instructs the agent to create or update the story file as part of its output. This is not optional. If the AI omits the story, the pull request should be treated as incomplete and sent back. Story coverage is what makes the rest of this pipeline functional; without it, Chromatic has nothing to snapshot.

On every push or pull request, Chromatic captures a snapshot of every Storybook story. For branches containing AI-generated changes, it produces a visual diff comparing the new rendering against the accepted baseline. This is the core mechanism for visual regression testing AI code: instead of squinting at JSX diffs and mentally rendering layout changes, reviewers approve or reject changes with the actual rendered output side by side.

Snapshot limits: Chromatic's free tier includes a limited number of snapshots per month. Review Chromatic's pricing page before enabling this workflow on large component libraries to avoid unexpected costs.

The following GitHub Actions workflow installs dependencies, builds Storybook, and runs Chromatic on every pull request.

Prerequisites: This workflow assumes you have a Chromatic account and project. Obtain your project token from the Chromatic project dashboard at chromatic.com and store it as a GitHub Actions secret named CHROMATIC_PROJECT_TOKEN.

The exitZeroOnChanges: true flag matters most when you first adopt the workflow. It allows Chromatic to report visual changes as informational rather than blocking the build, giving teams time to build confidence before making it a hard gate. Setting this to false and adding Chromatic as a required status check is the recommended progression once the team trusts the process.

Caution: With exitZeroOnChanges: true, visual regressions will never block a merge on their own. Ensure your team actively reviews Chromatic's results before merging, or transition to false once the workflow is established.

The fetch-depth: 0 on the checkout step ensures Chromatic can locate the correct baseline ancestor commit for comparison. Without it, Chromatic cannot traverse git history to find the right ancestor. All captured stories will appear as new with no baseline diff available, making the visual regression check useless.

The review UI presents the baseline snapshot alongside the new snapshot with pixel-level change highlighting. Changed areas appear overlaid in a diff color, making it immediately obvious where the rendering shifted. Reviewers accept or deny each changed story individually. When a reviewer accepts a snapshot, it becomes the new baseline for subsequent comparisons, which prevents false positives on future runs. After major design-token changes, teams should manually review all accepted baselines to confirm the new state is intentional rather than reflexively accepting everything.

Instead of squinting at JSX diffs and mentally rendering layout changes, reviewers approve or reject changes with the actual rendered output side by side.

Percy offers similar snapshot diffing capabilities and integrates with a broader set of CI providers, making it a reasonable choice for teams not using GitHub Actions or already invested in the BrowserStack ecosystem. For teams already using Storybook as their component development environment, Chromatic's Storybook-first design means less configuration overhead. Percy's documentation is available at https://docs.percy.io for teams evaluating alternatives.

Standard code-review checklists are built for human-authored changes. They miss visual regressions, accessibility attribute removal, and scope-creep concerns that are specific to AI output. An AI UI review checklist normalizes quality gates across team members regardless of their experience level with specific AI coding tools. It also provides a structured forcing function that prevents the "looks fine, ship it" reflex when reviewing a green CI build.

The following is a complete PR template checklist formatted for GitHub-flavored Markdown. It can be pasted directly into .github/PULL_REQUEST_TEMPLATE.md:

For teams that want automated enforcement, configure danger-js to auto-flag pull requests that exceed a file-change threshold or lack a linked Chromatic build URL in the PR description. The actions/labeler GitHub Action can apply warning labels to PRs that match patterns indicating scope creep, such as changes to global CSS files or shared utility modules. These automations turn the checklist from a social contract into a technical gate.

Teams should consider creating a visual diagram of this flow for their internal wiki. A simple flowchart with six numbered boxes and arrows communicates the process effectively in onboarding documentation.

Three metrics provide visibility into how well this workflow performs:

AI agents draft fast but review their own output inconsistently. Visual regression testing with Storybook and Chromatic closes the gap between code that compiles and code that renders correctly. The prompt scoping rule and PR checklist presented here require minimal setup effort, and most teams see review-time improvements within the first sprint of adoption.

AI agents draft fast but review their own output inconsistently. Visual regression testing with Storybook and Chromatic closes the gap between code that compiles and code that renders correctly.

The next step is concrete: paste the PR template into .github/PULL_REQUEST_TEMPLATE.md, add the Chromatic workflow to CI, and run the next AI-generated branch through visual review before merging. The tooling exists and the workflow is proven.

#10854. Tony Hawk kicked out of iconic Pro Skater 3 park during anniversary video

Tony Hawk has revealed that he was kicked out of a park featured in Tony Hawk’s Pro Skater 3 while filming a video to celebrate the game’s 25th anniversary.

Hawk visited Pershing Square in Los Angeles on October 30 – one of the real-world locations recreated in the game – to mark the 25th anniversary of Pro Skater 3’s release.

Hawk shared footage of his visit on Instagram, showing him skating through Pershing Square and replicating clips from the 2001 game.

However, filming seemingly didn’t go entirely to plan. “Happy 25th THPS3!” Hawk wrote in the video’s caption, adding, “fun fact: we got kicked out of Pershing Square while shooting this today.”

Hawk later clarified that a security guard had approached the group and asked them to leave.

“She just shoo’d us away,” he explained. “It was very anticlimactic.” Hawk didn’t reveal why they were told to leave.

Pershing Square is one of several real-world Los Angeles locations recreated in Pro Skater 3. The game’s Los Angeles level also features landmarks inspired by the nearby Downtown Car Wash, which Hawk revisited for the video.

Originally released on October 30, 2001, Tony Hawk’s Pro Skater 3 was the first entry in the series to launch on PlayStation 2.

The game was also brought back in 2025 as part of Tony Hawk’s Pro Skater 3 + 4, a remake collection developed by Iron Galaxy.

#10853. LLM Structured Output with JSON Schema: Typed Responses in TypeScript

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

LLM structured output remains one of the most persistent pain points in production systems. This article walks through native JSON schema mode, function calling, Zod runtime validation, retry logic with error feedback, and a concrete provider comparison to guide architectural decisions.

LLM structured output remains one of the most persistent pain points in production systems. Developers building on top of large language models routinely encounter inconsistent response structures, missing fields, hallucinated keys, and malformed JSON that breaks downstream logic. A single unexpected null where an array was expected can cascade through an application and take down an entire pipeline. The core issue: LLM outputs are, by default, untyped free text masquerading as structured data.

A single unexpected null where an array was expected can cascade through an application and take down an entire pipeline.

By the end of this article, readers will have a typed, provider-agnostic validation layer with bounded retries that works across OpenAI, Google Gemini, Anthropic, and self-hosted vLLM endpoints. The approach covers native JSON schema mode, function calling, Zod runtime validation, retry logic with error feedback, and a concrete provider comparison to guide architectural decisions.

This article assumes the following environment and package versions:

Two levels of structured output support differ in what they guarantee. JSON mode, available in providers like OpenAI and Gemini, guarantees that the model emits syntactically valid JSON. It does not guarantee any particular shape. The model might return {"foo": 42} when the application expected {"sentiment": "positive", "rating": 5}. Valid JSON is not the same as correct JSON.

Schema-constrained mode goes further. It enforces a declared JSON Schema at the token-generation level: the provider guides token decoding to produce output conforming to a specific structure. OpenAI supports this through the response_format parameter with type json_schema. Google Gemini offers it via generationConfig.responseSchema. vLLM provides schema-constrained decoding through its guided_json parameter. The strictness and subset of JSON Schema supported varies across providers, but the core mechanism is the same: constrain token sampling so only schema-valid sequences are generated.

Function calling (also referred to as tool calling) provides an alternative path to structured output. When a tool is defined with an input_schema, the provider forces the model to emit arguments matching the declared schema. This is Anthropic's primary structured output mechanism for Claude, which does not offer a dedicated response_format JSON schema mode. Instead, developers define a tool whose input schema matches the desired output structure, and the model returns the structured data inside a tool_use content block.

Use function calling when the application also needs to route actions based on the model's decision. Use response_format schema when the goal is purely typed data extraction. Both mechanisms benefit from the validation layer described below.

TypeScript's type system operates entirely at compile time. It erases all type information at runtime, which means a JSON.parse call returns any regardless of what interface was declared. Zod fills this gap by providing runtime type safety: it validates that a parsed object actually conforms to the expected shape, with rich error messages that pinpoint exactly which field failed and why. When an LLM returns "rating": "five" instead of "rating": 5, Zod reports the exact path, expected type, and received value. This level of detail is essential for debugging malformed LLM responses.

The zod-to-json-schema library converts a Zod schema into a standard JSON Schema object, enabling a single schema definition to drive both runtime validation and provider-level schema constraints.

This ProductReviewSchema becomes the single source of truth. The same Zod object validates LLM responses at runtime, generates the JSON Schema sent to providers, and infers the TypeScript type used throughout the application. There is no separate interface to keep in sync.

Note: Each provider code block below depends on ProductReviewSchema, ProductReview, and productReviewJsonSchema defined in the "Building a Reusable Schema" section above. These blocks are not standalone — copy the schema setup code first or structure your project as a single file.

OpenAI's response_format parameter with type json_schema provides token-level schema enforcement. The JSON Schema generated from Zod is passed directly into the API call.

When strict: true is set, OpenAI requires that the schema include additionalProperties: false and that all fields appear in the required array. Zod excludes .optional() fields from required by default; restructure them using .nullable() or a union to satisfy strict mode. Verify the JSON Schema output before sending it to the API for any schema that uses optional fields. The returned content is a JSON string that can be passed to JSON.parse and then validated with Zod.

The same JSON Schema works with Google Gemini's generationConfig.responseSchema, demonstrating the portability of the Zod-driven approach.

Gemini's schema support has notable subset limitations. It does not support $ref for schema references, and its handling of complex oneOf constructs is limited. The $refStrategy: "none" option passed to zodToJsonSchema earlier inlines all references, which avoids the $ref problem. For schemas involving discriminated unions, test against Gemini specifically to confirm support.

Anthropic's Claude models do not expose a response_format JSON schema mode. Structured output is achieved by defining a tool whose input_schema matches the target JSON Schema. The API call includes a tool definition with the ProductReview JSON Schema as its input schema, and the model returns the structured data inside a tool_use content block within the response.

The tool_use block's input field holds the structured object, not the top-level response content. Extract that specific block rather than attempting to JSON.parse the entire response body. The same Zod schema validates the extracted object identically.

vLLM supports schema-constrained decoding through its guided_json parameter, which accepts a JSON Schema object and constrains the model's token generation accordingly. When using the OpenAI SDK against a vLLM endpoint, pass the schema via extra_body:

This works with any model served through vLLM's OpenAI-compatible API endpoint. It avoids per-token billing and keeps data on-premises, making it well-suited for self-hosted deployments where provider-level schema enforcement is desired without vendor lock-in.

Azure OpenAI mirrors the OpenAI API's response_format contract. The same code that targets OpenAI's API works with Azure OpenAI by swapping the base URL and using Azure-specific authentication. Note that Azure OpenAI structured output support depends on the deployed model version and the API version header; not all deployments support json_schema. No schema format changes are required once a compatible deployment is confirmed.

Even with schema-constrained generation, responses still fail validation. A provider might not support strict mode for a particular schema feature. The model might truncate long responses, producing incomplete JSON. Function calling can return partial arguments when the model's context window is exhausted. For these reasons, the architecture follows a three-step flow: send request, parse and validate with Zod, and on failure, retry with the validation error injected into the prompt so the model can self-correct. A hard upper bound on retry attempts prevents infinite loops and runaway API costs.

The function is generic over T, which is inferred from the Zod schema. The fetchResponse callback abstracts away the provider entirely, accepting an optional feedback string that gets injected into the prompt on retry attempts. Parse errors and Zod validation errors are both handled, with feedback constructed from structured Zod issues rather than raw error text. The buildFeedback function caps the number of issues and truncates the message length to prevent overly long or adversarial content from being re-injected into the prompt. The StructuredOutputError thrown on final failure carries the last set of Zod issues for observability and logging.

Note that each retry incurs an additional API call with associated token cost and latency. The maxRetries parameter controls the number of retry attempts after the initial call, so maxRetries: 2 results in up to 3 total API calls.

The returned value carries the full ProductReview type. No type assertion or manual casting is needed. The same pattern applies to Gemini, Anthropic, or any other provider by swapping the callback implementation.

Gemini supports both responseSchema (schema-constrained mode, row 2) and function calling. These are distinct mechanisms with different schema subset limitations. The table above lists Gemini under schema-constrained mode, which is the mechanism demonstrated in this article.

For production systems, combining response_format JSON Schema with the Zod validation wrapper provides the strongest guarantees. The provider-level constraint eliminates most malformed responses at the source, while the Zod layer catches edge cases and provides type safety that persists regardless of which provider is in use. The retry mechanism acts as a safety net that fires infrequently when both layers are active, keeping costs and latency low.

Your Zod validation error says pros.0.details.subRating: Expected number, received string, three levels deep in a nested object the model half-hallucinated. Schemas beyond 3 levels of nesting increase token usage and raise failure rates during constrained generation. Flatten object hierarchies where possible; doing so reduces truncation probability and gives Zod simpler error paths to report on validation failure.

Never use open-loop retries with LLM APIs. Two to three retries (three to four total API calls) is a practical default when provider-level schema constraints are not active. With schema-constrained generation enabled, a single retry handles the most common failure mode: truncated output from long responses. Log every failure with the full Zod issue set for observability so your team can identify schemas that consistently cause problems.

Never use open-loop retries with LLM APIs. Two to three retries (three to four total API calls) is a practical default when provider-level schema constraints are not active.

The JSON Schema acts as a contract between the application and the LLM provider. When it changes, re-test against each provider. A field added to the Zod schema that Gemini cannot express, or a constraint that triggers an OpenAI strict mode rejection, will only surface at runtime. You will only catch these failures before production if you validate schema changes against each provider as part of your CI or deployment process.

Forget to set additionalProperties: false on an OpenAI strict mode call? The API rejects the schema outright, with an error message that does not always point to the root cause. OpenAI strict mode also requires every field in the required array. Gemini does not support $ref or complex oneOf constructs, making inlined, flat schemas the safer choice. Anthropic's tool use returns the structured object inside a tool_use content block. Attempting to JSON.parse the entire API response rather than extracting the specific block is a common source of bugs.

The pattern presented here forms a three-layer defense. Provider-level schema constraints catch malformed output at generation time. Zod runtime validation catches anything the provider misses. Bounded retry with error feedback gives the model a chance to self-correct. The Zod schema is the single source of truth powering all three layers, eliminating the synchronization burden of maintaining separate type definitions, JSON Schemas, and validation logic.

The Zod schema is the single source of truth powering all three layers, eliminating the synchronization burden of maintaining separate type definitions, JSON Schemas, and validation logic.

Adding the validateLLMResponse wrapper to existing LLM calls adds zero latency to calls that validate on the first attempt. It requires no changes to the initial prompt or provider configuration; the retry mechanism appends a correction prompt only on subsequent attempts. As more providers adopt strict JSON schema mode, the retry layer fires less frequently, but the Zod validation boundary still catches the edge cases that slip through.

#10850. 30,000 guests turn up to wedding after groom was worried they wouldn’t have enough people

Around 30,000 guests turned up to a wedding in China after the groom extended an invitation to people via social media.

When it comes to inviting people to your wedding, you might have to make some tough decisions on who makes the cut – especially if you only have a small party with close friends and family.

However, in the case of Xu Chenghong, he extended an invitation to people on social media due to the fact that he has very few relatives who could actually attend the ceremony. “Besides my older brother, there’s been no one else. Just myself, saving 167,500 RMB for the wedding,” he said.

Viewers quickly replied to Chenghong’s video, saying that they’d be there, and while the internet isn’t always great at pulling through on planning, around 10,000 people actually showed up days before the wedding.

As per local reports, the October 31st gathering was described as possibly “China’s biggest ever wedding,” with the sheer number of guests causing disruption in Yiliang County.

One report stated that hotels had no rooms available for all the people who had turned up from different parts of China. Other reports noted that local businesses saw massive boosts too, with some people sticking around for a few days.

Content creator Ur.Chinese.Unc added that people from Hong Kong, Sichuan, and Chongqing turned up with live ducks, cows, apples, home appliances, and a used car for the newlyweds, too.

#10843. React useEffect Cleanup Patterns for Timers, Listeners, and Fetch Races

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Code examples require React 18+. StrictMode double-invocation behavior was introduced in React 18.

Missed cleanup functions in React useEffect are among the most common sources of memory leaks, stale-state bugs, and race conditions. Typical beginner tutorials demonstrate the happy path: fetch data, set state, render. They rarely address what happens when a component unmounts mid-flight, when a dependency changes faster than an async operation resolves, or when event listeners silently pile up across re-renders. This article provides a unified mental model for useEffect cleanup across three categories (timers, listeners, and fetch races), a reference table mapping setup to cleanup to pitfall, and a copy-paste PR review checklist that development teams can adopt immediately.

The mental model is simple: React runs the setup function when the component mounts, runs the cleanup function when the component unmounts, and on every dependency change, runs cleanup for the previous render's effect before running setup for the current render's effect. This cleanup-then-setup cycle on dependency change is the part most developers either overlook or misunderstand. It means that every effect invocation is paired with a corresponding teardown, and React guarantees that ordering. The setup function acquires resources; the cleanup return releases them. The dependency array declares which values, when changed, trigger that cycle again.

React 18's StrictMode intentionally runs each component's effect twice in development by simulating an unmount and remount: setup → cleanup → setup. Your setup function executes twice on the initial render in development. This double invocation exists specifically to surface missing cleanup logic. If a component registers an event listener in setup but never removes it in cleanup, StrictMode will produce two listeners after the simulated remount, making the bug immediately visible during development. As documented in the React docs on StrictMode (https://react.dev/reference/react/StrictMode), this behavior only occurs in development mode and does not affect production builds. However, the bugs it exposes are entirely real. A component that behaves incorrectly under StrictMode's double-mount has a latent cleanup bug that will manifest in production whenever the component remounts due to navigation, conditional rendering, or parent re-renders.

A component that behaves incorrectly under StrictMode's double-mount has a latent cleanup bug that will manifest in production whenever the component remounts due to navigation, conditional rendering, or parent re-renders.

When a setInterval starts inside a useEffect that depends on a prop or state value, every time that dependency changes, React re-runs the effect. Without cleanup, the previous interval continues running. A second interval starts. Then a third. The observable symptom depends on what the interval does: a counter increments N times per tick (where N is the number of orphaned intervals), a polling callback fires multiple times per tick, or a UI animation speeds up erratically. Each orphaned interval holds a closure over stale state, compounding the problem. Under StrictMode's double-mount in development, this bug surfaces immediately as two intervals running from the very first render.

The correct pattern creates the interval in setup and clears it in cleanup. When the dependency changes, React clears the old interval before starting a new one with the updated value. This guarantees exactly one active interval at any point in time.

The same principle applies to setTimeout. Replace setInterval with setTimeout and clearInterval with clearTimeout. Store the timer ID returned by the scheduling function, then clear it in the cleanup return.

Adding an event listener to window, document, or any DOM node outside the component's own render tree requires manual cleanup. Without a matching removeEventListener in the cleanup return, every re-render (or StrictMode double-mount) attaches an additional handler. The handlers accumulate. A resize listener that updates state will trigger setState multiple times per resize event, once for each orphaned listener. This degrades performance and can cause subtle state corruption when multiple handlers race to update the same state variable.

The critical detail is that removeEventListener must receive the exact same function reference passed to addEventListener. An inline arrow function created inside the effect body produces a new reference on every invocation, so calling removeEventListener with a different inline arrow does nothing. The listener is never removed. Declare the handler as a named function (or a variable holding the function) inside the effect, then pass that same variable to both addEventListener and removeEventListener.

The critical detail is that removeEventListener must receive the exact same function reference passed to addEventListener.

If the handler depends on a prop or state value and is defined outside the effect (for example, with useCallback), that handler must appear in the dependency array. When the reference changes, React cleans up the old listener and attaches the new one. Note that re-adding the listener on each dependency change is correct behavior: the old handler (with stale closure) is removed and the new handler (with fresh values) is attached.

Consider a search component where the user types quickly. Each keystroke triggers a new fetch. The request for "rea" might take 300ms while the request for "react" takes 50ms. The "react" response arrives first, and the UI shows the correct results. Then the "rea" response arrives, and because nothing prevented it from calling setState, it overwrites the UI with stale, incorrect results. This is a classic race condition. The slower response wins not because it is more recent, but because it resolves later. Without cancellation, there is no mechanism to tell the browser to discard an in-flight request whose results are no longer relevant.

The modern solution is AbortController. Each effect invocation creates a new controller instance. The controller's signal property is passed to fetch as an option. When the cleanup function runs (because the dependency changed or the component unmounted), it calls controller.abort(), which cancels the in-flight request. The fetch promise rejects with an AbortError. Browsers generally attempt to cancel the underlying network request as well, though the behavior varies by engine (see the Fetch spec on AbortSignal and MDN's browser compatibility tables). This guarantees that only the most recent request can successfully set state.

Calling abort() rejects the promise. This is normal, not an error condition. The catch block must distinguish AbortError from genuine network failures. Without this guard, the UI would flash an error for every intentional cancellation, or the rejection would trigger error-logging infrastructure. The standard check inspects the error's name property: if it equals 'AbortError', the catch block returns early without updating state or surfacing an error.

Note that the effect function itself is not async. An inner async function is declared and called immediately. This matters because useEffect expects its return value to be either undefined or a cleanup function. React ignores a returned promise and never registers a cleanup function from it.

The following table captures the universal pattern across the most common side effects in React applications. Each row shows the same structure: acquire a resource in setup, release it in cleanup, and understand the specific failure mode when cleanup is missing.

WebSocket and Firebase patterns appear here for reference; see MDN's WebSocket API docs and Firebase documentation for full implementation details.

The pattern is always the same: acquire resource, release resource, declare dependencies honestly. If setup creates something, cleanup destroys it. If setup connects, cleanup disconnects. Memorize this symmetry and every new side effect becomes a direct application of the same principle.

This checklist is designed to be dropped directly into a team's contributing guidelines or used as a mental walkthrough during code review. Every question targets a specific category of cleanup bug.

A PR that passes all seven checks covers the most frequently reported categories of cleanup-related bugs before they reach production.

The react-hooks/exhaustive-deps ESLint rule exists to catch missing dependencies that cause stale closures. Disabling the rule hides the bug instead of fixing it. The effect closes over a stale value and never re-runs in response to that value changing, producing a stale closure that is difficult to reproduce and debug. When the rule flags a dependency, add it. If that causes unwanted re-runs, restructure the effect: extract logic, split into multiple effects, or stabilize references with useCallback or useMemo. Suppression should be a last resort, always paired with a comment explaining why the omission is intentional and safe.

Marking the effect callback as async is a common trap. An async function implicitly returns a promise. React expects the return value to be either undefined or a synchronous cleanup function. React ignores a returned promise and never registers a cleanup function, so nothing runs on unmount or dependency change. The fix is always the same: define an inner async function inside the effect and invoke it immediately, keeping the outer effect function synchronous so that the return statement provides a proper cleanup function.

React ignores a returned promise and never registers a cleanup function, so nothing runs on unmount or dependency change.
