#10770. Next.js ISR Transient Errors: Prevent notFound from Caching 404s

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Prerequisites: This article targets Next.js 14 (App Router). For Next.js 15+, see the params awaiting note in each code snippet. Requires Node.js 18.18+. Fetch caching defaults changed in Next.js 15; the ISR throw-to-preserve behavior described here is consistent across 13.4 through 14.x. Run the local simulation test described in the Testing section to confirm behavior on your target version.

A single transient database or CMS outage during Next.js ISR background revalidation can silently replace a perfectly valid cached page with a persistent 404. This is not a theoretical risk. Developers using Next.js ISR with notFound() have reported this exact failure mode, where a temporary infrastructure hiccup causes SEO damage and user-facing downtime that persists long after the underlying service recovers. The root cause is a common error-handling pattern that conflates "the data source is unreachable" with "this record does not exist." This article presents a concrete architectural fix that separates those two states, prevents Next.js from ever caching an error-driven 404, and provides an on-demand revalidation recovery strategy.

In the Next.js App Router, Incremental Static Regeneration operates on a stale-while-revalidate lifecycle. When you set a revalidate interval on a page, the first request generates and caches it. Subsequent requests within that window serve the cached version. Once the interval expires, the next request still receives the stale page, but Next.js triggers a background revalidation. This background render re-executes the page's data-fetching logic and, if successful, replaces the cached output with the fresh render.

The critical detail: the background revalidation replaces whatever is in the cache. If that render produces a 404 response, Next.js caches the 404 and overwrites the previously valid page.

The scenario, reported on Stack Overflow, follows a pattern that exists in countless Next.js codebases. A data-fetching helper wraps a CMS or database call in a try-catch block that returns null on any error, whether that error is a network timeout, a 500 from the upstream API, or a genuine "record not found" response. The page component then checks for null and calls notFound().

During a transient outage, the ISR background render hits the data-fetching error path, receives null, triggers notFound(), and the resulting 404 response is cached. The previously valid, fully rendered page is gone. When the infrastructure recovers seconds or minutes later, the damage is already done. The 404 persists until the next revalidation cycle, and if the timing is unlucky, users and search engine crawlers see a 404 for up to one full revalidation interval (60 seconds in this example, but potentially hours in production configs).

A single transient database or CMS outage during Next.js ISR background revalidation can silently replace a perfectly valid cached page with a persistent 404.

Here is the flawed pattern as it typically appears:

The getPost function treats a CMS outage identically to a missing blog post. Both return null. Both trigger notFound(). Both get cached as 404s.

When a Server Component calls notFound(), it returns a response with a 404 HTTP status code. Under ISR with a configured revalidate interval, Next.js treats this 404 as a valid cacheable response. From the framework's perspective, the page rendered successfully and produced a definitive answer: this resource does not exist. Next.js has no mechanism to distinguish between "this record genuinely doesn't exist in the database" and "an infrastructure error prevented the application from fetching it."

A successful background revalidation, including one that results in a 404, overwrites the existing cached page entirely. Next.js provides no built-in rollback mechanism when the page component returns a valid response (including a 404) without throwing an unhandled exception. If the revalidation completes without throwing, Next.js considers it successful and replaces the cache entry. An unhandled exception is the mechanism that prevents this overwrite -- the distinction the refactored code exploits. This is by design for the happy path but destructive when error handling collapses infrastructure failures into the same code path as legitimate missing records.

This fix hinges on a single architectural insight: when a data helper throws an unhandled exception, Next.js treats the background revalidation as failed and retains the previously cached page. This behavior is consistent with reported Next.js ISR behavior (see the Next.js ISR documentation and related community reports); run the local simulation test described in the Testing section to confirm on your deployment version. The distinction between throwing and returning null directly controls whether ISR preserves or destroys the cache.

The third and fourth rows are the critical behavioral change. By throwing on infrastructure errors, the revalidation is treated as a failure, and the stale cache survives.

The refactored data helper introduces a custom error class for infrastructure failures and only returns null when the upstream API explicitly confirms the record does not exist (a 404 response status). Network errors, timeouts, 500-level responses, and connection failures all throw, preventing ISR from caching the failure state.

Both files reside in the lib/ directory at the project root (i.e., lib/errors.ts and lib/data.ts).

Note on fetch caching: In some Next.js versions, fetch in Server Components defaults to force-cache, meaning the fetch itself may serve a cached response during ISR revalidation -- masking infrastructure failures. The refactored helper above uses { cache: 'no-store' } to ensure data is fetched live during each revalidation. If your data must be fetched live during each revalidation, add { cache: 'no-store' } or { next: { revalidate: 0 } } to your fetch call.

Only the fetch call itself sits inside the try-catch, handling network-level failures. HTTP error responses (non-404) are handled by explicit status checking after the response arrives. Only a 404 from the CMS returns null, signaling a genuinely missing record. A content-type check before JSON parsing prevents silent SyntaxError exceptions when the CMS returns an HTML error page.

The page component stays simple. It calls notFound() only when getPost returns null, meaning the CMS confirmed the record does not exist. Infrastructure errors thrown by the helper are not caught here. They propagate upward, causing the ISR background revalidation to fail gracefully and preserving the existing cache.

By throwing on infrastructure errors, the revalidation is treated as a failure, and the stale cache survives.

Path alias note: The @/lib/data import requires a paths alias configured in your tsconfig.json (e.g., "@/*": ["./*"]). This is set up automatically by create-next-app but must be added manually if not already present in existing projects.

For direct visits (not background revalidation), an uncaught error would show the default Next.js error page. Adding an error.tsx boundary in the route segment catches errors from Server Components in the same route segment during direct page renders. It does not intercept errors during ISR background revalidation -- those are handled server-side and the user continues to see the stale cached page.

If a 404 was cached before implementing this fix, or due to an edge case where the fix was not yet deployed, on-demand revalidation provides a manual or automated recovery mechanism. Next.js exposes revalidatePath() and revalidateTag() for this purpose. revalidatePath() purges and regenerates the cache for a specific URL path. revalidateTag() invalidates all cache entries associated with a given fetch tag, useful for bulk invalidation across related pages.

A route handler that accepts a path or slug, validates an authentication secret, and calls revalidatePath() gives teams a deployable recovery tool.

Environment variable: Set REVALIDATION_SECRET in your .env.local file. Use a long, random string (e.g., 32+ characters generated with openssl rand -base64 32).

Wire this route to a CMS publish webhook so every content update purges the affected path. It also works as an admin dashboard button for manual recovery after an outage. For post-outage scenarios, teams can iterate over a list of affected slugs and trigger revalidation for each, effectively warming the cache with fresh, valid data once the infrastructure has recovered.

Cost consideration: Calling revalidatePath() for many slugs simultaneously triggers a full server-side render for each one. Revalidating 10,000 paths at once means 10,000 concurrent renders -- batch in groups of 50-100 with a delay between batches to avoid spiking compute and CDN costs.

ISR only operates in production mode. Do not use next dev -- ISR is inactive in development mode and all pages are rendered on every request without caching. Tests will not reflect production caching behavior.

To test locally, run next build followed by next start. With the application running, visit a blog post page to populate the cache. Then temporarily modify the data helper to force a throw simulating an infrastructure failure by adding the following line at the top of the try block in getPost, guarded to prevent accidental production deployment:

Set FORCE_FETCH_ERROR=true in your .env.local file before rebuilding. After adding the simulated throw, you must rebuild (next build && next start) since code changes require a rebuild to take effect.

Wait for the revalidation interval to expire (60 seconds with the example configuration), trigger a request, and verify that the cached page survives. The previously rendered content should still be served.

To confirm the legitimate 404 path works, modify the data helper to return null for a specific slug and verify that notFound() produces the expected 404 page.

Check the terminal logs where next start is running. A failed background revalidation will produce error output indicating the thrown exception. Refresh the page in the browser. The stale cached version should still be served, confirming that the cache was not replaced with a 404.

Throw on transient failures so ISR retains the previously valid cached page. This is the single most important behavioral distinction in the entire pattern.

Yes. In the Pages Router, getStaticProps returning notFound: true during ISR revalidation has the same effect: the 404 is cached, replacing the previous page. The same principle applies. Throwing an error from getStaticProps causes the revalidation to fail and preserves the stale cache. Run the local simulation test described in the Testing section against your target version to confirm; see the Next.js Pages Router ISR documentation for authoritative behavior.

No stale cache exists to fall back on. The user sees an error page (or the error.tsx boundary). The page remains uncached until a successful render completes. This is an inherent limitation: the "preserve stale cache" strategy only works when a valid cache entry already exists.

No. Next.js does not support conditional revalidation intervals based on response type. The revalidate value applies uniformly. To retry sooner after a failure, use on-demand revalidation via revalidatePath() or revalidateTag(), triggered either manually or through automated health checks that detect recovery.

#10768. “Giant superatoms” could finally solve quantum computing’s biggest problem

Researchers at Chalmers University of Technology in Sweden have introduced a new theoretical design for quantum systems based on what they call "giant superatoms." This concept offers a fresh way to protect, control, and share quantum information, potentially bringing scientists closer to building large-scale quantum computers.

Quantum computers are expected to transform fields like drug discovery and encryption by solving problems that are far beyond the reach of conventional machines. However, progress has been limited by a major challenge known as decoherence. This occurs when quantum bits, or qubits, lose their information due to interactions with their surroundings. Even small amounts of electromagnetic noise can disrupt the fragile quantum states needed for computation.

"Quantum systems are extraordinarily powerful but also extremely fragile. The key to making them useful is learning how to control their interaction with the surrounding environment," says Lei Du, postdoctoral researcher in applied quantum technology at Chalmers.

Lei Du is the lead author of a study that outlines this new type of quantum system. The design is built around giant superatoms, which combine several important features. These systems reduce decoherence, remain stable, and consist of multiple interconnected "atoms" that function together as a single unit.

Giant superatoms bring together two previously separate ideas in quantum physics: giant atoms and superatoms. While each has been studied on its own, this is the first time they have been merged into a single system. These structures behave like atoms but are not found in nature. Instead, they are engineered by scientists (see fact box below).

The idea of giant atoms was first introduced by researchers at Chalmers over a decade ago and is now widely used in the field. A giant atom is typically designed as a qubit (which is the smallest unit of quantum information). Unlike ordinary atoms, it connects to light or sound waves at multiple, physically separated points. This allows it to interact with its environment in several places at once, helping it preserve quantum information.

"Waves that leave one connection point can travel through the environment and return to affect the atom at another point -- similar to hearing an echo of your own voice before you've finished speaking. This self-interaction leads to highly beneficial quantum effects, reduces decoherence and gives the system a form of memory of past interactions," explains Anton Frisk Kockum, Associate Professor of Applied Quantum Physics at Chalmers and co-author of the study.

Although giant atoms have improved understanding of quantum behavior, they have had limitations when it comes to entanglement. Entanglement allows multiple qubits to share a single quantum state and act as one coordinated system, which is essential for powerful quantum computers.

To overcome this limitation, the research team combined giant atoms with the concept of superatoms. A superatom consists of several natural atoms that share the same quantum state and behave collectively as one larger atom.

This combination is expected to make it easier to create complex quantum states needed for quantum communication, networks, and highly sensitive measurement systems.

"A giant superatom may be envisaged as multiple giant atoms working together as a single entity, exhibiting a non-local interaction between light and matter. This enables quantum information from multiple qubits to be stored and controlled within one unit, without the need for increasingly complex surrounding circuitry," explains Lei Du.

"Giant superatoms open the door to entirely new capabilities, giving us a powerful new toolbox. They allow us to control quantum information and create entanglement in ways that were previously extremely difficult, or even impossible," says Janine Splettstoesser, Professor of Applied Quantum Physics at Chalmers and co-author of the study.

Toward Scalable and Practical Quantum Systems

This work creates new possibilities for building quantum systems that are both scalable and reliable. The researchers plan to move from theory toward actually constructing these systems. Their design could also be integrated with other quantum technologies, serving as a building block for connecting different types of quantum platforms.

"There is currently strong interest in hybrid approaches, in which different quantum systems work together, because each has its own strengths," says Anton Frisk Kockum. "Our research shows that smart design can reduce the need for increasingly complex hardware and giant superatoms are bringing us one step closer to practically applicable quantum technology."

More on: Methods for protecting, controlling and distributing quantum information

The study shows that the way giant superatoms interact with light depends on their internal quantum states. This discovery gives researchers greater control over how quantum information moves through a system. They describe two different ways of connecting these structures to achieve useful outcomes.

In one setup, several giant superatoms are closely linked in a specific arrangement. This allows them to pass quantum states between each other without decoherence, meaning no information is lost.

In another setup, the atoms are spaced farther apart but connected in a carefully tuned way so that waves remain synchronized. This makes it possible to direct quantum signals and distribute entanglement over long distances.

Superatoms and giant atoms are engineered systems that behave like atoms rather than naturally occurring ones.

A superatom is a quantum system made up of multiple natural atoms that share a single quantum state and respond to light as one entity.

A giant atom, on the other hand, connects to light or sound waves at several separate points in space. It is called "giant" because it is larger than the wavelength of the light it interacts with.

Giant atoms have defined energy levels and follow the rules of quantum mechanics, yet they can reach sizes of up to millimeters, making them visible to the naked eye. Through electromagnetic or acoustic waves, they can interact with their surroundings at multiple locations at the same time. One way to picture this is as a single atom linked to a wave at several distant points. This unusual setup allows the atom to be influenced by the waves it produces.

Materials provided by Chalmers University of Technology. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10767. Reddit testing new feature that puts ads in search results

Reddit has announced that it’s testing new advertisements in its search results, meaning users could start seeing sponsored products related to whatever they’re looking for.

In a November 3 announcement, Reddit confirmed that it has started experimenting with additional advertising placements on its search pages.

Rather than showing the same promotions to everyone, the company says the ads can be influenced by what individual users are searching for.

“Ads in search may appear based on a range of factors, including contextual relevance to a user’s search query,” the statement reads.

They used the example that someone looking for advice on building a home office could encounter advertisements for desks, monitors, or office chairs. Likewise, searching for ways to set up a coffee station might bring up promotions for coffee machines, beans, and mugs.

Reddit explained that the idea is to reach users who are already researching particular products or considering purchases. However, the company isn’t rolling out the new placements to every advertiser immediately.

“This is an initial test with a select group of eligible advertisers,” Reddit confirmed. “During this test, we will evaluate and understand how users respond to these ads so we can refine the experience for users and advertisers over time.”

It’s worth noting that Reddit already features advertising across its platform, so this isn’t the introduction of ads to the site for the first time.

Instead, the experiment focuses on additional ads while searching, potentially making sponsored products more prominent when users are looking for posts or communities.

#10762. This simple change stops robot swarms from getting stuck

Imagine a swarm of robots rushing to complete an urgent job, such as cleaning up an oil spill or assembling complex machinery. At first, adding more robots speeds things up. But after a certain point, the space becomes crowded, robots start interfering with one another, and overall progress slows.

This raises a simple but important question: in a limited area, how many robots can you deploy before efficiency starts to drop? Researchers at Harvard believe they have found a clear answer.

A new study from the lab of L. Mahadevan, the Lola England de Valpine Professor of Applied Mathematics, Organismic and Evolutionary Biology, and Physics, shows that adding a controlled amount of randomness to how robots move can reduce congestion and improve performance in crowded environments.

The work combines mathematical modeling, computer simulations, and real-world experiments. It demonstrates how basic local movement rules can lead to organized, efficient outcomes on a larger scale. The findings could influence how robotic fleets are designed and may even apply to human crowd management and traffic flow. The research was published in Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences and led by applied mathematics Ph.D. student Lucy Liu, with guidance from SEAS Senior Research Fellow Justin Werfel.

Why Randomness Helps Predict Complex Behavior

Studying dense crowds is difficult because individuals can take countless possible paths and interact in unpredictable ways, Liu explained. To simplify the problem, the researchers treated each robot as a basic unit with a small, adjustable amount of variation in its movement.

"This might be counterintuitive, because how could randomness make things easier to work with?" said Liu. "But in this case, when you have a lot of randomness, it becomes possible to take averages -- average distances, average times, average behaviors. This makes it a lot easier to make predictions."

To explore this idea, the team created computer simulations of robot groups, referred to as agents. Each agent started at a random location and was assigned a random destination. Once it reached its target, it immediately received a new one, mimicking continuous task assignment in real-world systems.

Each agent moved toward its goal with a tunable amount of variation, described as "noise." With no noise, agents moved in straight lines. With high noise, their paths became erratic and inefficient. However, this wandering also helped them navigate around one another.

The simulations revealed a clear pattern. When agents moved in perfectly straight paths, they quickly formed dense clusters and traffic jams that halted progress. When movement became too random, congestion disappeared but efficiency dropped due to excessive wandering.

Between these extremes, the researchers identified a sweet spot. In this range, agents occasionally bumped into one another and formed short-lived clusters, but still managed to slip past and keep moving. This balance allowed the system to maintain a steady flow.

Using these insights, the team developed formulas to estimate "goal attainment rate," or how many destinations are reached over time. These equations made it possible to determine the ideal combination of crowd density and movement randomness to maximize performance.

To confirm their findings, Liu collaborated with physicist Federico Toschi at Eindhoven University of Technology in the Netherlands. Together, they set up experiments with small wheeled robots in a lab equipped with an overhead camera.

Each robot carried a QR code so its position could be tracked and updated with new destinations. Although the physical robots moved more slowly and less precisely than the simulated agents, they displayed the same overall patterns.

The experiments supported a key idea: highly complex coordination does not require advanced intelligence or centralized control. Instead, simple local rules can produce effective group behavior, at least within certain density limits.

"Understanding how active matter, whether it is a swarm of ants, a herd of animals, or a group of robots, become functional and execute tasks in crowded environments using the principles of self-organization, is relevant to many questions in behavioral ecology," Mahadevan said. "Our study suggests strategies that might well be much broader than the instantiation we have focused on."

Liu noted that she has long been interested in designing safer and more efficient crowded spaces. This research points toward a future where the movement of large groups, whether robots, vehicles, or people, could be predicted and optimized using mathematical tools.

The results suggest that introducing controlled variability into movement patterns may improve flow in many real-world systems, from factory floors to city streets.

Funding for the research came from the National Science Foundation Graduate Research Fellowship Program under Grant No. DGE 2140743, along with grants from the Simons Foundation and the Henri Seydoux Fund.

Materials provided by Harvard John A. Paulson School of Engineering and Applied Sciences. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10760. Codebase Design for AI Agents: Types, Lint Rules, and CI Gates

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

AI coding agents generate production code, shifting the developer's role from writer to reviewer. That shift exposes a specific failure mode: the constraints a codebase enforces at compile time and CI time determine agent output quality, not natural-language prompt quality.

AI coding agents -- Copilot, Cursor, Codex, Devin, and their successors -- are now standard tooling in many engineering teams. They generate production code, shifting the developer's role from writer to reviewer. That shift exposes a specific failure mode: natural-language prompt quality does not primarily determine agent output quality. The constraints a codebase enforces at compile time and CI time determine it.

Engineers who run AI coding agents in production, including Lauren Tan (@poteto), have argued publicly that traditional codebase hygiene is the highest-leverage investment for agent correctness. The core argument: making the correct path the easiest one, through types, invariants, and architectural boundaries, beats prompt engineering or post-hoc code review. When a compiler rejects invalid code before a pull request is even opened, no amount of conversational finesse matters.

When a compiler rejects invalid code before a pull request is even opened, no amount of conversational finesse matters.

This tutorial builds a minimal, runnable repository scaffold that demonstrates four complementary guardrails: branded types that enforce domain invariants at compile time, a custom ESLint rule that catches convention violations through static analysis, a GitHub Actions CI gate that prevents non-conforming code from merging, and an AGENTS.md guidance file that provides soft contextual nudges agents can read. These layers enforce constraints at four different points between code generation and merge, and each layer reinforces the others.

Before following this tutorial, ensure you have the following installed locally:

Node.js 20 or later is required. The project assumes TypeScript 4.5+ (the branded type pattern uses unique symbol, available since TS 2.7, but modern TS is expected). You will also need ESLint v9, since this tutorial uses the flat config API and context.filename, both v9 features. Install @typescript-eslint/parser for the RuleTester examples, and have a test runner available -- Vitest, Jest, or the built-in Node.js test runner all work.

AI coding agents generate code that satisfies the immediate request. They lack whole-codebase awareness of unwritten conventions, team norms, or architectural decisions that exist only in developers' heads. Without machine-enforceable constraints, an agent defaults to the loosest valid pattern. Asked to fetch a user, it will accept a raw string parameter rather than seeking out a branded UserId type. It will import the database client directly rather than routing through a service layer. The agent is not being careless. It optimizes for the shortest path to syntactically valid code.

A useful way to organize guardrails is as a hierarchy ordered by enforcement strength and feedback speed:

Each layer compounds the effectiveness of the others. Types catch the majority of structural errors. Lint rules catch convention drift that types miss. CI gates catch anything that slips past local tooling. Documentation explains the why behind constraints, helping agents make better choices in ambiguous situations.

Consider a common function signature: function getUser(id: string). That parameter accepts any string. An email address, a raw UUID, an order ID, an empty string. An AI agent generating a call to getUser will happily pass whatever string-shaped value is closest at hand, with no compiler objection.

Branded types solve this by intersecting a base type with a unique symbol tag, creating a compile-time-only distinction between structurally identical types. A UserId and an OrderId are both strings at runtime, but the compiler treats them as incompatible types. The technique carries zero runtime overhead because the compiler erases the brand during compilation.

Important: Any direct cast such as "value" as UserId anywhere in the codebase bypasses the brand guarantee entirely. The TypeScript compiler will not prevent this. To enforce constructor-only creation, add an ESLint rule (e.g., @typescript-eslint/consistent-type-assertions) configured to ban as casts to branded types.

When an agent generates a call to getUser, the TypeScript compiler immediately rejects a raw string argument. The error message in the agent's context window points to the type mismatch, forcing the agent to locate or create a UserId through the createUserId constructor function, which automatically enforces format validation. The agent does not need to know the convention. The type system prevents the wrong path. Every domain identifier that uses a branded type becomes a self-documenting, self-enforcing contract that agents must satisfy to produce code that compiles.

The agent does not need to know the convention. The type system prevents the wrong path.

Not every team convention belongs in a lint rule. Good candidates share a common trait: they can be detected via AST inspection and have a clear, unambiguous correct alternative. Banning direct imports from internal modules, enforcing barrel-file usage, and preventing any in specific directories are all strong fits. Rules that require judgment calls or contextual understanding are poor candidates.

This tutorial focuses on a rule that forbids importing from @/db outside the @/services directory, enforcing a service-layer boundary. Without this rule, an agent scaffolding a new HTTP handler would likely import the database client directly, bypassing the service layer and scattering data-access logic across the codebase.

Note: This rule requires ESLint v9 with flat config. The context.filename property is the ESLint v9 flat-config API. The legacy context.getFilename() is deprecated in v9.

Note for Windows users: The filename.replace(/\\/g, "/") normalization handles Windows backslash separators and mixed-separator strings reliably.

When an agent's context window includes the test file, RuleTester fixtures serve as living documentation: the agent can see concrete examples of what is permitted and what is not. This doubles as both verification and guidance.

An agent scaffolding a new handler tries to query the database directly by importing from @/db. The lint step fails with: "Use a service function from @/services instead." That message points the agent toward the correct architectural pattern -- immediate, actionable, and unambiguous. The agent does not need to have read a wiki page or remembered a Slack conversation.

Agents frequently push code to feature branches and open pull requests automatically. CI must run before any human review to surface issues cheaply and quickly. The workflow follows a fail-early sequential ordering: type-check first, then lint, then test. If the type-check fails, there is no point running linting or tests on code that does not compile. Because steps in a single job are sequential, any failing step halts the job immediately.

The CI workflow alone is not sufficient. Without branch protection, a determined agent or developer can merge a pull request despite failures. In the GitHub repository settings under Branches, enable branch protection for main: require status checks to pass before merging, specifically require the guardrails job from the Agent Guardrails workflow, and disable bypass permissions for bot accounts. This combination ensures that no code reaches the default branch without satisfying every automated check. The workflow becomes a hard gate rather than an advisory signal.

Note: Some branch protection features (such as restricting bypass permissions) may require a paid GitHub plan for private repositories.

AGENTS.md is a convention for placing natural-language guidance at the repository root that agent frameworks can ingest as system-level context. Variations of this convention include CLAUDE.md for Claude Code and .cursorrules for Cursor. OpenAI Codex CLI also reads AGENTS.md from the repository root. The agents and tools that currently support reading repository-level guidance files include OpenAI Codex CLI, Claude Code, Cursor, and Windsurf. The agent framework loads the content into the agent's context window alongside the codebase when supported, making it available during code generation without requiring explicit prompting.

Agents may ignore, truncate, or misinterpret documentation. For example, Claude Code truncates files that exceed its context window limit, and instructions that work with one model version may produce different behavior after an update. AGENTS.md works best as a complement to compiler and linter enforcement, not a replacement. It fills gaps that hard enforcement cannot cover: explaining why a boundary exists, pointing to specific file paths, and providing checklists that aid both agents and human reviewers.

A practical maintenance habit: review and update AGENTS.md during sprint retrospectives. As new branded types, lint rules, or architectural boundaries are added, the guidance file must reflect them, or agents will lack the context to follow new conventions.

The complete scaffold follows this directory structure:

The ESLint flat config file wires the custom rule into the project:

Your package.json should include at minimum the following scripts and dev dependencies:

Note: The "test" script above uses the built-in Node.js test runner (Node 20+) with an explicit glob pattern to ensure test file discovery works reliably across Node versions. Replace with your preferred test runner (e.g., vitest run or jest) if needed.

Your tsconfig.json should include path aliases to support the @/ import convention used in the codebase:

To run this scaffold locally, create the directory structure above, add each file from the code blocks in this tutorial, install the dev dependencies, and run npm install && npm test to verify all guardrails pass. From there, adapt the branded types and lint rules to match your domain.

Prompt instructions that work on GPT-4o may produce different results on Claude or on the next model update. Natural-language instructions degrade across context windows, model updates, and agent implementations. Structural enforcement through types, lint rules, and CI gates is durable. It works regardless of which agent generates the code, which model version powers it, or whether the human reviewer catches every subtlety.

Structural enforcement through types, lint rules, and CI gates is durable. It works regardless of which agent generates the code, which model version powers it, or whether the human reviewer catches every subtlety.

The practical path forward is incremental. Start with one branded type for a critical domain identifier and one lint rule for the most frequently violated architectural boundary. Expand coverage as agent usage grows and new patterns of convention drift emerge. Clone the scaffold, adapt it to your domain, and let the compiler do the reviewing.

#10758. Quantum AI just got shockingly good at predicting chaos

A new study led by researchers at UCL (University College London) shows that combining quantum computing with artificial intelligence can significantly improve predictions of complex physical systems over long periods. The hybrid approach outperforms leading models that rely only on conventional computers.

The results, published in Science Advances, could enhance simulations of how liquids and gases behave, known as fluid dynamics. These types of models are essential in fields such as climate science, transportation, medicine, and energy production.

The improved accuracy appears to come from how quantum computers process information. Unlike traditional computers that use bits set to either 1 or 0, quantum computers use qubits, which can exist as 1, 0, or anything in between. In addition, each qubit can influence others, allowing a relatively small number of qubits to represent an enormous number of possible states.

Professor Peter Coveney, senior author from UCL Chemistry and the Advanced Research Computing Centre, explained the challenge: "To make predictions about complex systems, we can either run a full simulation, which might take weeks -- often too long to be useful -- or we can use an AI model which is quicker but more unreliable over longer time scales.

"Our quantum-informed AI model means we could provide more accurate predictions quickly. Making predictions about fluid flow and turbulence is a fundamental science challenge but it also has many applications. Our method can be used in climate forecasting, in modeling blood flow and the interaction of molecules, or to better design wind farms so they generate more energy."

Although quantum computers are widely expected to surpass classical machines in power, their real-world use has so far been limited. This new approach integrates quantum computing into a specific stage of the AI training process.

Typically, AI models learn from large datasets generated by simulations or observations. In this case, the data is first processed by a quantum computer, which identifies key statistical patterns that remain stable over time. These patterns, known as invariant statistical properties, are then used to guide the training of an AI model running on a conventional supercomputer.

The quantum-informed AI system delivered about 20 percent greater accuracy compared to standard AI models that did not use quantum-derived patterns. It also maintained stable predictions over longer periods, even when modeling chaotic systems.

Another major advantage was efficiency. The method required hundreds of times less memory, making it far more practical for large-scale simulations.

This performance boost comes from two defining features of quantum computing. Entanglement allows qubits to influence each other regardless of distance, while superposition enables a qubit to exist in multiple states at once until measured. Together, these properties allow quantum systems to process vast amounts of information in a compact form.

Demonstrating Practical Quantum Advantage

First author Maida Wang of the UCL Centre for Computational Science said: "Our new method appears to demonstrate 'quantum advantage' in a practical way -- that is, the quantum computer outperforms what is possible through classical computing alone. These findings could inspire the development of novel classical approaches that achieve even higher accuracy, though they would likely lack the remarkable data compression and parameter efficiency offered by our method. The next steps are to scale up the method using larger datasets and to apply it to real-world situations which typically involve even more complexity. In addition, a provable theoretical framework will be proposed."

Co-first author Xiao Xue, from Advanced Research Computing at UCL, added: "In this work, we demonstrate for the first time that quantum computing can be meaningfully integrated with classical machine learning methods to tackle complex dynamical systems, including fluid mechanics. It is exciting to see this kind of 'quantum-informed' approach moving towards practical use."

The researchers suggest that quantum computers are particularly well suited to modeling these systems because they can compactly represent their underlying physics. Many complex systems behave in ways that resemble quantum effects, where changes in one region can influence distant parts of the system, similar to entanglement.

Overcoming Limits of Current Quantum Hardware

Current quantum computers face challenges such as noise, errors, and interference, which often require large numbers of measurements. The new method avoids these issues by using the quantum computer only once during the workflow, rather than repeatedly exchanging data between quantum and classical systems.

The study used a 20-qubit IQM quantum computer connected to powerful classical computing resources at the Leibniz Supercomputing Centre in Germany.

To function, quantum computers must operate at extremely low temperatures, around minus 273C (close to absolute zero, colder than anything in space).

The research was funded by UCL and the UK's Engineering and Physical Sciences Research Council (EPSRC), with additional support from IQM Quantum Computers and the Leibniz Supercomputing Centre in Munich.

As researchers continue to scale up this approach, it could open the door to more accurate and efficient predictions across a wide range of scientific and engineering applications.

Materials provided by University College London. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10756. The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time remake review – A masterpiece made better

The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time is the most critically acclaimed game ever released, and Nintendo has worked its magic once again, pairing a smorgasbord of improvements with breathtakingly beautiful visuals to make an extraordinary adventure even better.

It’s been nearly three decades since Ocarina of Time was released on the Nintendo 64, and while the 1998 epic famously returned with a remake for the 3DS, Link’s time-travelling quest has never truly received the overhaul it deserves.

While Ocarina of Time famously helped revolutionize gaming with its Z-targeting system and 3D interconnected world, the legendary title feels pretty dated when compared to new adventures like Breath of the Wild and Tears of the Kingdom.

Enter the Switch 2 remake, an enhanced version of Nintendo’s masterpiece boasting completely new visuals and game-changing quality-of-life improvements that make Ocarina of Time look and play better than ever before.

Ocarina of Time follows Link, a young boy tasked with saving the kingdom of Hyrule from Ganondorf. He teams up with Hyrule’s Princess Zelda, who tells him that Ganondorf is seeking the Triforce, an ancient relic capable of granting a wish to whoever secures all three pieces.

To stop Ganondorf from plunging Hyrule into darkness, the green-clad hero must claim the legendary Master Sword and embark on a time-hopping quest to awaken the Seven Sages, whose power is needed to defeat him and save the kingdom.

Ocarina of Time remake packs plenty of improvements that elevate the 27-year-old adventure, with many plucked from Breath of the Wild and Tears of the Kingdom. The biggest, and most welcome, is the addition of a jump and a run button, which gives you greater control and freedom when exploring Hyrule and its dungeons.

Zelda fans obsessed with Dark Link’s “aura farming” in Ocarina of Time remake trailer

Ocarina of Time remake launches November 5 with major gameplay changes

Zelda: Ocarina of Time remake sparks endless memes about Link’s new look

That’s provided 90s muscle memory doesn’t kick in, and you actually remember there’s no auto jump and you don’t plummet off a ledge into the ground with a pained yelp from Link.

Being able to run indefinitely is also a fantastic feature, especially given how time-consuming traversal can be as Young Link in early sections when you don’t have access to Epona or location-warping Ocarina songs. It also avoids your ears from being bombarded with the shrill ‘Hyuuuuup! Hyaaaaa!’ rolling sound effects of the original, always a bonus for anyone who wants to speed things up without losing their sanity.

Threads of Time also helps you keep track of every quest you pick up during your journey, which proved extremely useful when working through all the Happy Mask Salesman requests and trying to remember the steps in the comically long-winded Biggoron’s Sword quest.

One of the most wholesome additions is the ability to play Link’s ocarina by humming, singing, or playing a real ocarina using the Switch 2’s microphone to activate the songs in-game. While not necessary, being rewarded for memorizing Zelda’s Lullaby or Epona’s Song is pretty cute stuff, even if your neighbors may not share the same enthusiasm.

Then there’s the ability to use gyro controls to aim Link’s bow and ranged gadgets like the Hookshot, which really helps with catching Poes on horseback, hitting hidden switches to open secret doors, and whacking bosses where it hurts when chasing the best clear times in the new Hall of Fallen Foes boss rush mode.

Nintendo has also improved combat, with the Master Sword now having a real sense of heft behind it. Striking enemies comes with satisfying feedback from the Switch 2 controller’s improved HD rumble, giving your slashes a weighty feel. This is especially true when using the Megaton Hammer to flatten foes like a pancake or slicing an enemy with the two-handed Biggorn Sword.

While Wolfos and Skulltulas still rely on you waiting for that perfect opportunity to hit their weak points, tougher enemies like Lizalfos and Gerudo Warriors are more reactive, flitting in and out of combat and dashing in for some cheeky damage when you let your guard down.

Ocarina of Time’s bosses are also more of a spectacle now, with upgraded arenas, attacks, and flashier animations. Remake’s Ganon is bigger and badder than his older, blockier brother. In fact, he makes his 1998 counterpart look positively tame. From raining down blood-red lightning strikes from the sky to charging on all fours, his demonic beast form is even more badass than ever before.

While none of these improvements drastically change the adventure you know and love, they do make an already great game even better.

The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time Remake isn’t just the best-looking Nintendo Switch 2 game, it’s also the most detailed Zelda ever. Kokiri Forest is filled with lush green foliage, moss-covered rocks, and beautiful babbling brooks.

Hyrule Field is also blanketed with swaying grass and pretty wildflowers, while rugged terrain near Gerudo Valley and rocky formations near Zora’s River give these areas a more distinct look compared to the flatter landscape of the original. Environmental details like Kakariko Village’s signature windmill and houses nestled on the hills beneath Hyrule Castle also help make this smaller-scale adventure feel more lived-in than ever.

Ocarina of Time’s newly added signs of life were especially apparent in populated locations like the sleepy mountainside dwellings of Kakariko Village and the cobbled streets of Hyrule Castle Town. Ducking into alleyways of the latter and having a right good nosy proved particularly good fun, especially now that you can freely explore and interact with the colorful townsfolk.

Nintendo has carried this same level of detail into the remake’s dungeons, where atmospheric fog carpets the stone floors and reacts to Link’s footsteps and sword swings. Dust particles also now hang in the air and glisten as they catch the light, while deeper shadows and improved lighting heighten iconic areas like Kakariko’s nightmarish well and Ganondorf’s Castle.

Even the Hero of Time and his companions have received a graphical glow-up, with hyper-realistic hair and outfits. The most eye-catching is Link’s iconic tunic, which now features woven green straw fibers, intricately designed belts, and enough tassels to have cosplayers curse Nintendo for the level of intricacy in his new, highly detailed drip.

For veteran Zelda players, exploring these well-trodden environments feels like returning to your favorite childhood haunts as an adult. Although each setting has had a significant upgrade, navigating Hyrule and its dungeons feels familiar but fresh. Nintendo has taken so much care recreating its beloved world that it’s hard not to get pulled back into another 20+ hour adventure, gallivanting around Hyrule atop Epona searching for Heart Containers like it’s 1998 all over again.

Just like Breath of the Wild and Tears of the Kingdom, everyone barring Link and minor NPC characters is now fully voiced. While this may be a controversial addition for purists hoping to go back to Zelda’s silent era, the new cutscenes help add more life to the Seven Sages and provide more details that will have lore lovers happy to revisit Hyrule’s history.

Characters like the Goron patriarch Darunia are now way more expressive, while Nintendo has done more for Princess Ruto and Link’s one-sided romance than even the most die-hard fanfiction writer. There’s also a greater sense of emotion behind certain events, like when Link leaves Kokiri Forest thanks to some heartfelt voiced lines from Saria. Then there’s Ganondorf himself, whose grand entrance and signature organ playing are elevated further by some particularly infectious evil laughter that I may or may not have tried to imitate.

Whether you’re a returning player or a newcomer, Ocarina of Time remake is the definitive way to play Link’s legendary adventure. Nintendo has successfully breathed new life into its decades-old world of Hyrule, giving it a loving lick of paint that not only elevates it over the original, but also makes it the best-looking Switch 2 game.

Just like Link’s time-travelling epic, Ocarina of Time remake honors its past and carefully combines new improvements borrowed from the series’ present to give this timeless classic the treatment it deserves. Ocarina of Time remake isn’t just a masterpiece, it’s a timeless adventure everyone needs to play.

#10754. Think AI "knows" what it’s doing? Scientists say think again

These are everyday words people use to describe what goes on in the human mind. But when those same terms are applied to artificial intelligence, they can unintentionally make machines seem more human than they really are.

"We use mental verbs all the time in our daily lives, so it makes sense that we might also use them when we talk about machines -- it helps us relate to them," said Jo Mackiewicz, professor of English at Iowa State. "But at the same time, when we apply mental verbs to machines, there's also a risk of blurring the line between what humans and AI can do."

Mackiewicz and Jeanine Aune, a teaching professor of English and director of the advanced communication program at Iowa State, are part of a research team that studied how writers describe AI using human-like language. This type of wording, known as anthropomorphism, assigns human traits to non-human systems. Their study, "Anthropomorphizing Artificial Intelligence: A Corpus Study of Mental Verbs Used with AI and ChatGPT," was published in Technical Communication Quarterly.

The research team also included Matthew J. Baker, associate professor of linguistics at Brigham Young University, and Jordan Smith, assistant professor of English at the University of Northern Colorado. Both previously studied at Iowa State University.

Why Human-Like Language About AI Can Be Misleading

According to the researchers, using mental verbs to describe AI can create a false impression. Words such as "think," "know," "understand," and "want" suggest that a system has thoughts, intentions, or awareness. In reality, AI does not possess beliefs or feelings. It produces responses by analyzing patterns in data, not by forming ideas or making conscious decisions.

Mackiewicz and Aune also pointed out that this kind of language can overstate what AI is capable of. Phrases like "AI decided" or "ChatGPT knows" can make systems seem more independent or intelligent than they actually are. This can lead to unrealistic expectations about how reliable or capable AI is.

There is also a broader concern. When AI is described as if it has intentions, it can distract from the humans behind it. Developers, engineers, and organizations are responsible for how these systems are built and used.

"Certain anthropomorphic phrases may even stick in readers' minds and can potentially shape public perception of AI in unhelpful ways," Aune said.

How News Writers Actually Use AI Language

To better understand how often this kind of language appears, the researchers analyzed the News on the Web (NOW) corpus. This massive dataset contains more than 20 billion words from English-language news articles published in 20 countries.

They focused on how frequently mental verbs such as "learns," "means," and "knows" were used alongside terms like AI and ChatGPT.

Mental Verbs Are Less Common Than Expected

The study found that news writers do not frequently pair AI-related terms with mental verbs.

While anthropomorphism is common in everyday speech, it appears far less often in news writing. "Anthropomorphism has been shown to be common in everyday speech, but we found there's far less usage in news writing," Mackiewicz said.

Among the examples identified, the word "needs" appeared most often with AI, showing up 661 times. For ChatGPT, "knows" was the most frequent pairing, but it appeared only 32 times.

The researchers noted that editorial standards may play a role. Associated Press guidelines, which discourage attributing human emotions or traits to AI, could be influencing how journalists write about these technologies.

Context Matters More Than the Words Themselves

Even when mental verbs were used, they were not always anthropomorphic.

For instance, the word "needs" often described basic requirements rather than human-like qualities. Phrases such as "AI needs large amounts of data" or "AI needs some human assistance" are similar to how people describe non-human systems like cars or recipes. In these cases, the language does not imply that AI has thoughts or desires.

In other cases, "needs" was used to express what should be done, such as "AI needs to be trained" or "AI needs to be implemented." Aune explained that these examples were often written in passive voice, which shifts responsibility back to human actors rather than the technology itself.

The study also showed that not all uses of mental verbs are equal. Some phrases move closer to suggesting human-like qualities.

For example, statements like "AI needs to understand the real world" can imply expectations tied to human reasoning, ethics, or awareness. These uses go beyond simple descriptions and begin to suggest deeper capabilities.

"These instances showed that anthropomorphizing isn't all-or-nothing and instead exists on a spectrum," Aune said

Overall, the researchers found that anthropomorphism in news coverage is both less frequent and more nuanced than many might assume.

"Overall, our analysis shows that anthropomorphization of AI in news writing is far less common -- and far more nuanced -- than we might think," Mackiewicz said. "Even the instances that did anthropomorphize AI varied widely in strength."

The findings highlight the importance of context. Simply counting words is not enough to understand how language shapes meaning.

"For writers, this nuance matters: the language we choose shapes how readers understand AI systems, their capabilities and the humans responsible for them," Mackiewicz said.

The research team also emphasized that these insights can help professionals think more carefully about how they describe AI in their work.

"Our findings can help technical and professional communication practitioners reflect on how they think about AI technologies as tools in their writing process and how they write about AI," the research team wrote in the published study.

As AI continues to develop, the way people talk about it will remain important. Mackiewicz and Aune said writers will need to stay mindful of how word choices influence perception.

Looking ahead, the team suggested that future studies could explore how different words shape understanding and whether even rare uses of anthropomorphic language have a strong impact on how people view AI.

Materials provided by Iowa State University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10752. UK plans to restrict voice chat in games for under-16s

The UK government has confirmed that it intends to put restrictions on in-game voice chat for under-16s in games in the near future, but not everyone is pleased.

In-game voice chat has long been a way for gamers to get one over on their opponents. Even with a rise in third-party apps like Discord and TeamSpeak, players still flock to game chat to mock their enemies.

However, in the UK, that is likely to be restricted for anyone under the age of 16 as part of the under-16s social media ban that will come into effect in the spring.

“We will then bring forward a host of regulations on features and functions, including stranger communications, which is the biggest problem with Roblox, and some of the other issues that hon. Members have raised,” Ian Murray, the UK Government’s Online Safety Minister, told a debate on November 2.

“That will allow gamers to continue to have the functionality of stranger pairing, but without the ability to talk to strangers in a gaming context. That should resolve some of these issues.”

As per Gamer’s Voice, the new proposal would “require all gamers to prove their age in order to access communication features,” similar to how some nations deal with in-game gambling features.

“Gaming isn’t simply another form of social media. When young people join a gaming guild, work together in a team or make friends through a shared hobby, they are participating in a social activity that can be enormously positive,” Tom Shannon, spokesperson for Gamer’s Voice, said.

#10750. Artificial neurons successfully communicate with living brain cells

Engineers at Northwestern University have created printed artificial neurons that go beyond imitation and can directly interact with real brain cells. These flexible, low-cost devices produce electrical signals that closely resemble those generated by living neurons, allowing them to activate biological brain tissue.

In experiments using slices of mouse brain, the artificial neurons successfully triggered responses in real neurons. This result shows a new level of compatibility between electronic devices and living neural systems.

Toward Brain Interfaces and Energy-Efficient AI

This advance moves researchers closer to electronics that can directly interface with the nervous system. Potential uses include brain-machine interfaces and neuroprosthetics, such as implants that could help restore hearing, vision, or movement.

The technology also points toward a new generation of computing systems inspired by the brain. By replicating how neurons communicate, future hardware could perform complex tasks using far less energy. The brain remains the most energy-efficient computing system known, and scientists hope to apply its principles to modern technology.

The study will be published on April 15 in the journal Nature Nanotechnology.

"The world we live in today is dominated by artificial intelligence (AI)," said Northwestern's Mark C. Hersam, who led the study. "The way you make AI smarter is by training it on more and more data. This data-intensive training leads to a massive power-consumption problem. Therefore, we have to come up with more efficient hardware to handle big data and AI. Because the brain is five orders of magnitude more energy efficient than a digital computer, it makes sense to look to the brain for inspiration for next-generation computing."

Hersam is an expert in brain-inspired computing and holds multiple roles at Northwestern University, including the Walter P. Murphy Professor of Materials Science and Engineering at the McCormick School of Engineering. He also is a professor of medicine at Northwestern University Feinberg School of Medicine and a professor of chemistry at the Weinberg College of Arts and Sciences. In addition, he serves as chair of the department of materials science and engineering, director of the Materials Research Science and Engineering Center, and a member of the International Institute for Nanotechnology. He co-led the study with Vinod K. Sangwan, a research associate professor at McCormick.

Why the Brain Outperforms Traditional Silicon

Modern computers handle increasing workloads by packing billions of identical transistors onto rigid, two-dimensional silicon chips. Each component behaves the same way, and once manufactured, the system remains fixed.

The brain works very differently. It consists of many types of neurons, each with specialized roles, arranged in soft, three-dimensional networks. These networks are constantly changing, forming and adjusting connections as learning occurs.

"Silicon achieves complexity by having billions of identical devices," Hersam said. "Everything is the same, rigid and fixed once it's fabricated. The brain is the opposite. It's heterogeneous, dynamic and three-dimensional. To move in that direction, we need new materials and new ways to build electronics."

Although artificial neurons have been developed before, most produce overly simple signals. To achieve more complex behavior, engineers typically need large networks of devices, which increases energy use.

Printable Materials Enable Brain-Like Behavior

To better replicate real neural activity, Hersam's team built artificial neurons using soft, printable materials that more closely match the brain's structure. Their approach relies on electronic inks made from nanoscale flakes of molybdenum disulfide (MoS2), which acts as a semiconductor, and graphene, which serves as an electrical conductor. These materials were deposited onto flexible polymer surfaces using aerosol jet printing.

Previously, researchers treated the polymer in these inks as a flaw because it interfered with electrical performance. As a result, they removed it after printing. In this work, the team used that same feature to enhance the device.

"Instead of fully removing the polymer, we partially decompose it," he said. "Then, when we pass current through the device, we drive further decomposition of the polymer. This decomposition occurs in a spatially inhomogeneous manner, leading to formation of a conductive filament, such that all the current is constricted into a narrow region in space."

That narrow conductive path produces a sudden electrical response similar to a neuron firing. The resulting device can generate a wide variety of signals, including single spikes, continuous firing, and bursting patterns, closely resembling real neural communication.

Because each artificial neuron can produce more complex signals, fewer components are needed to perform advanced tasks. This could significantly improve computing efficiency.

Testing Artificial Neurons on Real Brain Tissue

To evaluate whether the artificial neurons could truly interact with living systems, the researchers partnered with Indira M. Raman, the Bill and Gayle Cook Professor of Neurobiology at Weinberg. Her team applied the artificial signals to slices of mouse cerebellum.

The results showed that the electrical spikes matched key biological properties, including their timing and duration. These signals reliably activated real neurons and triggered neural circuits in a way similar to natural brain activity.

"Other labs have tried to make artificial neurons with organic materials, and they spiked too slowly," Hersam said. "Or they used metal oxides, which are too fast. We are within a temporal range that was not previously demonstrated for artificial neurons. You can see the living neurons respond to our artificial neuron. So, we've demonstrated signals that are not only the right timescale but also the right spike shape to interact directly with living neurons."

Low-Cost, Sustainable Manufacturing and AI Implications

Beyond performance, the new approach offers environmental and practical advantages. The manufacturing process is simple and inexpensive, and the additive printing method places material only where it is needed, reducing waste.

Improving energy efficiency is especially important as artificial intelligence systems grow more demanding. Large data centers already consume vast amounts of power and require significant water for cooling.

"To meet the energy demands of AI, tech companies are building gigawatt data centers powered by dedicated nuclear power plants," Hersam said. "It is evident that this massive power consumption will limit further scaling of computing since it's hard to imagine a next-generation data center requiring 100 nuclear power plants. The other issue is that when you're dissipating gigawatts of power, there's a lot of heat. Because data centers are cooled with water, AI is putting severe stress on the water supply. However you look at it, we need to come up with more energy-efficient hardware for AI."

The study, "Multi-order complexity spiking neurons enabled by printed MoS2 memristive nanosheet networks," was supported by the National Science Foundation.

Materials provided by Northwestern University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10748. Next.js Hydration Errors from localStorage: 3 Ways to Fix Them

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Hydration is the process where React, running in the browser, attaches event handlers to server-rendered HTML and reconciles that static markup with the virtual DOM tree the client would have produced. For hydration to succeed, the HTML generated on the server must match, node by node, what the client renders on its initial pass. Any divergence triggers a hydration mismatch error.

The window object does not exist in Node.js. Because localStorage is a property of window, any direct access during server-side rendering fails. Developers hit one of two failure modes. The first is a hard crash: ReferenceError: localStorage is not defined, which kills the server render entirely. In the App Router, 'use client' components still pre-render on the server during next build, so this error fires during server pre-rendering, not in the browser. The second failure mode is subtler and more common: a developer wraps the access in a typeof window !== 'undefined' guard, which avoids the crash but causes the server to render with a default value (say, 'light') while the client reads a stored value (say, 'dark') from localStorage. React detects the difference and throws a hydration mismatch warning. This exact scenario has been reported repeatedly on Stack Overflow, often confusing developers who believe the guard should be sufficient.

The root cause is straightforward but easy to overlook: the server and client disagree on initial state.

The goal here is to compare three patterns that resolve both failure modes, with honest analysis of their trade-offs.

Here is the broken component that triggers the error:

Running next build && next start with this component produces either the ReferenceError during server pre-rendering or, if a typeof window check is added to the initializer, a hydration mismatch in production.

During SSR, Next.js runs the React component tree in Node.js to produce an HTML string. Next.js sends that string to the browser, where React builds its own virtual DOM tree and diffs it against the existing server-rendered markup. If the two trees diverge in text content, attribute values, or tree structure, React logs a hydration error. In production, React 18 logs a console error and attempts DOM recovery, but visual glitches, lost state, or broken interactivity may still occur. Development mode adds additional checks and stack traces, making mismatches easier to diagnose.

window.localStorage simply does not exist in the Node.js runtime. Browsers expose it; Node.js does not. The most common anti-pattern is reading it inside a useState initializer:

Adding a typeof window !== 'undefined' guard prevents the crash, but it introduces an asymmetry. The server always falls back to 'light'. The client reads the user's stored preference, which might be 'dark'. React sees 'light' on the server and 'dark' on the client, and the mismatch fires. This is not a theoretical concern; it is the exact mechanism behind the majority of localStorage-related hydration errors in Next.js applications.

The simplest fix defers the localStorage read until after the component mounts. Server and client both render the same safe default. Once the component mounts in the browser, a useEffect reads the stored value and updates state, triggering a client-only re-render.

The server renders "Current theme: light". The client also renders "Current theme: light" during hydration, so the trees match. After mount, useEffect fires, reads 'dark' from localStorage if present, and updates the button text. Hydration succeeds.

This pattern requires zero dependencies and uses only useState and useEffect, both covered in the official React tutorial. However, it introduces a flash of the default state on first paint, typically lasting one to two frames (under 50 ms on modern hardware, but visible if it triggers a layout shift or color swap). If the user has stored 'dark', they see 'light' for that brief window before the effect runs and triggers a re-render. This FOUC is the primary downside. The pattern also causes two renders on mount: the initial hydration render and the state update from the effect.

This approach works for low-stakes preferences where a brief visual flash is acceptable, such as non-critical UI settings or feature flags that do not affect layout. It also suits prototypes and MVPs where simplicity matters more than polish.

Save this hook as hooks/useLocalStorage.js (or .ts) at your project root and import it using Next.js's built-in @/ alias: import useLocalStorage from '@/hooks/useLocalStorage'.

This pattern is hydration-safe by contract. The getServerSnapshot parameter makes the server value explicit, and React handles the transition without a mismatch error. Cross-tab synchronization comes for free via the storage event listener. When the client snapshot differs from the server snapshot, React schedules a client re-render after hydration. FOUC still occurs; useSyncExternalStore's advantage is an explicit hydration contract and built-in cross-tab sync, not flash elimination.

FOUC still occurs; useSyncExternalStore's advantage is an explicit hydration contract and built-in cross-tab sync, not flash elimination.

This is the right choice for any application that treats localStorage as a live, subscribable store. Multi-tab dashboards, collaborative tools, and apps with many components reading the same stored value all benefit from the built-in synchronization. It also works well as a reusable hook consumed across many components, because the API returns a [value, setter] tuple matching the useState contract and the hydration behavior is explicit.

Wrapping components in if (typeof window !== 'undefined') causes tree-shape mismatches. The server renders nothing; the client renders the full subtree. React cannot reconcile a missing node with a present one, producing hydration errors that are harder to diagnose than value mismatches.

The suppressHydrationWarning prop is appropriate for content that is inherently different between server and client, such as timestamps or CSRF tokens. You can also apply it to or when browser extensions modify attributes outside React's control. Using it to suppress localStorage-related mismatches masks real bugs and can lead to silent UI inconsistencies in production.

Always test hydration behavior by running next build && next start against the production build. In production, React 18 logs a console error and attempts DOM recovery, but visual glitches may occur. Development mode adds additional checks and stack traces, making it easier to spot issues but also more lenient in some cases.

#10745. AI swarms could hijack democracy without anyone noticing

A new kind of political threat may be emerging, and it is far less visible than protests or traditional voter manipulation. Researchers warn that highly realistic AI-controlled personas could soon play a major role in shaping public opinion and influencing democratic systems.

A recent policy forum paper published in Science describes how large groups of AI-generated personas can convincingly imitate human behavior online. These systems can enter digital communities, participate in discussions, and influence viewpoints at extraordinary speed. Unlike earlier bot networks, these AI agents can coordinate instantly, respond to feedback, and maintain consistent narratives across thousands of accounts.

Rapid progress in large language models and multi-agent systems has made it possible for a single operator to manage vast networks of AI "voices." These personas can appear authentic, adopt local language and tone, and interact in ways that feel natural to other users.

They are also capable of running millions of small-scale experiments to determine which messages are most persuasive. This allows them to refine their communication strategies in real time and generate what appears to be widespread public agreement. In reality, that consensus is artificially created and designed to influence political discussions.

Deepfakes and fake news signal early risks

Although fully developed AI swarms are still largely theoretical, researchers say there are already warning signs. These include AI-generated deepfakes and fake news outlets that have influenced recent election conversations in countries such as the United States, Taiwan, Indonesia, and India, according to UBC computer scientist Dr. Kevin Leyton-Brown.

At the same time, monitoring organizations have identified pro-Kremlin networks spreading large volumes of online content. This activity is believed to be aimed at shaping the data used to train future AI systems, potentially influencing how those systems behave and what information they prioritize.

Experts warn of growing impact on democracy

Looking ahead, experts believe AI swarms could significantly affect the balance of power in democratic societies. Dr. Leyton-Brown cautioned that these systems are likely to change how people trust information online. "We shouldn't imagine that society will remain unchanged as these systems emerge. A likely result is decreased trust of unknown voices on social media, which could empower celebrities and make it harder for grassroots messages to break through."

Researchers suggest that upcoming elections may serve as a critical test for this technology. The key challenge will be recognizing and responding to these AI-driven influence campaigns before they become too widespread to control.

#10741. KFC reveals Avengers: Doomsday menu with black burgers, green mayo and ‘Doom Dust’

#10739. This AI knew the answers but didn’t understand the questions

Psychologists have long debated whether the human mind can be explained by a single, unified theory or if different functions such as attention and memory must be studied separately. Now, artificial intelligence (AI) is entering that debate, offering a new way to explore how the mind works.

In July 2025, a study published in Nature introduced an AI model called "Centaur." Built on standard large language models and refined using data from psychological experiments, Centaur was designed to simulate human cognitive behavior. It reportedly performed well across 160 tasks, including decision-making, executive control, and other mental processes. The results drew widespread attention and were seen as a possible step toward AI systems that could replicate human thinking more broadly.

A more recent study published in National Science Open challenges those claims. Researchers from Zhejiang University argue that Centaur's apparent success may come from overfitting. In other words, instead of understanding the tasks, the model may have learned to recognize patterns in the training data and reproduce expected answers.

To test this idea, the researchers created several new evaluation scenarios. In one example, they replaced the original multiple-choice prompts, which described specific psychological tasks, with the instruction "Please choose option A." If the model truly understood the task, it should have consistently selected option A. Instead, Centaur continued to choose the "correct answers" from the original dataset.

This behavior suggests that the model was not interpreting the meaning of the questions. Rather, it relied on learned statistical patterns to "guess" answers. The researchers compared this to a student who scores well by memorizing test formats without actually understanding the material.

The findings highlight the need for caution when assessing the abilities of large language models. While these systems can be highly effective at fitting data, their "black-box" nature makes it difficult to know how they arrive at their outputs. This can lead to issues such as hallucinations or misinterpretations. Careful and varied testing is essential to determine whether a model truly has the skills it appears to demonstrate.

The Real Challenge: Language Understanding

Although Centaur was presented as a model capable of simulating cognition, its biggest limitation appears to be in language comprehension. Specifically, it struggles to recognize and respond to the intent behind questions. The study suggests that achieving true language understanding may be one of the most important challenges in developing AI systems that can model human cognition more fully.
