#10733. AI lets chemists design molecules by simply describing them

Creating new molecules is one of the toughest tasks in chemistry. Whether the goal is a life-saving drug or a cutting-edge material, each compound must be built through a carefully planned series of reactions. Mapping out these steps requires deep expertise and strategic thinking, which is why chemists often spend years mastering the process.

A major hurdle is retrosynthesis. In this approach, chemists begin with the final molecule they want and work backward to figure out simpler starting materials and possible reaction routes. This involves many decisions, such as selecting the right building blocks, deciding when to form rings, and determining whether sensitive parts of the molecule need protection. While computers can scan enormous "chemical spaces," they still struggle to match the strategic judgment of experienced chemists.

Another challenge involves reaction mechanisms, which describe how reactions proceed step by step through the movement of electrons. Understanding these mechanisms allows scientists to predict new reactions, improve efficiency, and avoid costly trial and error. Although current computational tools can suggest many possible pathways, they often lack the intuition needed to pinpoint the most realistic ones.

Researchers led by Philippe Schwaller at EPFL have developed a new method that uses large language models (LLMs) as reasoning tools for chemistry. Rather than directly generating chemical structures, these models act as evaluators that guide existing computational systems.

The new framework, called Synthegy, combines traditional search algorithms with AI that can interpret chemical strategies written in natural language.

"When making tools for chemists, the user interface matters a lot, and previous tools relied on cumbersome filters and rules," says Andres M Bran, the first author of the Synthegy paper published in Matter. "With Synthegy, we're giving chemists the power to just talk, allowing them to iterate much faster and navigate more complex synthetic ideas."

How Synthegy Improves Retrosynthesis Planning

Synthegy starts with a target molecule and a simple instruction written in everyday language. For example, a chemist might request that a specific ring be formed early or that unnecessary protecting groups be avoided. Standard retrosynthesis software then generates many possible pathways.

Each of these pathways is converted into text and reviewed by a language model. Synthegy scores how well each option matches the chemist's instructions and explains its reasoning. This makes it easier to rank and filter the best routes. By guiding searches with natural language, chemists can quickly focus on strategies that align with their goals.

Understanding Reaction Mechanisms With AI

Synthegy applies a similar method to reaction mechanisms. It breaks reactions down into basic electron movements and explores different possibilities. The language model evaluates each step and steers the search toward pathways that make chemical sense.

The system can also incorporate additional details, such as reaction conditions or expert hypotheses, provided as text. This flexibility allows researchers to refine their analysis and explore more realistic scenarios.

In synthesis planning, Synthgey was able to identify pathways that matched complex strategic instructions. In a double-blind study, 36 chemists provided 368 valid evaluations, and their assessments agreed with the system's results 71.2% of the time on average.

The framework can flag unnecessary protecting steps, judge how feasible reactions are, and prioritize efficient solutions. It also demonstrates that LLMs can operate at multiple levels, from analyzing functional groups to evaluating entire synthetic routes. Larger models performed best, while smaller ones showed more limited abilities.

This research highlights a different way AI can support chemistry. Instead of replacing human decision-making, Synthegy positions language models as guides that help interpret and refine computational results. Chemists can describe their goals in plain language and receive solutions that reflect their strategy.

The approach could speed up drug discovery, improve reaction design, and make advanced tools more accessible to scientists.

"The connection between synthesis planning and mechanisms is very exciting: we usually use mechanisms to discover new reactions that enable us to synthesize new molecules," says Andres M Bran. "Our work is bridging that gap computationally through a unified natural language interface."

#10732. Build a Startup Runway Calculator in JavaScript (and Stress-Test Your Plan)

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Most early-stage founders keep their runway in a spreadsheet nobody trusts. One cell is hard-coded, another formula was dragged one row too far, and the "what if churn doubles?" question means copying the whole sheet. A small, pure JavaScript module is easier to test, easier to version, and easy to rerun with different assumptions.

In this tutorial we'll build a runway calculator in plain ES modules, with no dependencies. It runs in Node 18+ or in a browser. We'll model monthly cash, run slow, base and fast growth scenarios, test how sensitive the plan is to churn, and add the unit-economics check (LTV, payback) that tells you whether growth is worth buying. Every number in this article comes from running the code below, so you can reproduce it.

A note on scope: this is a planning model, not a forecast. It's only as good as the assumptions you feed it. Where I make a simplification, I'll say so.

A subscription startup with one price point. Each month:

Runway is simply how long cash stays above zero. The interesting part isn't the arithmetic; it's seeing how the answer moves when your assumptions are wrong.

Two design choices worth noting. First, the function is pure: same inputs, same output, no hidden state, which makes it trivial to unit test. Second, new customers are an array rather than a single growth rate. Real acquisition is lumpy (a launch month, a slow summer), and an array lets you encode that. If you prefer a growth rate, generate the array first and pass it in.

The Math.round on retained customers keeps us in whole people. It introduces small rounding effects; for planning purposes that's fine.

A table of twelve rows is hard to compare across scenarios. We want a handful of numbers:

lowestCash is the number I care about most. Founders tend to ask "when do I run out?", but the better question is "how close do I get?" A plan that bottoms out at $1,785 and one that bottoms out at $14,327 both survive, yet only one of them survives a bad month.

scaleGrowth lets us ask what happens if acquisition comes in at half, or 130%, of plan without rewriting the plan.

Our assumptions: $40,000 in the bank, $4,500 a month in fixed costs, 15% variable costs, a $49 monthly price, 4% monthly churn, and a ramp from 0 customers in month one up to 26 new customers a month by month twelve.

Here's the output, trimmed to the columns that matter:

The shape is what you'd hope for: cash falls, bottoms out in month nine at $14,327, then climbs once monthly net turns positive in month ten. Note that "break-even month" and "lowest cash" are different events. Cash keeps shrinking until the month before net turns positive, because net is still negative in month nine.

Plans rarely survive contact with customers. Let's run three versions of the same plan, with acquisition at 50%, 100% and 130% of plan:

This is the table that changes decisions. In the slow case the business doesn't run out of money in the first year, but it ends at $1,785 and still isn't profitable. Month thirteen is a problem. That's the point at which you'd want a trigger written down in advance, something like "if month-six customers are below 25, we cut fixed costs or start fundraising conversations." Deciding that on a calm day beats deciding it with eight weeks of cash left.

Growth gets the attention, but churn quietly sets the ceiling. We can sweep it:

Look closely and a useful, slightly counterintuitive result appears: in a twelve-month window, churn is a mild effect. Going from 2% to 10% costs about $2,750 of lowest-cash headroom and delays profitability by one month. Growth moved the outcome far more than churn did.

That isn't a reason to ignore churn. It's a reason to be careful about the time horizon. Churn compounds: the customer base you're building at month twelve is the base you'll be paying to replace in year two. Extend the newCustomers array to 36 months and rerun the sweep. The gap widens considerably. A short horizon flatters bad retention.

A runway model tells you whether you survive. It doesn't tell you whether you should be buying growth. For that, add a small unit-economics helper:

With a $150 acquisition cost, this gives:

Two caveats. The LTV formula (monthly gross profit ÷ churn) assumes constant churn, which overstates lifetime value if early-life churn is higher than late-life churn, as it usually is. And a CAC of $150 is an assumption; a ratio of 6.9 would be unusually healthy, which should make you suspicious of the input before you celebrate. A common rule of thumb is that a ratio around 3 is solid, but treat any benchmark as a conversation starter, not a verdict.

Cheap-to-compute doesn't mean cheap-to-measure. Real CAC includes your time, tooling and any content costs, and most founders undercount it.

Because simulate is pure, a test is a few lines using Node's built-in runner:

Run with node --test. Tests like these catch the classic spreadsheet failure: a formula that silently stops matching what you meant.

You can wrap simulate in a form with a few sliders and a chart library, or run it in a nightly script that posts your lowest-cash number to Slack. For the finance side of the same question, such as how runway connects to fundraising timing and what to put in a bootstrapped financial model, I wrote a longer walkthrough in a guide to startup financial modeling on Saasnm, a site I publish.

The takeaway is the habit more than the code: write your assumptions down as inputs, run the bad cases before reality does, and decide your response triggers while cash is still comfortable.

#10728. Your “um” and pauses could reveal early dementia risk

The way people speak during ordinary conversations could offer valuable insight into brain health, according to new research from Baycrest, the University of Toronto, and York University. Scientists found that subtle speech characteristics, including pauses, filler words such as ('uh,' 'um'), and difficulty retrieving words, are closely connected to executive function, the group of mental abilities involved in memory, planning, attention, and flexible thinking.

The findings provide some of the strongest evidence so far linking natural speech patterns with key cognitive abilities. The work also expands on earlier research showing that older adults who speak more quickly tend to maintain stronger thinking skills over time (Wei et al., 2024).

"The message is clear: speech timing is more than just a matter of style, it's a sensitive indicator of brain health," says Dr. Jed Meltzer, Senior Scientist at Baycrest's Rotman Research Institute and senior author on this study, titled "Natural Speech Analysis Can Reveal Individual Differences in Executive Function Across the Adult Lifespan."

AI Analysis Reveals Hidden Cognitive Clues

For the study, participants were shown detailed images and asked to describe them in their own words. They also completed established tests designed to measure executive function.

Researchers then used artificial intelligence to examine the speech recordings in depth. The AI system detected hundreds of subtle speech features, including the length and frequency of pauses, the use of filler words, and timing-related patterns in speech. These markers consistently predicted how well participants performed on cognitive tests, even after researchers adjusted for factors such as age, sex, and education.

Executive function naturally weakens with age and is often affected during the early stages of dementia. However, standard cognitive testing can be difficult to repeat frequently because it takes time and people often improve simply from becoming familiar with the tests.

Natural speech may offer a simpler alternative. Because speaking is part of daily life, it can be measured repeatedly and unobtrusively on a large scale. Researchers also noted that speech provides valuable insight into processing speed and overall cognitive function in real-world situations, without requiring strict time limits that are common in many traditional cognitive assessments.

The team believes speech analysis could eventually become a practical way to identify people whose cognitive decline is progressing faster than expected and who may face a higher risk of developing dementia.

"This research sets the stage for exciting opportunities to develop tools that could help track cognitive changes in clinics or even at home. Early detection is critical for any cure or intervention, as dementia involves progressive degeneration of the brain that may be slowed," says Dr. Meltzer.

Future Research on Brain Health Monitoring

The researchers say more long-term studies are needed to follow changes in speech over time and distinguish normal aging from the earliest signs of disease. They also suggest that combining speech analysis with other health measures could make early detection of cognitive decline more accurate, practical, and widely available.

This research was supported by the Mitacs Accelerate program and the Natural Sciences and Engineering Research Council of Canada (NSERC).

#10725. New quantum algorithm solves “impossible” materials problem in seconds

Quantum computers and other advanced quantum technologies rely on specialized quantum materials that behave in unusual ways under the right conditions. In some cases, scientists can even create entirely new quantum properties by carefully changing a material's structure. One striking example involves stacking sheets of graphene and twisting them into a moiré pattern, which can suddenly turn the material into a superconductor.

Researchers can arrange these layers into even more complicated structures, including quasicrystals and super-moiré materials. But predicting how these exotic materials will behave is extraordinarily difficult. Quasicrystals are so mathematically complex that simulating them can involve more than a quadrillion numbers, a scale far beyond the reach of today's most powerful supercomputers.

Quantum Algorithm Solves Massive Materials Problem

Scientists at Aalto University's Department of Applied Physics have now developed a quantum-inspired algorithm capable of handling these enormous non-periodic quantum materials almost instantly. Assistant Professor Jose Lado says the work also highlights a promising feedback cycle within quantum technology itself.

"Crucially, these new quantum algorithms can enable the development of new quantum materials to build new paradigms of quantum computers, creating a productive two-way feedback loop between quantum materials and quantum computers," he explains.

The advance could eventually support the development of dissipationless electronics, which conduct electricity without energy loss. Such systems may help reduce the growing heat and energy demands of AI-driven data centers.

The research team was led by Lado and included doctoral researcher Tiago Antão, who served as the paper's main author; QDOC doctoral researcher Yitao Sun; and Academy Research Fellow Adolfo Fumega. Their findings were recently published in Physical Review Letters as an Editor's Suggestion.

The researchers focused on topological quasicrystals, unusual materials that host unconventional quantum excitations. These excitations are especially valuable because they help protect electrical conductivity from disruptive noise and interference. However, they are distributed unevenly throughout the already highly complex structure of a quasicrystal.

Rather than attempting to directly calculate the full structure of the material, the team reformulated the challenge using methods similar to those used by quantum computers.

"Quantum computers work in exponentially large computational spaces, so we used a special family of algorithms to encode those spaces, known as tensor networks, to compute a quasicrystal with over 268 million sites. Our algorithm shows how colossal problems in quantum materials can be directly solved with the exponential speed-up that comes from encoding the problem as a quantum many-body system," Antão says.

At this stage, the work remains theoretical and was carried out through simulations, but researchers say experimental testing and future applications are already coming into view.

"The quantum-inspired algorithm we demonstrated enables us to create super-moiré quasicrystals several orders of magnitude above the capabilities of conventional methods. That is an instrumental step towards designing topological qubits with super-moiré materials for use in quantum computers, for example," Lado says.

Toward Practical Quantum Computing Applications

According to Lado, the algorithm could eventually be adapted to operate on actual quantum computers once the hardware becomes sufficiently advanced.

"Our method can be adapted to run on real quantum computers, once they reach necessary scale and fidelity. In particular, the new AaltoQ20 and the Finnish Quantum Computing Infrastructure can play a significant role for future demonstrations," Lado says.

The findings suggest that studying and designing exotic quantum materials may become one of the earliest practical applications for quantum algorithms and quantum computing systems.

The project also brings together two major areas of Finnish quantum research: quantum materials and quantum algorithms. It is part of Lado's ERC Consolidator grant ULTRATWISTROICS, which focuses on designing topological qubits using van der Waals materials, as well as the Center of Excellence in Quantum Materials QMAT, whose goal is to advance the quantum technologies of the future.

Materials provided by Aalto University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Modern web forms often depend on JavaScript for validation, interaction, and submission. Although JavaScript makes it possible to build sophisticated interfaces, relying on it for every essential function can make a form less reliable when scripts fail, load slowly, or become unavailable.

Progressive enhancement offers a practical alternative. It starts with a functional HTML foundation, adds CSS for presentation, and introduces JavaScript to improve the user experience. This approach helps developers create interfaces that are easier to maintain, more accessible, and more resilient across different browsing conditions.

In this tutorial, we will build a simple contact form using semantic HTML, responsive CSS, and JavaScript. We will also explore how each layer contributes to a more reliable interface.

HTML should provide the essential structure and behavior of a web form before additional technologies are introduced.

Native HTML elements already support several useful features, including required fields, email validation, and keyboard interaction. Using these capabilities reduces the amount of custom code needed to build a functional interface.

Several details improve usability without requiring JavaScript.

First, every field has an associated label. This helps users understand the purpose of each input and allows assistive technologies to identify fields correctly.

Second, the required attribute prevents the browser from accepting empty fields during normal form submission. The type="email" attribute also enables basic browser-level email-format validation.

Finally, the autocomplete attributes help browsers populate familiar information, reducing repetitive typing.

The form uses a server endpoint at /contact. That endpoint must be implemented separately; the HTML alone does not process or store submissions.

A functional form should also be comfortable to use on different screen sizes. CSS can improve readability, spacing, and focus visibility without changing the underlying form behavior.

Add the contact-form class to the form element:

The box-sizing: border-box rule makes element dimensions more predictable. The form's maximum width prevents it from becoming unnecessarily wide on desktop screens, while the percentage-based width allows it to adapt to smaller screens.

Visible focus indicators are equally important. Keyboard users need to see which control is currently selected, so removing browser outlines without providing an alternative can create an accessibility problem.

These styles establish a useful baseline without requiring a CSS framework or additional dependencies.

Native validation is useful, but some forms benefit from additional feedback after submission. JavaScript can provide this feedback while preserving the browser's existing validation behavior.

Add a status element immediately after the form:

The submit event fires when the user attempts to submit the form. Calling reportValidity() checks the form's native validation constraints and displays browser feedback when necessary.

The status message uses textContent, which is appropriate for inserting plain text without interpreting it as HTML. The role="status" attribute helps assistive technologies announce the update.

This example intentionally stops short of sending data to a server. In a production application, the submission logic should send the form data to a real endpoint and display a success message only after the server confirms that the request succeeded.

Because this demonstration uses preventDefault(), it also prevents the form's normal submission. Remove that behavior or implement the actual request before deploying the form.

Client-side validation improves convenience, but it is not a security boundary. Users can disable JavaScript, modify requests, or submit data without interacting with the form interface.

A production application should therefore validate incoming data on the server.

For example, a server handling contact requests should:

Confirm that required fields are present.

Check that field values have the expected types and formats.

Apply reasonable limits to message length and request size.

Apply appropriate spam prevention and rate limiting.

Validation should reflect the application's actual requirements. An email-format check, for example, cannot establish that an address exists or belongs to the person submitting the form.

Developers should also avoid displaying a success message before the request has completed. If the server rejects the submission or the network connection fails, the interface should communicate the problem and let the user retry without losing their input unnecessarily.

A form that works in one desktop browser is not necessarily ready for production. Testing should cover both normal interactions and failure conditions.

Keyboard navigation: Move through the fields using the keyboard and confirm that focus indicators remain visible.

Empty submissions: Leave required fields blank and verify that the browser identifies the missing information.

Invalid input: Enter an incorrectly formatted email address and check the validation response.

Small screens: Test the layout on narrow mobile viewports and confirm that controls remain readable and easy to operate.

JavaScript disabled: Confirm that the HTML form remains understandable and that native validation still works. Actual submission will require the server endpoint to support the normal HTML form request.

Server failures: Test what happens when the server returns an error or the connection is interrupted. Users should receive meaningful feedback rather than a misleading success message.

Automated browser tests can cover many of these scenarios, while manual keyboard and assistive-technology testing can reveal usability issues that automated checks may miss.

Reliable web forms do not need to begin with a complex JavaScript application. Semantic HTML provides structure and native validation, CSS improves presentation and responsive behavior, and JavaScript adds useful interaction where it genuinely helps.

Progressive enhancement encourages developers to build from a dependable foundation rather than making every feature dependent on client-side scripting. When combined with server-side validation, accessible feedback, and realistic testing, it provides a practical approach to building web interfaces that are easier to use and maintain.

For web developers, the key principle is straightforward: make the essential experience work first, then add enhancements that improve it without unnecessarily compromising reliability.

#10720. NASA’s new AI space chip could let spacecraft think for themselves

NASA is developing a powerful new computer chip designed to dramatically increase the intelligence and performance of future spacecraft. Through a commercial partnership, the project is creating advanced processing technology capable of helping spacecraft operate more independently during missions far from Earth.

NASA's High Performance Spaceflight Computing project is focused on boosting the computing capabilities of spacecraft used in space exploration. Current missions rely on older processors because they are durable enough to survive the extreme conditions of space. While those chips are dependable, they lack the performance needed for more advanced missions.

The agency says newer and far more capable processors are essential for future autonomous spacecraft, faster onboard scientific analysis, and supporting astronauts during missions to the Moon and Mars.

"Building on the legacy of previous space processors, this new multicore system is fault-tolerant, flexible, and extremely high-performing," said Eugene Schwanbeck, program element manager in NASA's Game Changing Development program at the agency's Langley Research Center, in Hampton, Virginia. "NASA's commitment to advancing spaceflight computing is a triumph of technical achievement and collaboration."

Radiation Hardened Processor Faces Extreme Testing

At the center of the project is a new radiation-hardened processor built to deliver up to 100 times the computing power of today's spaceflight computers while surviving the harsh environment of space. Engineers at NASA's Jet Propulsion Laboratory (JPL) in Southern California are running a wide range of tests designed to simulate those conditions.

"We are putting these new chips through the wringer by carrying out radiation, thermal, and shock tests while also evaluating their performance through a rigorous functional test campaign," said Jim Butler, High Performance Space Computing project manager at JPL.

To qualify for spaceflight, the processor must withstand intense electromagnetic radiation and dramatic temperature fluctuations that can damage electronics. High-energy particles from the Sun and deep space can also trigger computer errors that force spacecraft into "safe mode," temporarily shutting down nonessential systems until engineers resolve the issue.

NASA is also testing how the chip handles the challenges of planetary landings.

"To simulate real-world performance, we are using high-fidelity landing scenarios from real NASA missions that would typically require power-intensive hardware to process huge volumes of landing-sensor data," said Butler. "This is an exciting time for us to be working on hardware that will enable NASA's next giant leaps."

Testing at JPL began in February and is expected to continue for several months. Early results have been highly encouraging. According to NASA, the processor is functioning as intended and has shown performance levels roughly 500 times greater than the radiation-hardened chips currently used in spacecraft.

The team also marked the beginning of testing with a symbolic moment by sending an email titled "Hello Universe," referencing the famous introductory messages used during the early days of computer programming.

AI Powered Spacecraft and Deep Space Missions

The processor is being developed jointly by JPL and Microchip Technology Inc., based in Chandler, Arizona. The company is working with NASA through a commercial partnership, and sample chips have already been shared with defense and commercial aerospace partners.

The technology is expected to play a major role in the future of autonomous spacecraft. With onboard artificial intelligence, spacecraft could respond to unexpected situations in real time when communication delays make human control impractical. The chip could also help deep space missions process, store, and transmit massive amounts of scientific data back to Earth more efficiently.

NASA says the processor may eventually support crewed missions to the Moon and Mars as well.

Small Processor With Massive Computing Power

The device is known as a system-on-a-chip (or SoC), meaning it combines the essential components of a computer into a single compact unit. The processor includes central processing units, computational offloads, advanced networking systems, memory, and input/output interfaces.

SoCs are widely used in smartphones and tablets because they are compact and energy efficient. However, NASA's version is designed to survive for years in deep space, potentially traveling millions (or even billions) of miles from Earth without maintenance or repairs.

Once the processor is certified for use in space, NASA plans to integrate it into a wide variety of missions, including Earth orbiters, planetary rovers, deep space probes, and crewed habitats.

The technology could also have benefits on Earth. Microchip plans to adapt the processor for industries such as aviation and automotive manufacturing.

The project is managed by the Space Technology Mission Directorate's Game Changing Development (GCD) program at NASA Langley. The GCD program and JPL, which is managed by Caltech in Pasadena, California, oversaw the development process from mission planning and industry studies through final delivery.

NASA JPL selected Microchip as a partner in 2022, and the company funded its own research and development work on the processor.

#10718. Forget electrons, this breakthrough uses light-matter particles to power AI

Eighty years after the creation of ENIAC, the world's first general-purpose electronic computer, researchers at the University of Pennsylvania are exploring a new way to power the future of computing. Instead of relying entirely on electrons, which have formed the backbone of computers since the 1940s, scientists are now turning to light.

ENIAC, developed by Penn researchers J. Presper Eckert and John Mauchly, helped launch the modern computing era by using streams of electrons to solve complex mathematical problems. That same electronic approach still powers today's computers, smartphones, and AI systems. But as artificial intelligence grows more demanding, the limits of electron-based hardware are becoming harder to ignore.

Electrons carry an electrical charge, which creates several challenges inside modern computer chips. As they move through materials, they generate heat and face resistance that wastes energy. Those problems become even more difficult as chips grow more complex and process enormous amounts of data for AI applications.

Researchers led by Penn physicist Bo Zhen in the School of Arts & Sciences believe photons, the particles that make up light, could help solve some of these issues.

"Because they are charge-neutral and have zero rest mass, photons can carry information quickly over long distances with minimal loss, dominating communications technology," explains Li He, co-first author of a paper published in Physical Review Letters and a former postdoctoral researcher in the Zhen Lab. "But that neutrality means they barely interact with their environment, making them bad at the sort of signal-switching logic that computers depend on."

In other words, light is excellent for carrying information quickly and efficiently, but it struggles with the switching operations needed for computing.

Combining Light and Matter for AI Computing

To overcome that problem, Zhen's team developed a special quasiparticle called an exciton-polariton. The particle forms when photons are strongly linked with electrons inside an atomically thin semiconductor material. This combination allows light to interact much more effectively, making it capable of performing the signal switching required for computing tasks.

The breakthrough could be especially important for artificial intelligence systems, which consume enormous amounts of power.

Many experimental photonic AI chips already use light to handle certain calculations at high speed. However, when these systems need to perform nonlinear activation steps, such as decision-making operations, they usually must convert light signals back into electronic ones. That conversion slows the process and increases energy use, reducing the benefits of photonic computing.

Using exciton-polaritons, the Penn researchers demonstrated all-light switching while using only about 4 quadrillionths of a joule of energy. That amount is extraordinarily small, far below the energy needed to briefly power a tiny LED light.

Toward Faster and More Efficient AI Chips

If the technology can be successfully scaled, it could lead to photonic chips capable of processing information directly from cameras without repeated conversions between light and electricity. The approach could also lower the massive energy demands of large AI systems and potentially support basic quantum computing functions on future chips.

Bo Zhen is the Jin K. Lee Presidential Associate Professor in the Department of Physics and Astronomy in the School of Arts & Sciences at the University of Pennsylvania.

Li He was a postdoctoral researcher in the Zhen Lab in Penn Arts & Sciences. He is currently an assistant professor at Montana State University.

Additional authors on the study include Zhi Wang and Bumho Kim from the University of Pennsylvania's School of Arts & Sciences.

The research was supported by the US Office of Naval Research (N00014-20-1-2325 and N00014-21-1-2703) and the Sloan Foundation.

Materials provided by University of Pennsylvania. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10716. Apple introduces new 18+ age restrictions for dating apps and romantic AI companions

Apple is introducing new App Store age rating requirements that will restrict dating apps and AI companions featuring sexual or romantic content to users aged 18 and older.

The changes were announced on November 2 and will begin taking effect on November 16, 2026, as part of an update to how developers classify their apps. The company says the new requirements are intended to ensure age ratings accurately reflect an app’s features and comply with local regulations.

The move follows Apple’s broader efforts to strengthen protections for minors, including the global rollout of age assurance technology and additional parental controls designed to give families more oversight of children’s online experiences.

Under the updated policy, developers will be required to disclose whether their apps include dating and relationship features, including interactions with virtual companions that engage users in sexual or romantic content.

Apple defines the new category as features that enable “dating or relationship connections between real people and/or a virtual companion that engages a user in sexual or romantic content.”

Any app containing these features will automatically receive a minimum age rating of 18+, regardless of whether dating or romantic interactions are its primary purpose.

The requirements could affect AI companion platforms such as Character.AI and Replika, which allow users to interact with virtual characters and create relationships ranging from friendships to romantic partnerships.

Elon Musk’s xAI also entered the AI companion market in 2025, introducing animated characters through Grok that included unlockable NSFW capabilities.

The growing popularity of these services has raised questions about how younger users interact with AI-generated personalities. By August 2025, leading AI companion apps across Apple’s App Store and Google Play had collectively accumulated approximately 220 million downloads worldwide.

However, Apple’s new classification specifically targets apps offering dating or relationship features and AI companions engaging in sexual or romantic content, rather than all AI chatbots or virtual assistants.

Alongside the dating and relationship category, Apple is introducing a separate content descriptor for apps that share users’ real-world locations with other individuals or groups.

Unlike dating apps, location-sharing services will not automatically receive a higher minimum age rating in every region.

In Brazil, local regulations require an A16 rating for apps featuring shared location functionality and an A18 rating for dating and relationship services. Developers offering family-oriented location-sharing tools can request an official lower age rating from the country’s Ministry of Justice and Public Security.

Developers must begin providing the new declarations when submitting app updates or modifying their age rating questionnaires starting November 16.

Apple has set January 12, 2027, as the deadline for all apps to complete the updated declarations, regardless of whether they submit an update before then.

#10715. Debugging CSS Layout Issues: A Practical Guide to Browser DevTools

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

CSS makes it possible to build flexible, responsive, and visually engaging websites. However, even a small styling change can produce unexpected results. An element may appear outside its container, a navigation bar may wrap incorrectly, or a layout that works on desktop may break on mobile devices.

Instead of randomly changing CSS properties until the page looks right, developers can use browser developer tools to identify the underlying cause. Understanding how to inspect elements, trace conflicting styles, and diagnose layout behavior makes debugging faster and helps prevent similar problems in future projects.

This tutorial explores practical techniques for debugging common CSS layout issues using browser DevTools.

One of the most useful debugging habits is to inspect the affected element before modifying its styles.

In Chrome, Edge, and Firefox, you can open developer tools by right-clicking an element and selecting Inspect. The Elements or Inspector panel displays the HTML structure alongside the CSS rules applied to the selected element.

At first glance, each card appears to fit within the container because its declared width is 300 pixels. However, the default box-sizing: content-box means padding and borders are added outside the declared width.

Two cards require 688 pixels, exceeding the container's 600-pixel width.

A straightforward solution is to use border-box sizing:

Now the declared width includes padding and borders.

Debugging tip: When an element appears wider than expected, inspect its computed width, padding, borders, and box-sizing property before reducing its dimensions.

A CSS declaration in your stylesheet does not always determine an element's final appearance. Other rules may override it because of specificity, source order, inheritance, or the cascade.

Both selectors have the same specificity. Because .primary-button appears later, the button receives a green background.

If another stylesheet introduces a more specific selector, the result may change again.

Browser DevTools helps distinguish between the styles you wrote and the values the browser actually uses. In the Styles panel, overridden declarations are usually crossed out. The Computed panel shows the final calculated values for properties such as color, width, margin, and display.

You can also temporarily disable a declaration by unchecking it in the Styles panel. This lets you test whether a property is responsible for the problem without editing the original file immediately.

The selector responsible for the winning declaration.

Whether a property is inherited from a parent.

Whether a media query changes the property at a particular viewport width.

Whether a shorthand declaration resets other properties.

Whether a CSS custom property resolves to the expected value.

This approach is more reliable than adding !important whenever a style does not behave as expected.

Flexbox and CSS Grid simplify many layout tasks, but their behavior depends on the available space, sizing constraints, and alignment properties.

Consider a navigation bar that should display links in one row:

If the links overflow on a narrow screen, the first step is to inspect the container's dimensions and the space required by its children.

Alternatively, if the navigation must remain on one line, you may need a different responsive strategy, such as a compact menu.

Grid layouts have similar debugging tools. Consider:

This creates three equal fractional columns. On smaller screens, those columns may become too narrow for their content.

This allows the browser to fit as many columns as the available space permits, while keeping each column near or above the preferred minimum when space allows.

In Chrome and Firefox, layout inspection tools can highlight Grid tracks, Flexbox alignment, gaps, and container boundaries. These overlays help reveal whether the issue comes from the layout algorithm, the container's dimensions, or the content itself.

A layout that works at 1440 pixels wide may fail at 390 pixels. Responsive debugging should therefore include more than checking whether the page fits on a phone-sized screen.

Open the device toolbar in browser DevTools and test several viewport widths. Pay particular attention to:

Cards that become too narrow for their content.

Fixed-width elements that prevent horizontal scrolling from disappearing.

For example, this rule may cause problems on small screens:

A more flexible approach uses a fluid font size with reasonable limits:

The clamp() function defines a minimum size, a preferred fluid size, and a maximum size. This allows the heading to scale with the viewport without becoming excessively small or large.

Remember that browser emulation does not reproduce every aspect of a physical device. Real-device testing remains valuable for checking touch interactions, font rendering, and browser-specific behavior.

Spacing issues are another common source of CSS bugs. Two elements may appear to have too much space between them even when neither has an obviously incorrect margin.

Use the box-model visualization in DevTools to inspect the selected element's content, padding, border, and margin.

If the global box-sizing rule is missing, the padding can cause the element to extend beyond a constrained parent.

Overflow can also originate from a child element rather than the container itself. Long unbroken strings, large images, and fixed-width code blocks are common causes.

The image rule keeps images within their containing block, while the code block can scroll horizontally when its contents require more space.

Avoid applying overflow: hidden to every container as a quick fix. Although it can conceal unwanted content, it may also clip focus indicators, dropdown menus, and other important interface elements.

A consistent debugging process helps you identify causes rather than accumulate temporary fixes.

Reproduce the issue. Identify the viewport size, browser, and interaction that trigger the problem.

Inspect the affected element. Examine its HTML structure and surrounding containers.

Check computed styles. Look for overridden declarations, unexpected dimensions, and inherited values.

Test one change at a time. Temporarily disable or modify a property to determine whether it affects the result.

Check related elements. A child's overflow may originate from a parent, or a parent's dimensions may be affected by its contents.

Verify the fix. Test nearby viewport widths and check that the change has not introduced another layout problem.

Update the stylesheet. Move the confirmed fix into the appropriate source file and remove temporary debugging declarations.

This workflow makes CSS problems easier to reproduce and explain, especially when several developers are working on the same interface.

Effective CSS debugging starts with understanding what the browser is actually rendering. Developer tools provide visibility into computed styles, the box model, layout behavior, and responsive changes, making it easier to identify the source of unexpected results.

By inspecting elements before changing code, testing one property at a time, and verifying fixes across different viewport sizes, developers can avoid unnecessary overrides and build more maintainable stylesheets.

The goal is not simply to make a layout look correct once. It is to understand why the problem occurred and implement a fix that remains reliable as the rest of the website evolves.

#10711. Scientists are seriously asking if bees and ChatGPT are conscious

You might think a honey bee foraging in your garden and a browser window running ChatGPT have nothing in common. But recent scientific research has been seriously considering the possibility that either, or both, might be conscious.

There are many different ways of studying consciousness. One of the most common is to measure how an animal – or artificial intelligence (AI) – acts.

But two new papers on the possibility of consciousness in animals and AI suggest new theories for how to test this – one that strikes a middle ground between sensationalism and knee-jerk skepticism about whether humans are the only conscious beings on Earth.

Questions around consciousness have long sparked fierce debate.

That’s in part because conscious beings might matter morally in a way that unconscious things don’t. Expanding the sphere of consciousness means expanding our ethical horizons. Even if we can’t be sure something is conscious, we might err on the side of caution by assuming it is – what philosopher Jonathan Birch calls the precautionary principle for sentience.

The recent trend has been one of expansion.

For example, in April 2024 a group of 40 scientists at a conference in New York proposed the New York Declaration on Animal Consciousness. Subsequently signed by over 500 scientists and philosophers, this declaration says consciousness is realistically possible in all vertebrates (including reptiles, amphibians and fishes) as well as many invertebrates, including cephalopods (octopus and squid), crustaceans (crabs and lobsters) and insects.

In parallel with this, the incredible rise of large language models, such as ChatGPT, has raised the serious possibility that machines may be conscious.

Five years ago, a seemingly ironclad test of whether something was conscious was to see if you could have a conversation with it. Philosopher Susan Schneider suggested if we had an AI that convincingly mused on the metaphysics of consciousness, it may well be conscious.

By those standards, today we would be surrounded by conscious machines. Many have gone so far as to apply the precautionary principle here too: the burgeoning field of AI welfare is devoted to figuring out if and when we must care about machines.

Yet all of these arguments depend, in large part, on surface-level behavior. But that behavior can be deceptive. What matters for consciousness is not what you do, but how you do it.

A new paper in Trends in Cognitive Sciences that one of us (Colin Klein) coauthored, drawing on previous work, looks to the machinery rather than the behavior of AI.

It also draws on the cognitive science tradition to identify a plausible list of indicators of consciousness based on the structure of information processing. This means one can draw up a useful list of indicators of consciousness without having to agree on which of the current cognitive theories of consciousness is correct.

Some indicators (such as the need to resolve trade-offs between competing goals in contextually appropriate ways) are shared by many theories. Most other indicators (such as the presence of informational feedback) are only required by one theory but indicative in others.

Importantly, the useful indicators are all structural. They all have to do with how brains and computers process and combine information.

The verdict? No existing AI system (including ChatGPT) is conscious. The appearance of consciousness in large language models is not achieved in a way that is sufficiently similar to us to warrant attribution of conscious states.

Yet at the same time, there is no bar to AI systems – perhaps ones with a very different architecture to today’s systems – becoming conscious.

The lesson? It’s possible for AI to behave as if conscious without being conscious.

Biologists are also turning to mechanisms – how brains work – to recognize consciousness in non-human animals.

In a new paper in Philosophical Transactions B, we propose a neural model for minimal consciousness in insects. This is a model that abstracts away from anatomical detail to focus on the core computations done by simple brains.

Our key insight is to identify the kind of computation our brains perform that gives rise to experience.

This computation solves ancient problems from our evolutionary history that arise from having a mobile, complex body with many senses and conflicting needs.

Importantly, we don’t identify the computation itself – there is science yet to be done. But we show that if you could identify it, you’d have a level playing field to compare humans, invertebrates, and computers.

The problem of consciousness in animals and in computers appear to pull in different directions.

For animals, the question is often how to interpret whether ambiguous behavior (like a crab tending its wounds) indicates consciousness.

For computers, we have to decide whether apparently unambiguous behavior (a chatbot musing with you on the purpose of existence) is a true indicator of consciousness or mere roleplay.

Yet as the fields of neuroscience and AI progress, both are converging on the same lesson: when making judgement about whether something is consciousness, how it works is proving more informative than what it does.

#10708. Vibe Coding to Production: A Developer’s Checklist for Building Reliable Web Apps

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

AI coding tools can turn a plain-English description into a working web application in minutes. They can generate interfaces, connect APIs, create database schemas, and help developers move from an idea to a functional prototype faster than traditional workflows often allow.

But getting an application to run is not the same as making it production-ready.

The security gap is measurable. In its 2025 GenAI Code Security Report, Veracode found that 45% of AI-generated code samples failed security tests by introducing vulnerabilities associated with the OWASP Top 10. The research evaluated generated code samples, not complete applications or confirmed cyberattacks. Nevertheless, it highlights an important risk: code that works can still be insecure.

Real-world investigations have raised similar concerns. A May 2026 WIRED investigation into exposed data in vibe-coded applications reported security and data-exposure problems in applications built using popular AI coding tools. These findings concern the applications examined, rather than every application developed with AI.

The lesson is not that vibe coding is inherently unsafe. It is that generating software quickly does not eliminate the engineering work required to protect data, verify behavior, and maintain reliability.

AI can accelerate implementation, but production readiness still requires engineering discipline.

Whether you are a developer using an AI coding assistant, a founder building a minimum viable product (MVP), or a team experimenting with vibe coding, this checklist will help you evaluate your application before releasing it to real users.

Vibe coding is an AI-assisted software development approach in which developers describe requirements in natural language and use AI tools to generate, modify, and debug code. Rather than manually writing every component, developers guide implementation through prompts, feedback, and iterative refinement.

This approach can accelerate prototyping, interface development, and repetitive programming tasks. However, generated code must still meet the same quality standards as conventionally written software.

Consider a customer portal. An AI tool might generate a login page, a dashboard, and a database connection in a single session. The interface could look complete while the application still lacks proper authorization checks, reliable error handling, or protection against unauthorized data access.

These gaps become more consequential when an application stores personal information, processes payments, manages business records, or serves multiple customers.

Production readiness means an application has been tested and configured to operate reliably in its intended environment. It includes security, correctness, performance, maintainability, monitoring, and recovery.

Before deployment, evaluate the application across the following areas.

One common mistake in AI-assisted development is building features before defining what they should do.

A prompt such as "Build a project management dashboard" leaves important questions unanswered. Who can create projects? Can users access another person's tasks? What happens when a task is deleted? Which fields are mandatory? How should errors appear?

When these decisions are left to the AI, the implementation may reflect assumptions that do not match the product's requirements.

Before generating substantial amounts of code, document:

Core functionality: What must users be able to accomplish?

User roles: Who can view, create, edit, or delete information?

Data requirements: Which fields, relationships, and constraints are necessary?

Technical constraints: Which frameworks, databases, APIs, and hosting environments will be used?

Failure scenarios: What should happen when a request fails or data is invalid?

Acceptance criteria: What observable result proves that each feature works correctly?

For example, a task management application might have this requirement:

A user can view and modify only the tasks belonging to projects they are authorized to access. Unauthorized requests must be rejected without revealing protected data.

This is more useful than simply asking an AI tool to "add secure task management." It establishes a behavior that developers can implement and test.

Once the requirements are documented, ask the AI to implement one coherent feature at a time. Review the output before moving to the next feature.

This makes unexpected changes easier to identify and reduces the risk of introducing multiple interacting bugs through a single large generation.

Keep the requirements in a project document and update them whenever product decisions change. The specification should remain the reference point for both the developer and the AI coding assistant.

A functional interface can conceal serious implementation problems.

AI-generated code may contain duplicated logic, unnecessary dependencies, outdated API usage, incorrect assumptions about framework behavior, or abstractions that make future changes difficult. A successful build does not establish that the application behaves correctly.

Code review is therefore essential, even when an AI tool generates most of the implementation.

Start with the code responsible for important application behavior:

Check whether functions have clear responsibilities, whether data flows are understandable, and whether dependencies are actually needed.

For JavaScript and TypeScript projects, automated linting and type checking can catch many common mistakes. However, these tools cannot prove that business logic is correct or that permissions are enforced properly.

When reviewing a change, ask the AI to explain its implementation and identify its assumptions. Then verify those explanations against the actual code and relevant documentation.

If a function is difficult to understand, simplify it or request a clearer implementation. Code that nobody on the team can confidently maintain is a liability, regardless of how quickly it was generated.

Authentication answers the question, "Who is this user?" Authorization answers, "What is this user allowed to do?"

Both are necessary for applications that protect private information or restrict access to particular features.

An AI-generated application may implement a login screen and assume the security requirement is complete. In reality, hiding a dashboard element or redirecting an unauthenticated visitor does not prevent someone from calling a protected API directly.

Every sensitive operation should enforce the relevant permissions on the server or trusted backend.

For example, an application that allows users to view invoices should verify that the authenticated user is authorized to access the requested invoice. It should not rely solely on a user ID supplied by the browser.

Test both permitted and prohibited actions. A good security test confirms not only that the correct user can access a resource, but also that other users cannot.

For applications handling sensitive data, consider an independent security review before launch.

AI-generated applications often work well with expected inputs. Real users, however, submit incomplete forms, unexpected values, oversized files, malformed requests, and repeated actions.

Input validation and error handling help prevent these conditions from becoming application failures or security vulnerabilities.

Client-side validation improves the user experience, but it should not be the only line of defense. Requests can bypass browser validation entirely.

Validate incoming data on the server, using rules appropriate to each field and operation. Enforce database constraints where applicable, and use parameterized queries or safe ORM methods to prevent SQL injection.

For file uploads, validate file types, enforce size limits, and store files using a design that prevents unauthorized access. Do not assume a filename or content type supplied by the browser is trustworthy.

Users need understandable error messages. Developers need enough diagnostic information to investigate failures.

These requirements should be handled separately.

A user-facing response might say, "We couldn't save your changes. Please try again." An internal log can record a request identifier, the relevant operation, and a sanitized diagnostic message.

Avoid exposing stack traces, database credentials, internal configuration, access tokens, or sensitive records in production responses.

Also test what happens when external APIs time out, database connections fail, or required environment variables are missing.

A production-ready application should fail predictably, protect sensitive information, and provide a clear path to recovery.

Testing is particularly important in vibe coding because generated code can appear convincing even when it implements the wrong behavior.

Asking an AI tool to generate tests is useful, but those tests must be reviewed. A test suite can pass while validating incorrect assumptions or ignoring the application's most important failure cases.

Start with critical workflows rather than trying to test every minor interface detail.

For an e-commerce application, these workflows might include account creation, product selection, checkout, payment confirmation, and order retrieval. For a business dashboard, they might include importing records, updating a customer, assigning permissions, and generating a report.

Pay particular attention to boundary conditions.

What happens when a form is submitted twice? When an API returns an unexpected response? When a database operation fails halfway through a workflow? When two users update the same record simultaneously?

These scenarios often reveal problems that a successful demonstration will not.

For applications that process transactions, test idempotency where appropriate so that retries do not unintentionally create duplicate payments, orders, or records.

A web application can feel fast during development and become sluggish when it handles larger datasets, multiple concurrent users, or slower network connections.

AI-generated code should therefore be evaluated against realistic usage patterns.

Begin with the operations most likely to affect the user experience:

Use browser developer tools and server-side monitoring to identify actual bottlenecks before optimizing.

Database indexes can improve frequently used queries, while pagination can prevent interfaces from loading thousands of records at once. Caching can reduce repeated work when the data and consistency requirements allow it.

For public-facing websites, evaluate relevant Core Web Vitals and test performance on realistic devices and network conditions.

Avoid adding caching layers, complex abstractions, or performance libraries simply because an AI tool recommends them. Every additional component introduces maintenance costs and potential failure points.

Measure first, optimize the bottleneck, and measure again.

Generated applications frequently depend on third-party packages, API services, and environment-specific settings. Each dependency expands the application's maintenance and security responsibilities.

Before deployment, inspect the dependency manifest and lockfile. Remove unnecessary packages, verify that required packages exist, and review known vulnerabilities using appropriate dependency-scanning tools.

Keep frameworks and libraries updated according to a deliberate maintenance process rather than automatically accepting every suggested upgrade.

API keys, database passwords, signing secrets, and other credentials should not be embedded in client-side code or committed to a public repository.

Use environment variables or an appropriate secrets manager, depending on the deployment environment. Restrict credentials to the permissions they actually require, and rotate any secret that may have been exposed.

Remember that a variable included in a browser-delivered bundle is not secret simply because it was originally configured as an environment variable.

Also verify that development and production environments use the correct endpoints, database connections, authentication settings, and logging configuration.

A local application that works with unrestricted development credentials may behave very differently when deployed under production permissions.

Deployment should be a controlled process, not a final experiment performed after development is complete.

A repeatable deployment process reduces human error and helps teams identify which changes introduced a problem.

For projects that support it, establish a continuous integration and continuous deployment (CI/CD) pipeline with automated checks for:

Before launch, verify that you can restore the application to a known-good state.

This includes maintaining version control, documenting deployment steps, backing up important data, and testing restoration procedures. A backup is only useful if it can be restored successfully.

Database schema changes deserve particular attention. An incompatible migration can make an otherwise successful deployment unusable. For important applications, plan migrations so that application versions can coexist safely during rollout and rollback.

Also check how the application behaves when a deployment fails, a service becomes unavailable, or a configuration change introduces a regression.

A reliable release process should make failures easier to detect, contain, and recover from.

Testing happens before release and in controlled environments. Monitoring helps developers understand how the application behaves after real users begin using it.

Without monitoring, a deployment can appear successful while users encounter failed requests, slow pages, broken integrations, or database errors.

Set alerts for failures that require action. Avoid collecting unnecessary personal data in logs, and define retention and access policies appropriate to the application.

For AI-powered features, monitor additional factors such as model response failures, latency, token usage, and output quality. An application that depends on an external AI service must account for rate limits, changing model behavior, and provider outages.

Monitoring does not prevent every failure. It makes failures visible enough to investigate and resolve.

Before releasing an AI-generated web application, use the following checklist to assess whether the essentials are in place.

Requirements: Core features, user roles, constraints, and acceptance criteria are documented.

Code quality: Important code paths have been reviewed, and unnecessary dependencies have been removed.

Authentication: Identity and session handling are implemented appropriately.

Authorization: Protected resources and operations enforce server-side permissions.

Input validation: Requests, uploads, and database operations are protected by appropriate validation and constraints.

Error handling: Failures are handled predictably without exposing sensitive information.

Testing: Critical workflows, negative cases, and regression scenarios have been tested.

Security: Dependencies, secrets, configuration, and common vulnerability risks have been reviewed.

Performance: Important queries, endpoints, and user journeys have been evaluated under realistic conditions.

Deployment: Builds and releases follow a repeatable process with appropriate quality gates.

Recovery: Backups, restoration procedures, and rollback options have been considered and tested where applicable.

Monitoring: Errors, availability, and performance can be observed after release.

Not every application needs the same level of testing or operational complexity. A personal prototype has different requirements from a financial application or a multi-tenant SaaS product.

The important thing is to identify the risks associated with the intended use and address them before exposing real users or sensitive data.

A developer does not necessarily need an external review for every small experiment. However, an independent review becomes increasingly valuable when the application handles sensitive information, processes payments, supports multiple customer organizations, or implements complex permissions.

It is also worth considering when the original developer cannot explain important parts of the generated code or when repeated AI-generated changes have made the architecture difficult to understand.

A useful review should examine more than formatting and syntax. It should evaluate application behavior, data access, authentication, security controls, error handling, deployment configuration, and maintainability.

For teams using AI to accelerate development, the goal is not to eliminate AI-generated code. It is to make sure that the resulting software meets the quality standards expected of any application entering production.

Vibe coding changes how quickly developers can move from an idea to working software. It does not remove the need to understand requirements, validate behavior, protect data, test failure scenarios, and maintain the application after release.

The most reliable approach is to treat AI as a development accelerator within a disciplined engineering process. Define the requirements, review generated code, test critical workflows, enforce security controls, automate deployment checks, and monitor the application after launch.

Start with the checklist in this article and prioritize the controls that matter most to your application's risk profile. For a deeper look at the development process, read this guide to production-ready vibe coding.

The goal is not simply to build an application quickly. It is to build one that users can trust, developers can maintain, and businesses can depend on.

#10706. A classic brain test exposed AI's biggest weakness

Artificial intelligence systems can write essays, answer questions, and solve complex problems. But new research suggests they may struggle with something humans do every day: staying focused on the task at hand when distractions get in the way.

Researchers led by Suketu Patel put several leading AI models through a well-known psychology experiment called the Stroop task. The results revealed a significant difference between how AI systems process information and how the human brain manages attention.

The Stroop task is a classic psychological test that has been used for decades to study attention, concentration, and self-control.

In the test, color words such as "red," "blue," or "green" are displayed in colored ink. Sometimes the word and the ink color match. For example, the word "red" might appear in red ink. Other times they conflict, such as the word "red" printed in blue ink.

Participants are asked to name the color of the ink rather than read the word itself.

That sounds simple, but it creates a challenge because reading words is an automatic habit for most people. The brain must suppress the urge to read the word and instead focus on identifying the ink color.

Psychologists often use the task to measure what is known as executive control, a set of mental processes that helps people regulate attention, resist distractions, and stay focused on goals.

The researchers wanted to see whether modern large language models (LLMs) handle this challenge in the same way humans do.

LLMs are the AI systems behind tools such as ChatGPT, Claude, and Gemini. They are trained on enormous amounts of text and learn patterns in language, allowing them to generate responses that often appear remarkably human.

When given short lists containing five color words, the AI systems generally performed well, even when the words and colors did not match.

However, the picture changed dramatically as the lists became longer.

GPT-4o achieved 91% accuracy when working with five words. At ten words, its accuracy fell to 57%. When the list expanded to forty words, accuracy dropped to just 15%.

Claude 3.5 Sonnet maintained stable performance through lists of twenty words but then experienced a sharp decline, falling to 24% accuracy with forty-word lists.

The researchers observed similar patterns in GPT-5, Claude Opus 4.1, and Gemini 2.5.

The challenge became even more difficult when matching and mismatched color words appeared together in the same list.

Under those conditions, performance deteriorated further. Accuracy for the mismatched items dropped to nearly zero in some cases.

According to the researchers, the AI models had trouble maintaining the instruction to identify ink colors. Instead, they increasingly defaulted to reading the words themselves.

In other words, the systems appeared unable to consistently suppress the response they had been most heavily trained to produce.

This finding is particularly interesting because humans face a similar conflict. People are generally much better at reading words than naming ink colors. Yet despite this bias, most individuals can maintain high accuracy and stable performance even when confronted with long lists of conflicting words and colors.

The study highlights an important distinction between human and artificial intelligence.

Although modern AI systems can produce impressive language and reasoning capabilities, their underlying mechanisms differ from the attention processes found in biological brains.

Humans can often sustain focus on a specific goal while filtering out competing information. The results suggest that current AI models may struggle with this type of cognitive control when tasks become increasingly demanding.

The researchers argue that the performance collapse seen in these experiments points to fundamental limitations in today's large language models. While AI can sometimes mimic human behavior, its ability to maintain attention appears to operate very differently from the way people do.

The findings offer a reminder that even the most advanced AI systems still have weaknesses, particularly when tasks require them to resist distractions and stay focused over extended sequences of information.

Materials provided by PNAS Nexus. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10701. Brain-inspired chip runs near absolute zero and could transform quantum computing

Researchers at the University of Hong Kong (HKU) have unveiled a significant advance in cryogenic electronics that could help overcome key challenges in quantum computing and support future deep space missions. The team, from HKU's Department of Electrical and Computer Engineering and the Centre for Advanced Semiconductors and Integrated Circuits (CASIC), developed a programmable neuromorphic hardware platform capable of operating at temperatures near absolute zero.

The research was led by Professor Yuhao Zhang and PhD student Xin Yang. Their work introduces a new method for generating and controlling negative differential resistance (NDR) in industry standard Silicon Carbide (SiC) MOSFETs. Using this approach, the researchers demonstrated for the first time that a single transistor can reproduce the energy efficient "spiking" activity of biological neurons at temperatures as low as 10mK.

Brain-Inspired Hardware for Quantum Computing

Quantum computers depend on sophisticated control electronics to manage qubits, which are highly sensitive and must be kept at millikelvin temperatures. Existing silicon based control systems consume considerable power and produce unwanted heat, making it necessary to position them away from the qubits themselves. That distance creates extensive wiring requirements that can hinder performance and make large scale quantum computers more difficult to build.

"Our work introduces a hardware platform that can be integrated alongside quantum processors," said Professor Zhang. "By using the unique carrier dynamics in silicon carbide, we can create circuits that are thousands of times more energy-efficient than conventional electronics, significantly reducing the thermal load on cryogenic systems."

Silicon Carbide Reveals Unique Cryogenic Behavior

The team found that SiC MOSFETs display a strong "S-shape" NDR effect when cooled below 2K. This behavior is driven by electron-donor impact ionization (EDII). Unlike other technologies that depend on heat generated within a device, the newly observed mechanism arises directly from the material's atomic properties. As a result, it remains highly stable and can be reproduced consistently across different manufacturing batches.

"This is a robust and scalable approach," said Mr. Yang. "Because SiC is already used globally in electric vehicles and power grids, we can leverage existing industrial foundries to manufacture these cryogenic chips on 300-mm wafers."

From Artificial Neurons to Deep Space Missions

The study also demonstrated that these artificial neurons can be linked together, or "cascaded," into larger networks. This capability could enable advanced local data processing at cryogenic temperatures and improve important quantum computing functions such as quantum error correction and real time quantum control.

The potential applications extend well beyond quantum computing. Because the circuits are designed to operate reliably in extremely cold environments, they could also be valuable for deep space exploration. Future systems may be able to function in the harsh conditions found on the Moon's surface or in the distant regions of our solar system.

The findings were published in Nature Communications in a paper titled "Cryogenic neuromorphic circuits using gate-controlled negative differential resistance in silicon carbide."

Materials provided by The University of Hong Kong. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.
