#10699. How I Built a CRM with Vibe Coding Instead of Spending $1,000+ on App Builders

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

I have designed more than 100 websites and helped clients set up over 12 CRM and ERP systems. For years, my work revolved around WordPress, digital marketing, business automation, and helping companies choose the right technology.

But building a web application from scratch was a different challenge.

I wanted to move beyond configuring existing software and experience the entire product development process myself. I had an idea, a clear understanding of business workflows, and access to AI coding agents. What I did not want was another expensive subscription that would limit how I built, deployed, or maintained the application.

That decision led me to build a CRM using Claude, Next.js, Firebase Firestore, Tailwind CSS, Vercel, and Git.

The experience taught me something important: AI can reduce the cost of building software, but understanding what you are building still matters.

My background gave me a useful starting point. After working on more than 100 websites, I understood how users interact with interfaces, how businesses collect leads, and why a confusing workflow can make even good software difficult to use.

Setting up CRM and ERP systems for clients also helped me understand the operational side of software. I had seen how businesses manage contacts, sales pipelines, follow-ups, and reporting.

However, configuring an existing platform and building an application from scratch are two very different experiences.

When you configure a CRM, many architectural decisions have already been made. When you build one, you must think about the data model, authentication, permissions, integrations, deployment, and what happens when something fails.

I decided to use this project to learn those things through practical experience rather than limiting myself to website design.

My first instinct could have been to use a visual app builder such as Lovable or Bolt. These tools can be excellent for quickly generating prototypes and getting an idea off the ground.

But I wanted greater control over the codebase, architecture, and deployment process. I also wanted to avoid accumulating app-builder subscription costs during development.

Instead, I chose an AI-assisted coding workflow.

Claude acted as my coding partner. I described requirements, generated implementation drafts, reviewed the output, debugged problems, and refined features through repeated iterations.

This did not mean I could simply write a prompt and receive a finished application. I still had to make decisions, understand the generated code, test the results, and correct mistakes.

The financial advantage was meaningful. By avoiding paid app-builder subscriptions for this project, I saved more than $1,000 in potential tool costs and related expenses I would otherwise have budgeted for. That was my project-specific saving, not a claim that every custom application will be cheaper to build.

More importantly, I ended up with a codebase and development workflow I could control rather than depending entirely on a particular app-building interface.

I wanted a modern stack that supported fast iteration without sacrificing control over the application.

Claude helped me translate requirements into code, work through implementation problems, and iterate on features. I treated its output as a starting point, not something to trust without review.

Next.js provided the foundation for the web application. It gave me a structured way to build the interface and application logic while keeping the project organized as it grew.

Firestore handled the application's data storage. Its document-based model and integration with the Firebase ecosystem made it a practical choice for my development workflow.

Choosing a database is only part of the job, though. Data structure, access rules, query patterns, and the way records relate to users all require careful thought.

Tailwind CSS helped me build and refine the interface without maintaining a large collection of separate CSS rules. It also made iterative UI improvements easier.

Git gave me a history of code changes and a way to manage development safely. When an AI-generated change caused a problem, version control helped me inspect what had changed and recover when necessary.

Vercel provided the deployment environment for the application. Its integration with Git made it easier to move from code changes to deployed versions.

Together, these tools allowed me to build with AI assistance while retaining control over the underlying code and deployment process.

The biggest surprise was how quickly AI could produce a first version of a feature—and how much work could remain afterward.

A screen might look correct but behave incorrectly when connected to real data. An API integration could work in a simple test but fail when an external service returned an unexpected response. A feature could appear complete while overlooking permissions or edge cases.

Connecting different services required more than generating a request. I had to consider authentication, request and response formats, error handling, timeouts, and what the application should do when an external service became unavailable.

I learned to break integrations into smaller steps and verify each one before building additional functionality around it.

An application that stores customer information cannot rely on a good-looking login screen for security.

I had to pay attention to authentication, database access rules, permission boundaries, and the possibility of users accessing data they should not see. AI assistance made implementation faster, but security decisions still required careful review and testing.

A feature can technically work and still feel frustrating to use.

I learned to pay attention to loading states, error messages, navigation, responsiveness, and how many steps users needed to complete common tasks. I also had to think about how database queries and application behavior would change as the amount of data increased.

These were not problems that could be solved by writing a better prompt alone. They required observation, testing, and repeated refinement.

The most valuable lesson was that vibe coding is not the same as handing over software development to an AI model.

The developer's role changes. Instead of writing every line manually, more time goes into defining requirements, directing implementation, reviewing code, testing behavior, and deciding which trade-offs are acceptable.

My experience in WordPress and business systems helped me understand the workflows I wanted to support. AI coding tools helped me move from those requirements to a working implementation more quickly.

The combination was more valuable than either one on its own.

I also learned to avoid building too many features at once. Small, testable changes made debugging easier and helped me understand the application as it evolved.

Finally, I realized that shipping an application is not the end of the work. Maintenance, security updates, monitoring, and improvements based on actual usage remain important after launch.

Building this application expanded my skills beyond website design and configuring third-party software. I gained hands-on experience with application architecture, database integration, API workflows, version control, and deployment.

The project also became a meaningful addition to my personal portfolio. You can read the NeerSoft CRM project case study to see how I approached the build.

I am not suggesting that every business should build its own CRM or that app builders are the wrong choice. For some projects, a ready-made platform is the fastest and most economical option. For others, a visual app builder is an excellent way to validate an idea before investing in custom development.

The right approach depends on the requirements, budget, technical constraints, and long-term plans.

For me, building from scratch with AI coding assistance was the right learning experience. It helped me avoid more than $1,000 in potential app-builder costs while giving me greater control over the application and teaching me skills I could not have gained by configuring an existing platform alone.

My biggest takeaway is simple: AI makes building software more accessible, but the ability to define problems, make sound technical decisions, and verify the results is what turns generated code into a useful application.

About me: Ritesh Sharma is an SEO specialist, WordPress website designer, and vibe-coding practitioner with 11+ years of experience in digital marketing and web development. He specializes in technical SEO, AEO, GEO, website performance, and building user-focused digital experiences. He also explores AI-powered development, automation, and no-code technologies to help businesses build smarter, search-friendly websites and digital solutions.

#10698. Alabama high school offering $180K to coach struggling football team

An Alabama high school is offering up to $180,000 a year for a new football coach, nearly three times what the state’s average teacher earns.

Dothan High School is looking for someone to turn its struggling football program into a championship contender, and the school district is prepared to pay big money to make it happen.

According to an official job posting published October 28, Dothan City Schools is offering between $150,000 and $180,000 annually for its next head football coach, depending on experience.

For comparison, the average Alabama teacher earns $62,659 annually, according to figures reported by AL.com in February 2026. Starting salaries for first-year teachers were just $47,600 during the 2025-26 school year.

That means the school’s highest advertised coaching salary is approximately 2.9 times the statewide teacher average and nearly four times the starting pay for a new educator.

The job description makes it clear that the district expects results for that money. Applicants must have a proven record of winning football games, making playoff appearances and developing championship-caliber teams.

The position is also a full-time, 12-month role, with the district offering additional salary supplements to attract offensive and defensive coordinators.

Dothan’s search comes after former head coach Jed Kennedy resigned in September following a 2-3 start to the season. Kennedy finished his tenure with a 29-19 record, while the Wolves ended their 2026 regular season at 3-7.

The district hopes to have its new coach in place by late February 2027, according to Yahoo Sports.

College offers to pay students’ tuition if they can watch a full football game

Rivals starts charging high school athletes $500 for scouting evaluations and ranking consideration

High school football coach fired after “one in a million” kick hits player in groin

The unusually high coaching salary highlights the money surrounding high school football, where expectations can be enormous.

Just weeks earlier, a high school football coach was fired after his team took a 63-0 lead in a game that was ultimately called off at halftime.

For Dothan, the message is clear: The district is prepared to spend big to build a winning football program. Whether that investment delivers a championship remains to be seen.

#10696. SpaceX wants to build AI data centers in space. Will it work?

Imagine if one company could become the railroad, electric utility and cloud-computing provider of the emerging space economy. That potential fueled excitement around the long-anticipated initial public offering of SpaceX. Investors are not simply betting on rockets anymore. They are betting on an entire orbital ecosystem.

Among the most ambitious and challenging ideas riding this wave of enthusiasm is something that sounds almost like science fiction: orbital data centers. SpaceX may be one of the most well-known companies seeking to build them, but it is not the only one.

The logic is seductive: Launch the data centers into orbit, where solar energy is abundant and land, water and local power grids are no longer constraints. As artificial intelligence drives an explosion in computing demand, companies are pitching orbital data centers as a way to escape the growing environmental and infrastructure pressures of Earth-based computing. Data centers often also face backlash from the public at having these centers located in their communities.

But there is a vast difference between launching satellites and operating an industrial-scale computing infrastructure in orbit. Space is unforgiving. Radiation damages electronics. The electronics generate enormous amounts of heat, and getting rid of that heat is surprisingly difficult in space. Repairs are extraordinarily expensive, and every pound launched into orbit still carries a significant cost.

We are engineering professors who study data center design and space systems engineering. Building a space-based data center will involve considerations from both sides.

First off, consider what goes into an Earth-based data center, like those that you’ve probably begun to see pop up everywhere. These facilities power cloud computing, video streaming, online banking, scientific computing and, increasingly, artificial intelligence. But a data center is much more than a room full of servers.

A data center needs several things to operate reliably. The first is electric power. Servers, networking equipment and storage devices consume large amounts of electricity, and that power demand is growing rapidly with AI.

The second is cooling. Almost all the electricity consumed by servers eventually becomes heat. If that heat is not removed quickly and reliably, equipment performance drops, failures increase and the data center can shut down. Cooling systems often include air handling units, chillers, cooling towers, pumps and, increasingly, liquid-cooling equipment. In many facilities, cooling is the largest energy consumer after the computing equipment itself.

The third is physical infrastructure, including the necessary land, buildings, structural support, backup power, water systems, communication networks and maintenance access. Data centers also need to be close enough to users and network backbones to provide fast digital services.

In short, Earth-based data centers are large electrical and thermal infrastructure systems built around computing hardware.

So what would it take to build these data centers in space, and why are companies finding this possibility such an interesting business proposition?

As on Earth, these data centers would require massive amounts of power. In space, this power would come from solar panels. The Sun always shines in space and can’t be blocked by clouds. However, depending on the orbit the solar panels are put in, the Earth may shadow them for some portion of the orbit.

And even the best solar cells available today can convert only about half the sunlight that hits them to electricity.

Another potential advantage found in space is cooling. The cold background of space (near minus 455 degrees Fahrenheit, or minus 270 degrees Celsius) creates an opportunity: waste heat from the data center could escape into space through radiators, keeping the electronics cool.

In principle, that design could eliminate some of the bulky and water-intensive cooling infrastructure used on Earth. However, those thermal radiators would require a large amount of surface area, and that would be in addition to the area required by the solar panels.

In space, there is no air to blow across hot equipment and help heat escape. The heat has to leave as infrared radiation, which is a relatively slow process. As a result, removing 10 megawatts of waste heat can require radiator surfaces comparable to the size of two football fields.

Space-based data centers could also avoid some of the local conflicts that come with building large data centers on the ground. Many communities resist new data center developments because of their land use, energy and water demand, and noise and environmental impact.

A space-based system would avoid competing for local land and water resources, and it would not generate neighborhood noise or require local zoning approval in the same way.

However, space is already getting crowded, and launching thousands of large orbital data centers would accelerate this issue. Orbital debris and micrometeorites are hazards because they can puncture the space data center, and a worst-case collision could destroy it and create even more space debris.

The frequency of space launches necessary to send all the equipment to orbit may also become a concern for some communities. SpaceX has had protests at its launch complex in Boca Chica, Texas, from local activists who argue that its rocket testing and launches damage the surrounding environment.

All that data would need to be sent between Earth and these data centers – and between the data centers themselves – using radio waves or laser communications systems. Although satellite constellations such as Starlink and Amazon Leo have demonstrated that doing this is possible, the amount of data sent to and from space would balloon.

These data centers, along with their solar panels and radiators, cannot be launched in one piece and would need to be assembled in space. This process would require new equipment for in-space servicing, assembly and manufacturing.

Another key challenge is the refresh cycle of computing hardware. Data center servers are not built to last forever. Operators on Earth usually replace or upgrade hardware every three to five years as chips improve, workloads change and equipment ages.

And equipment failures can require replacing components. The refresh and repair processes are relatively straightforward on Earth, where workers can physically remove and replace servers.

In space, refresh and repair becomes much harder. Hardware sent to orbit may be difficult or too expensive to upgrade. If the computing platform cannot be updated, or too many components fail, it may become obsolete long before the surrounding infrastructure reaches the end of its useful life.

In a field where performance improves so rapidly and demand from computing continues to increase, this hurdle could prove a major economic and operational challenge.

Then there is the harshness of space. These data centers would be in a near vacuum, with constant radiation hitting them. And depending on their orbit, they would go from hot when in the sunlight to cold in Earth’s shadow many times a day. All of these challenges, and more, are issues that will need to be addressed.

Despite these challenges, companies are moving forward with designing space-based data centers. SpaceX just announced the design for its AI1 Compute Satellite, which it hopes to use as an orbital data center spacecraft. However, this satellite is 100 to 1,000 times less capable than current Earth-based data centers.

Not every computing task makes sense to do in space. Many data center applications depend on fast response times and close connections to users on Earth. Financial transactions, interactive AI services and most cloud applications are extremely sensitive to delay.

More feasible early applications may be those that are less latency-sensitive and more tightly connected to space operations. Examples could include processing Earth observation data from satellites, military or intelligence data processing, scientific computing related to space missions, or specialized computing for satellites and other space assets.

In other words, the first viable space data centers may serve space-based customers before they compete with mainstream cloud data centers on Earth.

Materials provided by The Conversation. Original written by Sven BilÃ©n and Wangda Zuo. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10695. Les supermarchés de l'UE paient d'un firmware de réfrigérateur non mis à jour

Breaking cybersecurity news, news analysis, commentary, and other content from around the world, with an initial focus on the Middle East & Africa, the Asia Pacific, Europe, and Latin America.

Des centaines de magasins ont subi des pertes se chiffrant en millions d'euros parce qu'ils avaient fait appel à des tiers pour gérer le micrologiciel OT obsolète qui pilotait leurs systèmes de climatisation.

This translation is provided by our internal AI translation tool.

Des centaines de supermarchés français ont subi cette semaine des pertes s'élevant à plusieurs dizaines, voire plusieurs centaines de millions d'euros, à la suite de cyberattaques qui ont perturbé leurs systèmes de réfrigération.

Dans la nuit de lundi, des pirates informatiques ont piraté les contrôleurs des systèmes de réfrigération commerciaux de grandes chaînes de supermarchés françaises. Ils ont transformé les réfrigérateurs, les congélateurs et les chambres froides en appareils de chauffage, faisant grimper leur température jusqu’à 50 degrés Celsius (plus de 120 degrés Fahrenheit), ce qui a complètement abîmé tous les aliments qu’ils contenaient et aurait même provoqué quelques petits incendies. L’identité et les motivations de l’auteur de cette attaque restent inconnues.

Les contrôleurs de réfrigération permettent aux commerçants de régler et de surveiller à distance la température de leurs réfrigérateurs, 24 heures sur 24 et 7 jours sur 7. Il s’ensuit donc que les gérants de magasin auraient pu être alertés ou se rendre compte manuellement que quelque chose n’allait pas. Pour empêcher cela, des sources françaises indiquent que les attaquants ont prolongé les cycles de dégivrage des systèmes ciblés — processus au cours duquel les congélateurs font fondre intentionnellement le givre et la glace accumulés — afin que le réchauffement passe pour un entretien de routine.

Related:EU Grocers Pay a Steep Price for Unpatched Fridge Firmware

Selon les propres rapports des entreprises, les attaques ont touché un peu plus de 700 magasins d’alimentation et commerces de détail, tous au cours de la même nuit. Un analyste de la société française de cybersécurité Sekoia a déclaré à Reuters que l’Espagne, le Portugal, la Pologne, l’Allemagne et la Belgique avaient également été touchés, et qu’une grande chaîne de supermarchés aux Pays-Bas avait mis ses systèmes hors service par mesure de précaution, selon l’AP. Une seule chaîne française, Intermarché, a fait état de pertes s’élevant à plusieurs dizaines de millions d’euros.

Pire encore, les experts soulignent que ces attaques auraient très certainement pu être évitéesgrâce à des mesures élémentaires de cybersécurité.

Les premiers rapports suggéraient que les attaques de lundi étaient liées aux contrôleurs de réfrigération du fabricant danois Danfoss. Danfoss occupe une place importante dans le domaine des solutions de contrôle pour les supermarchés et la grande distribution alimentaire, avec plus de 50 000 installations de sa technologie à travers le monde, selon son site web.

Related:Taiwan Bullet Train Hack Highlights Cybersecurity Gaps in Rail Systems

Le 9 août, des chercheurs en cybersécurité de l’équipe Team82 de Claroty ont révélé trois vulnérabilités dans la plateforme AK-SM 800A de Danfoss. Ces failles — référencées sous les numéros CVE-2025-41450, CVE-2025-41451 et CVE-2025-41452 — permettaient aux attaquants de procéder à l’exécution de code à distance (RCE) ou à un déni de service (DoS) contre les parcs de réfrigération des entreprises. Danfoss a corrigé ces failles mais comptait sur ses clients pour mettre à jour eux-mêmes leurs systèmes.

Dans une déclaration à Reuters, Danfoss a rejeté la responsabilité sur les clients utilisant un micrologiciel obsolète, indiquant qu’elle « conseillait depuis longtemps à ses clients de maintenir les systèmes pris en charge à jour avec le dernier micrologiciel et de migrer les systèmes hérités vers des solutions de dernière génération ».Le fabricant d'équipement d'origine (OEM) a fourni davantage d'informations à une publication spécialisée dans la réfrigération, Cooling Post. Selon lui, la « majorité » des unités concernées fonctionnaient sous AK-SM 800 ou son prédécesseur, l'AK-SC355. Ces deux systèmes ont depuis longtemps atteint leur fin de vie : le premier en 2021, le second en 2016.

Related:AI-Driven Cyberattack on Mexico Couldn't Breach OT Systems

Mardi, le CERT national français (CERT-FR) avait identifié qu’une autre plateforme de contrôleurs de réfrigération — « XWEB », des fournisseurs Dixell et de sa société mère Copeland — avait également été impliquée dans les attaques. Il n’a pas précisé quelles vulnérabilités ou versions de micrologiciel pouvaient être en cause, bien que les indices circonstanciels indiquent désormais clairement que les auteurs de ces menaces s’appuient sur les recherches de Team82. Le jour même où ils ont révélé ces vulnérabilités affectant Danfoss, ces mêmes chercheurs ont divulgué 23 autres touchant XWEB Pro, dont 21 de gravité élevée.

Le 26 août, une série étrange de rapports similaires a commencé à filtrer depuis des installations militaires américaines.Les groupes frigorifiques tombaient en panne. Dans chaque base où cela se produisait, les gens ont probablement pensé qu’il s’agissait d’un problème électrique inhabituel. Mais peu à peu, de plus en plus de sites ont signalé le même phénomène.

Après être tombée sur ces rapports étranges sur les réseaux sociaux, une journaliste indépendante a émis une hypothèse hasardeuse. « Je suis suffisamment lucide pour savoir que cela semble insensé, mais je vous demande de me suivre », a écrit M. Elizabeth sur Substack. « Je pense qu’il y a une probabilité non nulle que quelqu’un ait piraté les congélateurs des commissariats. »

À ce jour, l’armée américaine n’a pas confirmé qui ou quoi était à l’origine des pannes survenues dans les cantines réparties sur au moins 14 bases de l’armée de l’air, installations de la marine et postes de l’armée de terre distincts. Il se pourrait que ce soit une coïncidence que ces attaques aient eu lieu seulement deux semaines après la divulgation des vulnérabilités par Danfoss.

Ce serait d’autant plus une coïncidence que la Defense Commissary Agency (DeCA) — l’agence centrale du ministère de la Défense (DoD) chargée de superviser les magasins militaires — a explicitement mentionné avoir utilisé les technologies de Danfoss par le passé. Par exemple, dans un modèle de cahier des charges techniquedestiné aux prestataires des magasins militaires datant de juin 2022, la DeCA citait le Danfoss AK-SM 800A comme « base de conception » pour les différentes unités de contrôle.

Fort de son expérience, Bill Moore, PDG de Xona Systems, se souvient : « Dans bon nombre de ces magasins physiques — mais aussi dans les usines de fabrication, les centrales électriques, les sites pétroliers et gaziers, c’est le cas partout —, le plus gros problème est que les organisations parviennent plutôt bien à segmenter leurs propres réseaux. Mais en matière de technologies opérationnelles (OT), il y a tous ces équipementiers et tiers qui ont besoin d’un accès. Et les entreprises n’y ont pas vraiment prêté attention. »

En accordant à de nombreux fournisseurs et équipementiers l’accès à leurs systèmes, les entreprises finissent par s’exposer à des attaques à distance. « Et il n’y a littéralement aucune visibilité ni gouvernance sur bon nombre de ces connexions. Je dirais que c’est vrai dans un pourcentage élevé de cas — la moitié, peut-être plus, dans l’ensemble. Elles laissent en quelque sorte tous leurs équipementiers agir à leur guise, mais nous avons constaté que de nombreux pirates compromettent ces connexions tierces », déplore-t-il.

Dans un e-mail adressé à CNBC, Les Mousquetaires — société mère d’Intermarché et de Netto — a expliqué que l’attaque avait eu lieu via un prestataire de services chargé des systèmes de réfrigération dans certains de ses magasins.

« En résumé : déconnectez vos équipements d’Internet », déclare Andrew Ginter, vice-président chargé de la sécurité industrielle chez Waterfall Security Solutions. Les recherches effectuées cette semaine sur Shodan ont par exemple révélé des milliers de systèmes Danfoss exposés sur Internet, dont environ 1 500 rien qu’en France.

« Dans un monde idéal, les entreprises protégeraient leurs systèmes de contrôle derrière un accès à distance contrôlé par du matériel », explique M. Ginter, « mais même un simple pare-feu entre ces appareils et Internet serait déjà utile. »

Pour les pare-feu de base, il conseille aux entreprises :

De mettre en place une règle « tout refuser »

De n’autoriser les connexions qu’à partir d’adresses IP spécifiques

D’appliquer un mot de passe et une authentification à deux facteurs à ces connexion

De mettre à jour automatiquement le pare-feu, pour éviter de l’oublier

Utilisent des appareils de qualité commerciale ou industrielle, et non grand public ; de nombreux appareils grand public ne proposent pas de mises à jour, ou ne se mettent pas à jour rapidement

Enfin, ajoute-t-il, « Remplacez le pare-feu strictement tous les quatre ans. Pourquoi ? Parce que si nous avons des appareils exposés à Internet, neuf fois sur dix, nous n’avons pas d’équipe de sécurité, et nous ne remarquerons pas quand nos pare-feu ne seront plus pris en charge et que les mises à jour automatiques cesseront. »

« Les mesures de base ne suffisent pas pour les infrastructures critiques de grande envergure ou l’industrie lourde, mais elles contribueront grandement à empêcher les hacktivistes de s’introduire dans vos congélateurs », conclut Ginter. « Retirez vos équipements d’Internet. Faites-le de toute urgence. »

#10692. Millions of exploding stars could soon reveal dark energy's secrets

Researchers led by the Institute of Cosmos Sciences of the University of Barcelona (ICCUB) have developed a new technique that could greatly improve how scientists study the expansion of the Universe and investigate the mysterious force known as dark energy.

Published in Nature Astronomy, the research introduces a framework called CIGaRS that can extract far more information from Type Ia supernovae, the powerful stellar explosions used to measure vast cosmic distances. Unlike many current approaches, the method relies mainly on imaging data rather than expensive spectroscopic observations. The advance is expected to help astronomers take full advantage of the enormous datasets that will soon arrive from next generation sky surveys, particularly those conducted by the Vera C. Rubin Observatory.

Type Ia supernovae occur when white dwarf stars explode. Because these explosions reach nearly the same intrinsic brightness, astronomers use them as "standard candles": by comparing their actual brightness with how bright they appear from Earth, researchers can calculate their distance.

These measurements played a crucial role in the discovery that the Universe is expanding at an accelerating rate. Scientists attribute that acceleration to dark energy, one of the most significant unsolved questions in modern physics.

However, there is an important complication. Type Ia supernovae are not perfectly identical.

How Host Galaxies Affect Supernova Measurements

Over the past 20 years, astronomers have found that a supernova's observed brightness is influenced by the galaxy where it occurs. Supernovae found in older or more massive galaxies can appear slightly different from those occurring in younger or less massive galaxies.

Researchers have typically accounted for these differences using relatively simple correction methods. While useful, these approximations can limit the accuracy of distance measurements and, in turn, the precision of cosmological studies.

A Unified Model of Supernovae and the Universe

The new framework addresses this challenge by modeling multiple factors simultaneously. Rather than treating each component independently, the researchers built a single, integrated model that includes the supernova explosions themselves, their host galaxies, the dust that alters their light, changes in supernova rates throughout cosmic history, and even the expansion of the Universe.

By connecting all of these ingredients within one statistical and physical framework, the team can capture relationships that are often overlooked when the pieces are analyzed separately.

"A powerful way of modelling the Universe is to simulate it ab initio in the computer using Bayesian inference," says Raúl Jiménez (ICREA-ICCUB), co-author of the study. "This provides a way to vary all possible parameters at the same time to predict what Universe we live in. Furthermore, by having this capacity, one can look into possible 'unknown unknown' systematics to understand their effect. The impact of these systematics in our inference is arguably the most important missing ingredient in current approaches to model the Universe."

Using Artificial Intelligence To Analyze the Cosmos

Building such a comprehensive model would normally require enormous computing power. To make the approach practical, the researchers turned to a modern technique called simulation-based inference.

The process begins with scientists generating large numbers of simulated universes based on physical models. A neural network (a type of artificial intelligence) then learns how the simulated observations relate to the physical properties that produced them. Once trained, the system can compare real astronomical observations with its simulations and determine the most likely underlying parameters.

This strategy makes it possible to analyze tens of thousands of supernovae simultaneously, a task that would be impractical using traditional techniques.

Accurate Galaxy Distances From Images Alone

One of the study's most significant findings is that the framework can determine galaxy distances (redshifts) with high accuracy using only imaging data.

Redshift measures how much a galaxy's light has been stretched as the Universe expands. It provides information about both the galaxy's distance and how far back in time we are observing it.

According to the researchers, the new method delivers redshift estimates with precision comparable to spectroscopic measurements, but without requiring spectra. This capability is especially important because upcoming surveys are expected to identify millions of supernova candidates, while only a small percentage can realistically receive spectroscopic follow-up observations.

Ready for the Rubin Observatory Data Deluge

The Vera C. Rubin Observatory, currently being built in Chile, is expected to begin a decade long survey of the sky in the near future. During that mission, it will discover an unprecedented number of supernovae. Roughly 99% of those objects will be observed only photometrically, meaning through images taken in different colors rather than detailed spectra.

The CIGaRS framework was specifically developed with this challenge in mind.

"Unlike other frameworks, which require analytic simplifications, our no-compromise end-to-end simulation-based inference approach is uniquely capable of extracting the full cosmological and astrophysical information from the Rubin Observatory's hard-earned data, while avoiding the pitfalls of selection and modelling biases," says Konstantin Karchev (ICCUB-SISSA Trieste), lead author of the study.

The benefits extend beyond measuring dark energy. The framework also provides new information about the origins of Type Ia supernovae themselves.

By reconstructing how supernova occurrence rates vary with the ages of stars in different galaxies, the model helps scientists investigate long standing questions about the systems that eventually produce these explosions.

The researchers found that combining physics based simulations with artificial intelligence can overcome several limitations of current cosmological methods. They estimate that the approach could improve cosmological constraints by as much as a factor of four compared with traditional techniques that depend only on a relatively small sample of spectroscopically observed supernovae.

As the Rubin Observatory prepares to usher in a new era of astronomical discovery, tools such as CIGaRS could help scientists extract the maximum amount of information from its observations and gain a deeper understanding of the Universe.

Materials provided by University of Barcelona. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10688. Quantum mechanics once baffled scientists. Now it's changing the world

For much of the early 20th century, quantum mechanics was one of the most puzzling ideas in science. The theory challenged conventional thinking and left even leading physicists struggling to make sense of its implications. A century later, it has become the foundation of technologies that influence daily life, including lasers, microchips, secure communications, and emerging quantum computers.

In a new perspective article published in Science, Dr. Marlan Scully of Texas A&M University reflects on the remarkable evolution of quantum mechanics, from an abstract theory about tiny particles to a powerful framework helping researchers tackle some of the most difficult questions in science.

"Quantum mechanics started as a way to explain the behavior of tiny particles," said Scully, who is also affiliated with Princeton University. "Now it's driving innovations that were unimaginable just a generation ago."

Scully has played a major role in advancing the field. He co-authored the influential textbook Quantum Optics, a resource that has educated generations of physicists. His research in coherent nanoscale laser spectroscopy helped make it possible to study molecules with atomic-scale precision. He has also developed groundbreaking concepts involving quantum heat engines, which challenge traditional assumptions about thermodynamic efficiency and may one day lead to new energy technologies.

From Schrödinger's Cat to Quantum Technology

One of the most famous illustrations of quantum mechanics came in 1935, when Erwin Schrödinger proposed his cat paradox. The thought experiment suggested that a cat could exist in a state that is both alive and dead until it is observed. Schrödinger introduced the idea to highlight how strange quantum theory appeared.

Today, those once-bizarre concepts have moved far beyond philosophical debates.

"That 'quantum weirdness' is no longer just a philosophical puzzle," said Scully. "It's the foundation of quantum computing, quantum cryptography and even gravitational wave detection."

The foundations of quantum mechanics were built by pioneers including Schrödinger and Werner Heisenberg. They developed two different mathematical approaches, wave mechanics and matrix mechanics, to describe quantum systems. Over time, these approaches were unified and contributed to the development of quantum field theory, which explains how particles interact through electromagnetic and nuclear forces.

Their work expanded on Niels Bohr's early atomic model, which pictured electrons orbiting the nucleus much like planets orbit the sun. While later discoveries refined that picture, Bohr's model helped pave the way for modern quantum theory.

Among the most important concepts in quantum mechanics is quantum coherence. This phenomenon allows particles such as atoms and photons to remain linked in a coordinated state, even across significant distances.

Quantum coherence led directly to the development of the laser, a technology that many once believed could never work. Today, lasers are used throughout modern society, from supermarket barcode scanners to vision correction procedures and advanced scientific instruments.

Coherence is also closely tied to quantum entanglement, the phenomenon that prompted Albert Einstein to describe it as "spooky action at a distance."

Entanglement enables particles to share information through uniquely quantum properties. These effects form the basis of quantum encryption systems and improve the sensitivity of sophisticated instruments such as the Laser Interferometer Gravitational-Wave Observatory (LIGO), which detects tiny ripples in spacetime.

Quantum Heat Engines Challenge Classical Limits

One of the more surprising applications of quantum physics involves quantum heat engines.

Traditional engines are constrained by the Carnot Limit, which defines the maximum efficiency allowed by classical thermodynamics. Researchers have found that by exploiting quantum coherence, it may be possible to create engines that exceed those classical limits.

"It's a striking example of how quantum principles can rewrite the rules of classical physics," Scully said.

The influence of quantum mechanics now extends far beyond physics.

In biology, techniques such as coherent Raman spectroscopy allow researchers to examine viruses and other structures at the nanoscale, providing valuable insights into the microscopic world.

Quantum ideas are also shaping efforts to understand the universe itself. Scientists working on concepts such as string theory and quantum gravity are attempting to reconcile quantum mechanics with Einstein's theory of relativity, one of the biggest unsolved problems in modern physics.

Researchers are even applying quantum concepts to the long-standing challenge of understanding turbulence. The chaotic motion of air and fluids affects weather patterns, climate systems, and aircraft performance. By studying superfluid helium, a substance that displays unusual quantum behavior, scientists are uncovering patterns that could improve climate modeling, storm prediction, and aviation safety.

Despite a century of success, quantum mechanics continues to raise profound questions.

Can gravity be quantized (i.e., does gravity behave like other forces at the quantum level)? Could quantum computers transform medicine and materials science? What new insights about the universe might emerge from future quantum technologies?

Scully believes the search for answers is only beginning.

"At the start of the 20th century, many thought physics was complete," he said. "Now, in the 21st century, we know the adventure is just beginning."

Five Ways Quantum Mechanics Affects Everyday Life

Materials provided by Texas A&M University. Original written by Lesley Henton. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10687. Build a Reliable Analytics Pipeline Before Moving to a Data Warehouse

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Category: Databases / Back-End Development

Meta description: Build a reliable analytics pipeline with SQL. Learn to handle duplicate records, late updates, data quality tests, and reporting before warehouse migration.

A dashboard can load quickly and still report the wrong number. A retried import might count an order twice. An update might arrive after the daily report has run. A join might multiply rows without producing a database error.

For developers moving application data into analytics, these problems deserve attention before platform selection. A larger warehouse can process an incorrect query faster, but it cannot decide what your metrics should mean.

This tutorial walks through a small order-reporting pipeline. You will define the grain of a dataset, handle repeated deliveries, select the latest record version, and test the output before exposing it to a dashboard.

“Show daily sales” sounds specific until someone asks which date, currency, or order status counts.

What is the current total value of paid orders, grouped by order date?

Orders whose latest version has a paid status

Integer minor units; the sample uses USD cents

Later order versions can revise earlier dates

Current-state reporting, not an as-of ledger

That final distinction matters. If an order changes from paid to refunded, this report removes it from the paid-order total on its original order date. It does not record a refund event on the refund date.

An accounting-style report would need a different model, usually based on transactions or adjustments. A table of current order states cannot answer every historical question.

A useful pipeline has three responsibilities:

●     Raw storage preserves incoming records and ingestion metadata.

●     Staging models normalize types, identify duplicates, and apply source ordering rules.

●     Reporting models express business definitions at a documented grain.

Keep these responsibilities distinct even if they initially live in one database.

For example, raw data might contain five deliveries of an order update. Staging resolves those deliveries to one current order. Reporting then groups qualifying orders into daily totals.

This separation gives failures a location. If the raw layer is missing an update, changing the dashboard query will not fix the extraction process.

For a refresher on the statements used to create, populate, and query these layers, see SitePoint’s guide to SQL commands.

The following SQL uses PostgreSQL-compatible syntax and fictional records. Run it in a disposable development database.

The dataset contains three deliberate challenges:

●     Order 101 moves from pending to paid.

●     Its paid version is delivered twice.

●     Order 102 changes from paid to refunded.

A naive sum of every row marked paid returns 27,500 cents. Under our metric contract, the correct result is 12,500 cents: the latest paid versions of orders 101 and 103.

The difference comes from record semantics, not query performance.

Use a window function to select the newest version of each order:

The order of operations is essential. If you filter for paid records before ranking versions, order 102’s refunded version disappears before the query can consider it. The earlier paid version then looks current.

This example assumes the source supplies a monotonically increasing version per order. The ingestion identifier breaks ties between repeated deliveries; it does not establish which business update is newest.

If two deliveries share an order ID and source version but contain different values, treat that as a source-contract violation. A deterministic tie-breaker makes the result repeatable, but it cannot determine which conflicting payload is correct.

A successful SQL execution only proves that the database accepted the query. Add checks for the assumptions the report depends on.

For example, this query identifies conflicting payloads for the same source version:

When the project grows, dbt data tests provide a way to organize assertions about uniqueness, nulls, relationships, accepted values, and custom business rules. Custom tests can return the records that violate an assumption.

Tests should also cover behavior, not only table shape. Extend the fixture with a late-arriving older version and confirm that it does not replace the latest state. Add an exact repeated delivery and confirm that the totals remain unchanged.

The views above recompute their results from all available raw records. That makes the demonstration easy to understand, but repeatedly scanning the full history may become expensive.

An incremental pipeline needs additional rules:

●     Track which order IDs changed in the incoming batch.

●     Recompute current state for those IDs using their available history.

●     Replace or merge their modeled rows instead of blindly appending.

●     Rebuild the affected reporting groups.

If an update changes an order’s date or currency, both the old and new reporting groups need attention. Refreshing only the new group leaves a stale amount behind.

Do not assume that “yesterday’s partition” contains every record that changed yesterday. An update received today can affect an order created months ago.

Apache Airflow’s best-practice guidance discusses designing tasks for retries and repeatable inputs. Regardless of scheduler, make the batch boundary explicit and advance checkpoints only after the required output has been committed successfully.

Hard deletes need their own policy too. If a source silently removes a record, a pipeline that only imports changed rows will not discover the deletion. Use deletion events, tombstones, or periodic reconciliation according to the source’s capabilities.

You do not need an orchestration platform, transformation framework, and new warehouse to validate one metric.

Prototype schemas, transformations, and reporting queries

When a separate development or analytics database is sufficient

Organize SQL models, dependencies, documentation, and tests

When transformations become a maintained project

Schedule dependent jobs and manage retries

When several pipeline steps require coordinated execution

Lint SQL using the project’s configured dialect

When shared SQL needs consistent review standards

Choose storage separately from orchestration. A scheduler does not decide how facts should be modeled, and a transformation framework does not guarantee source completeness.

Before adopting a warehouse, measure query duration, concurrency, freshness requirements, and interference with application traffic. For some workloads, precomputed results are enough to improve reporting responsiveness; SitePoint’s materialized-view tutorial explains that approach in PostgreSQL. Refresh behavior and acceptable staleness still need an explicit policy.

A migration becomes easier to evaluate when the team can show a small working pipeline, documented metrics, representative queries, and known failure cases.

For teams evaluating data warehouse consulting services, those artifacts make a useful review brief. Ask for concrete decisions about source ordering, historical changes, reconciliation, access controls, and recovery procedures, together with the assumptions behind any cost estimate.

Keep the review focused on observable outcomes. For example:

●     Can a failed batch be replayed without changing correct totals?

●     How are conflicting source versions detected?

●     Which reports change when an old order is updated?

●     Can a dashboard total be traced back to source records?

●     Who owns freshness alerts and recovery?

These questions also help an internal team review its own design. The important deliverable is a system whose behavior can be explained and tested.

A dashboard total needs context about when its underlying data became usable.

Track the latest successful ingestion, transformation, and validation separately. A recent extraction timestamp does not prove that downstream models finished building.

Likewise, a quiet source can make an event timestamp look old even when ingestion is healthy. Combine source activity with pipeline execution metadata before deciding that the data is stale.

Define the serving behavior when checks fail. You might keep the last validated result available with a visible freshness warning rather than replace it with an unverified total. Store the successful version if that is the policy: a live view over changing raw data does not preserve a previous validated snapshot.

A trustworthy analytics pipeline begins with a precise question and a testable data model.

Define the reporting grain, preserve incoming records, resolve source versions before filtering, and verify that retries do not alter correct results. Then measure whether the workload needs a different storage platform.

Those foundations make a future warehouse migration easier to reason about and make the dashboard useful before that migration begins.

#10686. Apple Watch now listens to in-person conversations & lets you rewind them

Apple is introducing a new AI feature that allows Apple Watch users to rewind in-person conversations and see what someone just said.

The feature, called Live Rewind, is part of Apple’s new Audio Intelligence technology, which began rolling out to developers with the iOS 27.3 beta on November 3.

First announced in September, the technology also lets users generate AI summaries of conversations, helping them remember key details without taking notes.

Live Rewind allows users to double-press their Apple Watch’s digital crown to see a text version of what was said during the previous 15 seconds.

For example, someone could use the feature to remember directions, check what a waiter said, or recall a name they missed during a conversation.

Users can also ask Siri questions about what was said or save the text for later. Otherwise, it automatically disappears after 30 seconds.

Apple is also introducing Siri Recaps, which uses AI to summarize conversations and highlight important details such as dates, deadlines, and other information.

Unlike Live Rewind, Siri Recaps can run automatically throughout the day. Users can choose when it operates, such as during work hours, and turn it off whenever they want.

The summaries can be saved to Apple’s Notes or Journal apps. Any that aren’t saved will automatically disappear within seven days.

The company says Siri Recaps does not save raw audio, identify individual speakers, or create complete transcripts of conversations.

Instead, audio is securely processed using the Apple Watch, a paired iPhone, and Apple’s Private Cloud Compute technology to generate summaries.

New AirPods leak reveals built-in cameras and Visual Intelligence

Live Rewind also plays a sound and displays an animation when activated to alert nearby people that the feature is being used.

Audio Intelligence includes two other features that can identify songs playing nearby and recognize important sounds such as alarms, sirens, and doorbells.

The technology is limited to the Apple Watch Series 12 and Apple Watch Ultra 4, paired with a compatible iPhone.

It’s currently available through the iOS 27.3 developer beta, with a public beta planned before the features become widely available.

#10682. Scientists discovered the brain doesn't make decisions the way we thought

Scientists at the University of Illinois Urbana Champaign have uncovered evidence that could reshape how researchers think about both the brain and artificial intelligence. Their findings suggest that decision making begins much earlier in the brain than traditional theories propose, offering fresh ideas for designing future AI systems that are more capable and far more energy efficient.

Led by electrical and computer engineering professor Yurii Vlasov at The Grainger College of Engineering, the research was published in Proceedings of the National Academy of Science (PNAS). The study points to an unexpected role for early sensory brain regions in decision making, challenging the long accepted view that decisions emerge only after information moves through a strict hierarchy of brain regions.

The human brain is widely regarded as the most complex structure in the known universe. Scientists still do not fully understand how it works, which is why reverse engineering the brain was identified by the National Academy of Engineering in 2008 as one of the 14 grand challenges for engineering in the 21st century.

For decades, many artificial intelligence systems, including convolutional neural networks, have been inspired by the idea that the brain processes information in a one way sequence. According to this traditional model, sensory information travels upward through increasingly complex brain regions until it reaches the frontal cortex, where decisions are made.

Vlasov and other researchers have increasingly questioned whether that picture is complete.

Instead, they are exploring a model based on natural intelligence, which has been refined through evolution over hundreds of millions of years. In this framework, the brain does not rely only on a step by step flow of information. Decision making also depends on interconnected feedback loops that allow information to move in both directions between brain regions.

Because biological intelligence performs remarkably complex tasks while using far less energy than today's AI systems, understanding this architecture could help guide the development of future artificial intelligence.

"We want to learn from a billion years of evolution," Vlasov said. "How is that biological intelligence organized architecturally? Can we learn from the architectural side of the brain and emulate that to make AI more effective, less power hungry, and more intelligent than it currently is? In the level of decision-making, that's where current AI is lacking."

Early Brain Regions Show Decision Making Activity

To investigate how these processes work, the research team focused on the brain's earliest stages of sensing and perception.

The scientists recorded neural activity in mice as they navigated a virtual reality corridor and made perceptual decisions. They found evidence of decision related activity in the primary somatosensory cortex (S1), one of the brain's earliest sensory processing areas.

Rather than simply passing information forward, S1 appeared to be influenced by higher brain regions through feedback loops. This top down regulation suggests that decision making involves continuous communication across multiple brain areas instead of a simple one direction flow of information.

"The neural code of the brain is still mostly an unknown language," Vlasov said. "But this systems-level understanding can be viewed as a potential impact on how more efficient artificial neural networks can be built -- how the next generation of AI can be thought through. Maybe with these analogies that we learn from real brains, we can improve AI further."

What the Findings Could Mean for Future AI

The researchers emphasize that the study does not provide a blueprint for building better artificial intelligence. Instead, it offers new insights into how the brain organizes decision making that could eventually inspire future AI architectures.

Next, Vlasov and his team plan to investigate the timing of these brain signals in greater detail. They also intend to develop new technologies for measuring neural activity to better understand how feedback loops emerge and coordinate different levels of brain processing.

"By looking at the fast temporal dynamics of neural activity, maybe we can understand better how these feedback loops are engaged in making decisions," Vlasov said. "Maybe that's the approach that potentially uncovers these currently unknown mechanisms -- how these feedback loops are organized dynamically and how they form and shape different levels of processing. Maybe that can be implemented in new architectures for AI."
